A p p -adic adjoint L L -function and the ramification locus of the Hilbert modular eigenvariety
Baskar Balasubramanyam, John Bergdall, Matteo Longo

Abstract
. Let F F be a totally real field and ℰ \mathscr{E} the middle-degree eigenvariety for Hilbert modular forms over F F , constructed by Bergdall–Hansen. We study the ramification locus of ℰ \mathscr{E} in relation to the p p -adic properties of adjoint L L -values. The connection between the two is made via an analytic twisted Poincaré pairing over affinoid weights, which interpolates the classical twisted Poincaré pairing for Hilbert modular forms, itself known to be related to adjoint L L -values by works of Ghate and Dimitrov. The overall strategy connecting the pairings to ramification is based on the theory of L L -ideals, which was used by Bellaïche and Kim in the case where F = ℚ F=\mathbb{Q} .



2000 Mathematics Subject Classification
11F85, 11F41 (11F67, 11F33, 11G18)

1.
Introduction

Let
p
be a prime number. This article focuses on the arithmetic of automorphic forms and
L
-functions in
p
-adic families of Hilbert modular forms. These families arrange themselves into a
p
-adic analytic space called an eigenvariety. The eigenvariety lies over its weight space, which interpolates the weights of classical Hilbert modular forms into
p
-adic ones. We establish a clear link between

(i)

the geometry of the eigenvariety relative to its weight space, and

(ii)

the variation of a
p
-adic adjoint
L
-function over the eigenvariety.

This link generalizes results previously obtained by Kim and Bellaïche for elliptic modular forms
[
Kim06
,
Bel21
]
. Related investigations have been carried out for automorphic forms on symplectic groups by Wu and for
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
over an imaginary quadratic field by Lee and Wu
[
Wu21
,
LW23
]
. Before describing our results, we expand on the history of ideas.

1.1.
Context

A half-century ago Hida and Doi observed that congruences of elliptic cuspidal eigenforms of
equal
weight are connected to the
p
-adic properties of certain Dirichlet series. Their unpublished work (see
[
Hid16
]
for a chronology) was soon reformulated by Hida in terms of adjoint
L
-values.

For a cuspform
f
, let
L(s,f,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
be its adjoint
L
-function. After normalizing by a transcendental constant, which includes a period factor, the adjoint
L
-value at
s=1
is an algebraic number
L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,f,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
. Hida proves, in two works, that the prime numbers dividing
L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,f,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
are the primes modulo which there exists a congruence between
f
and a second cuspform
g
of the same weight
[
Hid81a
,
Hid81b
]
. (Some of Hida’s work required that
f
be ordinary at
p
, an assumption later removed by Ribet
[
Rib83
]
. Also, the cited papers technically study symmetric square
L
-functions. These are the same as the adjoint
L
-functions, up to a shift in the variable
s
.) Analogous theorems for Hilbert modular forms are proven in much later works by Ghate and Dimitrov. See, for instance,
[
Gha02
, Theorem 5]
(and its corollaries in
op. cit.
) and
[
Dim05
, Theorem A]
. These works, and Hida’s, additionally quantify depths of congruences between eigenforms, by linking congruence modules to adjoint
L
-values.

The phenomenon of congruences between eigenforms of equal weight can be recast in terms of
p
-adic families of eigenforms. In Hida’s work, consider two
p
-ordinary cuspforms
f
and
g
of the same weight
k
. A congruence between them means they lie on a common component of a Hida family, in the same weight fiber. The total family is finite flat over weight space, and locally at
f
or
g
it is isomorphic to its weight space. The total multiplicity of the weight fiber above
k
is greater than one, so there is a weight near to
k
at which the weight parameter ramifies. On the one hand, the depth of the congruence between
f
and
g
roughly corresponds to the proximity to this ramification point. On the other, the theory of congruence modules alluded to above shows the depth of the congruence is also related to
p
-adic properties of
L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,f,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
. Therefore, as the eigenform
f
is taken closer and closer to a ramification point in the Hida family, the “function”
f\mapsto L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,f,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
becomes more and more divisible by
p
.

Kim’s Ph.D. thesis expands the meaning of the function
f\mapsto L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,f,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
, from the context of Hida families to more general families of eigenforms
[
Kim06
]
. His work was re-exposed and strengthened in a text of Bellaïche
[
Bel21
]
. Let us explain what those works achieve. The geometric space we consider is the cuspidal part of the Buzzard–Coleman–Mazur eigencurve, which is glued together by families of finite slope overconvergent
p
-adic eigenforms
[
CM98
,
Buz07
]
.

Over the eigencurve, Bellaïche and Kim construct an ideal sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
that has two important, independent, features. First,
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
is related to adjoint
L
-values in the following sense. Consider a point
x
on the eigencurve, associated with a classical
p
-refined eigenform
f_{x}
, which we assume is
p
-distinguished (the notions of
p
-refined eigenform and the property of being
p
-distinguished are shortly discussed at the beginning of §
1.2
and fully reviewed in §
3.4
and §
6.4
).
The sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
is shown to be principally generated in a neighborhood of
x
, say by a germ
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}
. Assume, further, that
f_{x}
is of non-critical slope: its
p
-th Hecke eigenvalue has
p
-adic valuation below
k-1
. Then,
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}(x)=L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,f_{x},\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
, up to a non-zero scalar. Since classical points of non-critical slope are dense in neighborhoods of any eigencurve point of integer weight, the sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
interpolates the adjoint
L
-value at
s=1
.

Second,
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
is related to the relative geometry of the eigencurve over its weight space. Indeed, Bellaïche uses the theory of
L
-ideals in
[
Bel21
, Chapter 9]
to show that the ramification locus of the weight parameter is always contained in the locus cut out by the ideal sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
. (Warning: the writing in Theorem 9.4.2 of
loc. cit.
reverses this containment on accident!) Even more strongly, locally at classical points on the eigencurve, the underlying reduced subspace of the ramification locus and the underlying reduced locus cut out by the ideal sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
are equal. Putting this feature together with the first, we find the classical points on the eigencurve at which the weight map ramifies are precisely the classical points on the eigencurve where the germ
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}
vanishes.

The classical ramification points are the so-called
\theta
-critical points, which are a special class of points of critical slope
[
Ber14
, Theorem 1.1]
. We stress, to end this discussion, that
L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,f_{x},\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
is always non-vanishing (see Proposition
3.4.2
). Therefore, the theorem and construction of Bellaïche and Kim does
not
say that
L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,f_{x},\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
vanishes at
\theta
-critical points, but rather that in order to interpolate
f\mapsto L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,f,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
from the dense set of classical non-critical slope points into the local germ
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}
, vanishing of
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}
is
forced
at
\theta
-critical points.

1.2.
Main results

In this article, we generalize the apparatus just described to the setting of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
over a totally real field. The primary result is Corollary
3
, which is a combination of results in the text. We will try to explain this quickly, leaving notations and concepts to be fully explained later.

Throughout the article, we fix a totally real number field
F
of degree
d
, and we consider cuspidal, cohomological, automorphic representations
\pi
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
over
F
. We write
\omega_{\pi}
for the central character of
\pi
. We focus in the introduction on
\pi
such that
\pi_{v}
is an unramified principal series at
p
-adic places
v
. (This simplifies the discussion. The text also allows
\pi
to have
p
-adic Steinberg components.) For such
v
, we write
a_{\pi}(v)
for the
v
-th Hecke eigenvalue,
q_{v}
for the residue field degree of
F
at
v
and
\varpi_{v}
for a uniformizer at
v
. A
p
-refinement
of
\pi
is a choice of roots
\alpha=(\alpha_{v})_{v\mid p}
for the Hecke polynomials
X^{2}-a_{\pi}(v)X+\omega_{\pi}(\varpi_{v})q_{v}
. The pair
(\pi,\alpha)
is called a
p
-refined
automorphic representation. We say
\pi
is
p
-distinguished
if its
p
-adic Hecke polynomials have simple roots for each
v
. This is conjectured to be automatic if
p
is totally split in
F
, but otherwise it is truly a condition.

The analogue of the eigencurve, in this work, is the middle-degree eigenvariety
\mathscr{E}
studied by the second author and Hansen
[
BH24
]
. It is a rigid analytic variety that lies over the
p
-adic weight space
\mathscr{W}
. The weight space is a finite union of polydiscs of dimension
1+d+\delta_{F,p}
, where
\delta_{F,p}
is the defect in Leopoldt’s conjecture for
F
at
p
. The rigid space
\mathscr{E}
is reduced and equidimensional of dimension
\dim(\mathscr{E})=\dim(\mathscr{W})
. In constructing
\mathscr{E}
, one must fix an integral ideal
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
that is co-prime to
p
. Let
\mathbf{p}
be the product of primes lying over
p
in
F
, and let
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0}\mathbf{p}
. The eigenvariety itself is defined using Hecke eigensystems of level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
, appearing in various cohomology spaces. (See §
1.3.2
or §
6
.)

If
\pi
has level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0})
then each
p
-refined pair
(\pi,\alpha)
defines a point
x_{\pi,\alpha}\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. The properties of the eigenvariety at such points is the subject of §
6
. For instance, we recall what it means for such points
x_{\pi,\alpha}
to be
non-critical
. Each
\alpha
that satisfies a numerical criterion analogous to the non-critical slope condition will generate a non-critical point
x_{\pi,\alpha}
. In §
6.4
, we also introduce a more general notion of
x_{\pi,\alpha}
being
very decent
. For non-critical
x_{\pi,\alpha}
, it is equivalent to
\pi
being
p
-distinguished. For critical
x_{\pi,\alpha}
, it additionally involves assumptions on Selmer groups of adjoint Galois representations and on companion point phenomena. Those added assumptions are technical, for one conjectures that
x_{\pi,\alpha}
is very decent if and only if
\pi
is
p
-distinguished. As evidence, as long as
p>2
,
\pi
is
p
-distinguished and its Galois representation modulo
p
has image containing
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
, then the points
x_{\pi,\alpha}
are all very decent; so if
\pi
is non-CM and
p
-distinguished and
p
is sufficiently large, then each
x_{\pi,\alpha}
is very decent. (See the discussion in §
6.4
.) To summarize, the fundamental hypothesis in the next theorem is just that
\pi
is
p
-distinguished.

Theorem 1

(Theorem
6.4.7
(vi)
)
.

If
x_{\pi,\alpha}
is a very decent classical point on
\mathscr{E}
, then
(\pi,\alpha)
is critical if and only if the weight map
\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}
ramifies at
x_{\pi,\alpha}
.

As indicated, this is the sixth part of Theorem
6.4.7
in the text. The remaining portions (for instance, the smoothness of
\mathscr{E}
at very decent points) were already proven in joint work of the second author and Hansen
[
BH24
]
. The second author recalls discussing Theorem
1
with Hansen while writing
op. cit.
, but the result was not included there because it did not have direct bearing on the intended applications.

The construction of an ideal sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
over
\mathscr{E}
, analogous to the one constructed by Bellaïche and Kim over the eigencurve, occurs in two stages, in §
5
and
7
. We describe the construction in §
1.3
below. Before that description, the primary goal is the following theorem that shows how
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
is closely related to adjoint
L
-values at
s=1
. In part
(i)
, the notation “
\overset{\cdot}{=}
” means “up to a non-zero constant” and
L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
denotes the adjoint
L
-value at
s=1
for
\pi
, normalized by periods in order to make it algebraic.

Theorem 2

(Theorem
7.4.3
and Remark
7.5.1
)
.

If
x_{\pi,\alpha}
is a very decent classical point on
\mathscr{E}
, then
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
is locally principal at
x_{\pi,\alpha}
. Assuming
x_{\pi,\alpha}
is very decent, choose a generator
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}
for
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
over a sufficiently small neighborhood
x_{\pi,\alpha}
. Then, the following hold.

(i)

If
(\pi,\alpha)
is non-critical, then

L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}(x_{\pi,\alpha})\overset{\cdot}{=}e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0}),

where
e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)
is a non-vanishing
p
-adic interpolation factor.

(ii)

If
(\pi,\alpha)
is critical, then
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}(x_{\pi,\alpha})=0
.

The interpolation factor
e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)
in Theorem
2
(i)
is familiar in studies of
p
-adic interpolation for
L
-series. The normalization of
L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
is only defined up to some non-zero constants. By additionally writing “
\overset{\cdot}{=}
”, we emphasize that the meaning of
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}(x_{\pi,\alpha})
itself is only defined up to a non-vanishing function on a neighborhood of
x_{\pi,\alpha}
on
\mathscr{E}
.

These two theorems combine to give an equivalence between weight ramification at very decent points and the vanishing of the germ
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}
in Theorem
2
. To state it without reference to germs, write
\mathscr{Z}^{\operatorname{ad}}
for the reduced locus in
\mathscr{E}
cut out by the ideal sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
and
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{ram}}
for the reduced ramification locus of the weight map.

Corollary 3

(Theorem
7.4.3
)
.

If
x=x_{\pi,\alpha}
is a very decent classical point on
\mathscr{E}
, then the following are equivalent.

(i)

The point
x
belongs to
\mathscr{Z}^{\operatorname{ad}}
.

(ii)

The point
x
is a critical point.

(iii)

The point
x
belongs to
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{ram}}
.

Note that Theorem
2
and Corollary
3
refer to the same result in the text (Theorem
7.4.3
). We wrote them separately here to help communicate the proof of Corollary
3
as two separate but equivalent conditions for critical points
x
. Namely, critical points are precisely those classical points where the weight map ramifies and critical points are also precisely those belonging to
\mathscr{Z}^{\operatorname{ad}}
. The same logical connection with critical points is used in
[
Bel21
, Chapter 9]
, though perhaps not as emphasized.

We indicated before that, once
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
is constructed, one has
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{ram}}\subseteq\mathscr{Z}^{\operatorname{ad}}
. It would be interesting to better understand the difference between these two subspaces. In the setting of the eigencurve, Bellaïche shows the containment
\mathscr{Z}^{\mathrm{ram}}\subseteq\mathscr{Z}^{\operatorname{ad}}
is an equality in neighborhoods of points with non-algebraic weight. See
[
Bel21
, Theorem 9.4.7]
. Such a theorem should be true in the Hilbert modular setting, but generalizing the proof requires overcoming a series of technical challenges, and we did not extensively pursue it. Likewise, it would be interesting to study
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
versus weight fibers at the level of non-reduced structures. Again, Bellaïche provides some analysis
[
Bel21
, Theorem 9.4.8]
, but we did not investigate generalizations in that direction either.

1.3.
Adjoint
L
-ideals and cohomological pairings

To end this introduction, we sketch the construction of the ideal sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
and the proof of Theorem
2
. To do that, we describe the cohomological pairings from which the ideal sheaf arises.

1.3.1.
Adjoint
L
-values and the twisted Poincaré pairing

The first step we take is examining a classical pairing, called the twisted Poincaré pairing. In §
2
-
3
we define this pairing rigorously and relate it to adjoint
L
-values. For the purpose of the introduction, we skip straight to the
p
-adic setting of §
4
.

Let
L
be a sufficiently large
p
-adic field. An
L
-valued classical algebraic weight for Hilbert modular forms is a pair
\lambda=(n,w)
where
w\in\mathbb{Z}
,
n=(n_{\tau})_{\tau}
is a tuple of non-negative integers indexed by embeddings
\tau:F\rightarrow L
, and
n_{\tau}\equiv w\bmod 2
for all
\tau
. For such
\lambda
, and an integral ideal
\mathfrak{n}
for
F
, there is a natural
L
-linear algebraic local system
\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}
on
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
. The
p
-adic twisted Poincaré pairing is a pairing

[-,-]_{\lambda}:H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{L}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\longrightarrow L

that ultimately arises from duality considerations, hence the reference to Poincaré. The “twisted” part is that the definition also involves a twisting by an Atkin–Lehner operator. This has the significant impact that the standard Hecke operators for the congruence subgroup
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
act on
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})
via operators that are self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{\lambda}
.

Let
\pi
be a cuspidal, cohomological, automorphic representation of level
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
, where
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
is co-prime to
p
. Write
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0}\mathbf{p}
and let
\alpha
be a refinement for
\pi
. Using
\mathbb{C}
-linear coefficients for
\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}
, a variant of constructions going back to Eichler and Shimura gives rise to a natural class
\phi_{\pi,\alpha}
in
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})
defined over some number field, after a period normalization. (The cuspidality is crucial to interpret these classes as lying in cohomology with compact supports.) More precisely, each sign
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{d}
defines a Hecke-stable sign component

H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}\subseteq H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})

and for each such
\varepsilon
, there is a class
\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon}
in
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}
defined up to a period scaling depending on
\varepsilon
. Each
\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon}
is a Hecke eigenclass and, in particular, for
v\mid p
we have
U_{v}\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon}=\alpha_{v}\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon}
.

In Theorem
3.8.2
and Corollary
3.8.4
of §
3
, we prove that

(1.3.1)

[\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon},\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{-\varepsilon}]_{\lambda}=e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0}),

where
e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)
is a simple interpolation factor and
L^{\mathrm{alg}}(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
is the
L
-value
L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
normalized by a collection of periods, including some that depend on the choice of signs
\pm\varepsilon
. There is a prior history of calculations such as (
1.3.1
). A similar formula at level
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
is implicit in the methods of Ghate and Dimitrov in studying congruence modules for Hilbert modular forms
[
Gha02
,
Dim05
]
. In our work, we follow the perspective taken in work of the first author with Raghuram
[
BR17
]
. The main task in proving (
1.3.1
) is making specific local (adelic) calculations to nail down the precise interpolation factor and the influence of the periods.

1.3.2.
An analytic Poincaré pairing

Kim’s thesis aims to interpolate adjoint
L
-values by interpolating the pairing values on the left-hand side of (
1.3.1
). We adopt a similar strategy, with three stages of interpolation. First, one interpolates weights from classical algebraic ones to
p
-adic ones. Second, one interpolates the
\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}
into families of local systems
\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda}
defined using locally analytic distributions. Third, one interpolates the pairings from self-pairings on
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})
to self-pairings on
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})
.

The construction of
p
-adic weight space is well-studied. Let
F_{p}=F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Likewise, let
\mathscr{O}_{F}
be the ring of integers of
F
and
\mathscr{O}_{p}=\mathscr{O}_{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Set
\Sigma_{p}=\mathrm{Hom}(F,L)
. We have
\mathscr{O}_{p}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\Sigma_{p}}
and for
x\in\mathscr{O}_{p}
we write
x_{\tau}
for its
\tau
-coordinate. A
p
-adic weight
valued in an
L
-affinoid algebra
A
is a continuous character
\lambda:(\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times})^{2}\rightarrow A^{\times}
that is trivial on the diagonal image of all totally positive units. The space of
p
-adic weights is naturally a rigid analytic variety over
L
, with dimension
1+d+\delta_{F,p}
. The algebraic weight
\lambda=(n,w)
corresponds to the algebraic character
(x,y)\mapsto\prod_{\tau}x_{\tau}^{\frac{n_{\tau}+w}{2}}y_{\tau}^{\frac{w-n_{\tau}}{2}}
on
(\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times})^{2}
.

For each
p
-adic weight
\lambda
, valued in some affinoid
L
-algebra
A
, we then consider
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
. This is the
A
-module of locally
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-analytic distributions on
\mathscr{O}_{p}
, equipped with the weight-
\lambda
action of the monoid

\Delta^{+}=\left\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{M}_{2}(\mathscr{O}_{p})\cap\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F_{p})\mid c\in\varpi_{p}\mathscr{O}_{p}\text{ and }d\in\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}\right\}

where if we write
\mathscr{O}_{p}\simeq\prod_{v\mid p}\mathscr{O}_{v}
for
v\mid p
, then
\varpi_{p}
is the element of
\mathscr{O}_{p}
defined by
\varpi_{p}=(\varpi_{v})_{v\mid p}
under this isomorphism; see §
5.3
for precise formulas and §
1.4
for a more details on the notation. One primary point is that
\Delta^{+}
contains the Iwahori subgroup, so
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
defines a local system
\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda}
on
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
. The cohomology
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})
is equipped with a natural Hecke action, including operators at
p
-adic places. The modules
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
interpolate the algebraic representations
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
in the following sense. If
\lambda
is a classical algebraic weight, there is a natural Iwahori-equivariant map
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}:\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
, that then induces a natural specialization map

\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}:H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\longrightarrow H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}).

The map
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}
is Hecke equivariant, up to a twist at
p
-adic places. This specialization map also gives meaning to a pair
(\pi,\alpha)
being
non-critical
. Namely, provided
\pi
is
p
-distinguished (and assuming
\pi
has level
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
), it has a one-dimensional eigenspace
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}[\pi,\alpha]
for the Hecke action in any sign component. The pair
(\pi,\alpha)
is non-critical if
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})[\pi,\alpha]\simeq H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})[\pi,\alpha]

via
the specialization map.

The third step is constructing, for any
A
-valued
p
-adic weight
\lambda
, a pairing

[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}:H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{A}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\longrightarrow A

meant to interpolate the classical self-pairings
[-,-]_{\lambda}
on
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})
at classical algebraic weights. We define
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
in §
5.4
, and in §
5.5
we show our candidate pairings satisfy the basic relationship

(1.3.2)

[\Phi,\Psi]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}=\varpi_{p}^{\frac{n-w}{2}}[\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\Phi),\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\Psi)]_{\lambda},

for all
\Phi,\Psi\in H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})
. Here,
\varpi_{p}^{\frac{n-w}{2}}
is an expression using multi-index notation. It is not analytic in
\lambda
. Its presence is one difference between
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
over
\mathbb{Q}
and the general
F
. What we find in general is that the pairings
[-,-]_{\lambda}
do not literally interpolate into
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
until they are properly scaled. This scaling is closely related to the fact that
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}
is not Hecke-equivariant on the nose, but only after a small twist. In the case
F=\mathbb{Q}
, and working explicitly with elliptic modular eigenforms, one always has
n=w
and such normalizing factors are hidden.

We do not describe the construction of
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
here. However, we do stress that the construction is direct, based on generalizing various ingredients that go into constructing
[-,-]_{\lambda}
in the first place. By contrast, the work of Bellaïche and Kim proceeds by building
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
through an interpolation process from the pairings
[-,-]_{\lambda}
. The presence of the scalar factor in (
1.3.2
), which does not interpolate, is one indication as to why a direct construction of

[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
is adopted.
A direct construction also makes sense when one examines proofs of natural properties enjoyed by the pairing
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
. For instance, in §
5.4
and Appendix
A
we prove that the Hecke operators act on
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})
through operators that are self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
. We also explain that for each pair of signs
\varepsilon,\eta\in\{\pm 1\}^{d}
, the subspace
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}
is orthogonal to
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})^{\eta}
under
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
except when
\eta=-\varepsilon
. In the case
F=\mathbb{Q}
, such properties are established through
p
-adic interpolation from the corresponding classical facts. However, those arguments often seem (to the authors) to rely on special features of working with modular symbols, rather than the cohomology of a higher-dimensional Hilbert modular variety. We note that the work of Wu and Lee–Wu (see
[
Wu21
,
LW23
]
) also proceeds in a direct fashion.

1.3.3.
The sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad}}
on
\mathscr{E}

We recall the construction of
\mathscr{E}
in §
6
. Here, we note that as a rigid space, it is admissibly covered by a basis of open sets
U
called the good neighborhoods. We recall a few relevant features. Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathscr{W}
be the image of such a
U
under the weight map. The eigenvariety is also equipped with a natural coherent sheaf
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}
. The sections
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)
over a good neighborhood are finite projective over
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
, arising as a submodule

\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)\subseteq H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})

that is a Hecke-stable direct summand. The ring of rigid analytic functions
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
is, by construction, the largest
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
-algebra containing the Hecke operators away from
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
and which acts faithfully on
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)
. Therefore, for good neighborhoods
U
the analytic Poincaré pairings above define natural pairings

[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}:\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)\longrightarrow\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)

and the functions in
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
act on
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)
via operators that are self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}
. The construction of
[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}
is natural in
U
, and everything preserves sign components.

We summarize the abstract situation as follows. Fix a sign
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{d}
. For each good neighborhood
U
we have
A=\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
, which is a noetherian ring. We also have a finite
A
-algebra
T=\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
. There are, additionally, a pair of
T
-modules
M=\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)^{\varepsilon}
and
N=\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)^{-\varepsilon}
that are finite projective as
A
-modules. And, finally, there is an
A
-bilinear pairing

[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}:M\otimes_{A}N\longrightarrow A

and
T
acts on
M
and
N
through operators that are self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}
. This is the precise data to which the theory of
L
-ideals explained in
[
Bel21
, §9.1.3]
applies. The output of this theory is a natural ideal
\mathfrak{L}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}_{U}\subseteq T=\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
. These ideals glue to form the ideal sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}
. (The sheaves depend theoretically on
\varepsilon
, but it was not until now that we felt it necessary to point that out. Theorem
2
and Corollary
3
work for any sign.)

The final step toward Theorem
2
is now based on relating the sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
to
bona fide
values of the pairings
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
as follows. Suppose
x_{\pi,\alpha}
is very decent. Then, the geometrical theorem Theorem
6.4.7
on the eigenvariety at very decent points implies that, for small enough
U
,

(i)

the ideal
\mathfrak{L}_{U}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
is principally generated by some element
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}\in T
, and

(ii)

the eigenspaces
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})^{\pm\varepsilon}[\pi,\alpha]
are one-dimensional.

The definition of
\mathfrak{L}_{U}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
then implies that for any non-zero vectors
\Phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\pm\varepsilon}\in H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})^{\pm\varepsilon}[\pi,\alpha]
, we have

(1.3.3)

L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}(x_{\pi,\alpha})\overset{\cdot}{=}[\Phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon},\Phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{-\varepsilon}]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}.

The proof of Theorem
2
is carried out by analyzing the right-hand side of (
1.3.3
), in light of (
1.3.2
).

1.4.
Notations

We summarize the notations introduced above and include some used in the text.

The letter
F
represents the fixed totally real field. Its degree is
d=[F:\mathbb{Q}]
. Its ring of integers is
\mathscr{O}_{F}
. The real embeddings
F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
are written
\Sigma_{\infty}
. We generically write
E
for a number field in
\mathbb{C}
such that
\tau(F)\subseteq E
for all
\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}
. The adeles of
\mathbb{Q}
are written
\mathbbm{A}
, with the finite adeles being
\mathbbm{A}_{f}
. Similarly, the adeles over
F
are indicated with a subscript
\mathbbm{A}_{F}
and their finite counterpart is
\mathbbm{A}_{F,f}
. We write
\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F}=\mathscr{O}_{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
. We write
F_{\infty}=F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
. Similarly, if
v
is a finite place of
F
, we write
F_{v}
for its completion. The ring of integers in
F_{v}
is
\mathscr{O}_{v}
. We often use
\varpi_{v}\in\mathscr{O}_{v}
for a uniformizer at
v
.

Starting in §
3
, we fix the prime number
p
. A bold roman
\mathbf{p}
means the product of primes above
p
in
F
. The letter
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
represents a fixed ideal in
\mathscr{O}_{F}
that is co-prime to
\mathbf{p}
, and
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0}\mathbf{p}
. We also write
\mathfrak{p}_{v}
for the prime ideal in
\mathscr{O}_{F}
corresponding to any finite place
v
. Its residue field has size
q_{v}=|\mathscr{O}_{F}/\mathfrak{p}_{v}|
and the ramification index is
e_{v}
.

Starting in §
4
we fix an algebraic closure
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
of the
p
-adic numbers. Let
\Sigma_{p}
denote the embeddings
F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. The letter
L
generically means a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
within
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
such that
\tau(F)\subseteq L
for all
\tau\in\Sigma_{p}
. We will sometimes write

F_{p}=F\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}\simeq\prod_{v\mid p}F_{v}.

The symbol
\mathscr{O}_{p}
refers to the product of the
\mathscr{O}_{v}
for
v\mid p
(under the previous isomorphism). The element
\varpi_{p}\in\mathscr{O}_{p}
refers to the tuple
(\varpi_{v})_{v\mid p}
. We also fix a field isomorphism
\iota:\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. For
v\mid p
, write
\Sigma_{v}
for the embeddings of
F_{v}
into
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. Thus we have a bijection and disjoint union decomposition

\Sigma_{\infty}\overset{\iota}{\longleftrightarrow}\Sigma_{p}=\bigsqcup_{v\mid p}\Sigma_{v}.

In §
5
-
6
, we consider a few compact and abelian
p
-adic Lie groups
\Gamma
. For each,
\mathscr{X}(\Gamma)
is its
p
-adic character variety, the rigid analytic space of continuous characters on
\Gamma
.

p
-adic
L
-functions and eigenvarieties. The second author was a student in his Fall 2010 course at Brandeis University, a course on the foundations of modular symbols, the eigencurve, and
p
-adic
L
-functions. The material from that course eventually formed his Eigenbook
[
Bel21
]
. As much as we could, we have prioritized the kind of clarity and generosity Bellaïche valued in his own writing. His energy and enthusiasm are sorely missed.

2.
The twisted Poincaré pairing for Hilbert modular varieties

In this section, we recall the twisted Poincaré product on the cohomology of Hilbert modular varieties. We will use the notations from the introduction and just above, in §
1.4
.

2.1.
Hilbert modular varieties

Let
G=\operatorname{Res}_{\mathscr{O}_{F}/\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{GL}_{2}
. The center of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
defines
Z\subseteq G
by restriction of scalars. Similarly, the diagonal matrices in
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
define a torus
T\subseteq G
, by restriction of scalars.
Let
H
be any of these algebraic groups and
g\in H(\mathbbm{A})
. When
\ell
is a prime number, we write
g_{\ell}\in H(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell})
for its
\ell
-adic component. We also write
g_{\infty}\in H(\mathbb{R})
for its Archimedean one. Note that
H(\mathbb{R})\subseteq G(\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
. Thus, we often write
g_{\infty}=(g_{\tau})_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}}
.

A level is an open compact subgroup
K\subseteq G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
. We fix
K_{\infty}\subseteq G(\mathbb{R})
to be the maximal subgroup that is compact modulo the center in
G(\mathbb{R})
. Write
K_{\infty}^{+}\subseteq G(\mathbb{R})^{+}
for its identity component. We also denote by
G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}\subseteq G(\mathbb{Q})
the subgroup of
\gamma\in G(\mathbb{Q})
such that
\gamma_{\infty}\in G(\mathbb{R})^{+}
. We describe the Hilbert modular variety of level
K
three ways. First, by definition, it is the double quotient

Y_{K}=G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A})/K_{\infty}^{+}K.

Second, let
D_{\infty}=G(\mathbb{R})^{+}/K_{\infty}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{h}
be the complex upper half plane. Then, there is an identification
D_{\infty}\simeq\mathfrak{h}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
via
g_{\infty}\mapsto(g_{\tau}(i))_{\tau}
. So, we have

(2.1.1)

Y_{K}\simeq G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}\backslash(D_{\infty}\times G(\mathbbm{A}_{f}))/K.

We denote by
(z,g_{f})\in Y_{K}
typical coordinates in (
2.1.1
). Third, by strong approximation we may choose a finite set of elements
t_{1},\dotsc,t_{h}\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
such that

(2.1.2)

G(\mathbbm{A})=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{h}G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}t_{i}KG(\mathbb{R}).

Set
\Gamma_{i}=t_{i}KG(\mathbb{R})t_{i}^{-1}\cap G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}\subseteq G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
. Then,

(2.1.3)

Y_{K}\simeq\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{h}\Gamma_{i}\backslash D_{\infty}.

2.2.
Cohomology

Let
H^{\ast}(Y_{K},-)
and
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{K},-)
be the cohomology and compactly supported cohomology, respectively, on
Y_{K}
. The parabolic cohomology
H^{\ast}_{!}(Y_{K},-)
is, by definition, the image of
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{K},-)
in
H^{\ast}(Y_{K},-)
. The coefficients may be either constant or non-trivial sheaves.

When
Y_{K}
is a manifold, it has a fundamental class
[Y_{K}]
in the Borel–Moore homology
H_{2d}^{\mathrm{BM}}(Y_{K},\mathbb{Z})
. This class depends on choices of orientation, which we make as follows. Fix an orientation on
D_{\infty}
. We then define an orientation on
Y_{K}
via pushforward onto each connected component
\Gamma_{i}\backslash D_{\infty}
in (
2.1.3
). For any ring
A
, the Poincaré duality map

(2.2.1)

H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{K},A)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{PD}}A

is
\operatorname{PD}(\Phi)=\operatorname{tr}(\Phi\cap[Y_{K}])
where
\operatorname{tr}:H_{0}(Y_{K},A)\rightarrow A
is the trace map. The level
K
is safely omitted from the notation because, if
K^{\prime}\subseteq K
is another level and
\pi:Y_{K^{\prime}}\rightarrow Y_{K}
is the projection map, then on
H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{K},A)
we have

(2.2.2)

\operatorname{PD}\pi^{\ast}=\operatorname{PD}.

It is convenient now (for use in Appendix
A
) to recall compatibility with respect to multiplication. Let
g\in G(\mathbbm{A})
such that
g_{\infty}
normalizes
K_{\infty}^{+}
. Let
r_{g}:Y_{gKg^{-1}}\rightarrow Y_{K}
be the map induced by
r_{g}(h)=hg
on
G(\mathbbm{A})
. For
g_{\infty}\in G(\mathbb{R})
write
\operatorname{sgn}(\det g_{\tau})=\pm 1
depending on the sign of
\det g_{\tau}
. Define
\operatorname{sgn}(\det g_{\infty})=\prod_{\tau}\operatorname{sgn}(\det g_{\tau})
. Then, on
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{K},A)
, we have

(2.2.3)

\operatorname{PD}r_{g}^{\ast}=\operatorname{sgn}(\det g_{\infty})\operatorname{PD}.

If
Y_{K}
is not a manifold but
A
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra, then
\operatorname{PD}
may be defined as follows. Choose a finite index normal subgroup
K^{\prime}\subseteq K
such that
Y_{K^{\prime}}
is a manifold (see
[
Hid88
, p. 340]
). Then, since
(K:K^{\prime})
acts invertibly on
A
, we have an isomorphism
\pi^{\ast}:H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{K},A)\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{K^{\prime}},A)^{K/K^{\prime}}
. We define
\operatorname{PD}
via this isomorphism. It is independent of the choice of
K^{\prime}
by (
2.2.2
) above.

2.3.
Bimodules and adelic cochains

We briefly recall adelic cochains, which provide a way to compute cohomology without focusing on decompositions such as (
2.1.3
). We will use this description for calculations, especially in Appendix
A
.

Set
D_{\mathbb{A}}=D_{\infty}\times G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
. This is a topological space, with the Archimedean topology on the first factor and the discrete topology on the second. The singular (
\mathbb{Z}
-)chains
C_{\bullet}(-)
are given by

(2.3.1)

C_{\bullet}(D_{\mathbb{A}})\simeq C_{\bullet}(D_{\infty})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[G(\mathbb{A}_{f})].

Fix a level
K
. A
(G(\mathbb{Q})^{+},K)
-bimodule is an abelian group
M
equipped with a left
\mathbb{Z}
-linear action
(\gamma,m)\mapsto\gamma m
of
G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
and a right
\mathbb{Z}
-linear action
(m,k)\mapsto m|_{k}
of
K
and such that
\gamma(m|_{k})=(\gamma m)|_{k}
. The complex
C_{\bullet}(D_{\mathbbm{A}})
is equipped with the structure of a
(G(\mathbb{Q})^{+},K)
-bimodules as follows. On simple tensors
\sigma=\sigma_{\infty}\otimes[g_{f}]\in C_{\bullet}(D_{\infty})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})]
, the action is

\gamma\sigma|_{k}=\gamma_{\infty}\sigma_{\infty}\otimes[\gamma_{f}g_{f}k].

Let
M
be a
(G(\mathbb{Q})^{+},K)
-bimodule. Its adelic cochain complex is

C^{\bullet}(K,M)=\mathrm{Hom}_{(G(\mathbb{Q})^{+},{K})}(C_{\bullet}(D_{\mathbb{A}}),M).

See
[
BH24
, §2.2]
or
[
Han17
, §2.1]
. So, an adelic cochain
\varphi
is a linear map
\varphi:C_{\bullet}(D_{\mathbbm{A}})\rightarrow M
such that

(2.3.2)

\varphi(\gamma\sigma k)=\gamma\varphi(\sigma)|_{k}

for all
\sigma\in C_{\bullet}(D_{\mathbbm{A}})
,
\gamma\in G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
, and
k\in K
. For an adelic cochain
\varphi
, and
g\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
, the linear map
{\varrho_{g}}(\varphi)(\sigma_{\infty})=\varphi(\sigma_{\infty}\otimes[g])
defines an
M
-valued cochain on
D_{\infty}
. The adelic cochains with compact support are defined as

C^{\bullet}_{c}(K,M)=\{\varphi\in C^{\bullet}(K,M):{\varrho_{g}}(\varphi)\text{ is a cochain with compact support for all $g\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})$}\}.

We finally recall that adelic cochains calculate cohomology. Namely, assume
M
is a
(G(\mathbb{Q})^{+},K)
-bimodule and
F^{\times}\cap KF_{\infty}^{\times}\subseteq G(\mathbb{Q})
acts trivially on
M
. (We implicitly assume this in all that follows.) Then, we may consider the associated local system
\underline{M}
on
Y_{K}
. By definition, it is the sheaf of locally constant sections of

G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}\backslash(D_{\infty}\times G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})\times M)/K\twoheadrightarrow Y_{K},

though we often abuse notation and simply write
\underline{M}
for the double quotient appearing here. By
[
Han17
, Proposition 2.1.1]
, or its proof in the case of compact supports, there is a natural isomorphism

H^{\ast}(C^{\bullet}_{?}(K,M))\simeq H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{K},\underline{M})

where
?=\emptyset
or
?=c
. (Warning: the adelic cochain complexes in
op. cit.
are written
C^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{ad}}(-,-)
. The notation
C^{\bullet}(-,-)
in
op. cit.
is different.)

2.4.
Two geometric pullbacks

Fix a level
K
and let
A
be a ring. We assume
M
is an
A
-linear
(G(\mathbb{Q})^{+},K)
-bimodule on which
F^{\times}\cap KF_{\infty}^{\times}
acts trivially. We recall two separate geometric pullback operations that we will repeatedly use.

The first pullback is via right multiplication. Let
{g}\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
. Then
r_{g}(h)=h{g}
, as a function on
G(\mathbbm{A})
, descends to a morphism

r_{g}:Y_{{g}K{g}^{-1}}\longrightarrow Y_{K}.

Define
{g}^{\ast}M=M
as an
A
-module with no change in the left
G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
-action. We make
{g}^{\ast}M
a right
{g}K{g}^{-1}
-module (with action written
\cdot|^{{g}^{\ast}}
for the moment) via

m|^{{g}^{\ast}}_{k^{\prime}}=m|_{{g}^{-1}k^{\prime}{g}}

for all
k^{\prime}\in{g}K{g}^{-1}
. It is straightforward to see that
{g}^{\ast}\underline{M}
is the pullback of
\underline{M}
along
r_{g}
. In fact, the identity map
{g}^{\ast}M\rightarrow M
induces a lift of
r_{g}

\textstyle{{g}^{\ast}\underline{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(h,m)\mapsto(h{g},m)}
\textstyle{\underline{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{{g}K{g}^{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{g}}
\textstyle{Y_{K}}

and therefore we get a pullback in cohomology
r_{{g}}^{\ast}:H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\rightarrow H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{{g}K{g}^{-1}},{g}^{\ast}\underline{M})
.

Remark 2.4.1
.

The reader may note the close relation between the map
r_{g}
and the map
\varrho_{g}
introduced in §
2.3
; indeed, one may verify that the map
\varrho_{g}
induces on cohomology is exactly
r_{g}^{*}
.

For the second pullback, we
assume
that
K
satisfies
\det(K)\subseteq Z(\mathbbm{A})\cap K
. This is true for
K=K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
in §
2.6
, for instance. Define
\mathrm{d}(g)=\det(g)^{-1}g
as a function on
G(\mathbbm{A})
. Since
\mathrm{d}
preserves
G(\mathbb{Q})
and
K_{\infty}^{+}
, our assumption on
K
implies that
\mathrm{d}
descends to a morphism

\mathrm{d}:Y_{K}\longrightarrow Y_{K}.

Suppose also that
M
has a central character. This means that
Z(\mathbbm{A})\cap G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
and
Z(\mathbbm{A})\cap K
both act through characters. We use
\chi
to denote this character. Technically, the domain of
\chi
is one of two possible groups, but there can be no confusion because
F^{\times}\cap KF_{\infty}^{\times}
acts trivially on
M
. So, we write
\chi_{\det}=\chi\circ\det
both for
\chi_{\det}:G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}\rightarrow A^{\times}
and
\chi_{\det}:K\rightarrow A^{\times}
. Define
M(\chi_{\det}^{-1})=M
as an
A
-module where the action of
g\in G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
or
g\in K
has been twisted by
\chi_{\det}^{-1}(g)
. Then,
\underline{M}(\chi_{\det}^{-1})
is naturally isomorphic to the pullback of
\underline{M}
along
\mathrm{d}
. In fact, the identity map
M(\chi_{\det}^{-1})\rightarrow M
induces a lift of
\mathrm{d}

\textstyle{\underline{M}(\chi_{\det}^{-1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(g,m)\mapsto(\mathrm{d}(g),m)}
\textstyle{\underline{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}}
\textstyle{Y_{K}.}

We denote by
\mathrm{d}^{\ast}:H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\rightarrow H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{K},\underline{M}(\chi_{\det}^{-1}))
the pullback in cohomology.

2.5.
Hecke operators

Suppose
\Delta\subseteq G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
is a submonoid and
K
is a level contained in
\Delta
. Let
M
be an abelian group with left actions of
G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
and
\Delta
that commute with one another. We equip
M
with a right
K
-module structure via
m|_{k}=k^{-1}m
. This makes
M
a
(G(\mathbb{Q})^{+},K)
-bimodule.

Now assume that, if
g\in\Delta
, then
gKg^{-1}\cap K
is finite index in
K
. Thus, for any
g\in\Delta
there is a finite decomposition
KgK=\bigsqcup_{i}g_{i}K
, where
g_{i}\in\Delta
is of the form
g_{i}=k_{i}g
and
\{k_{i}\}
are coset representatives for
K/(gKg^{-1}\cap K)
. For
\varphi\in C^{\bullet}_{?}(K,M)
and
\sigma\in C_{\bullet}(D_{\mathbbm{A}})
we define

(2.5.1)

([KgK]\varphi)(\sigma)=\sum_{i}g_{i}\varphi(\sigma g_{i}).

The action of
[KgK]
depends only on
g
and each
[KgK]
is a morphism of cochain complexes. The induced map in cohomology is written the same way. It recovers the usual Hecke action on
H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{K},\underline{M})
(see
[
BH24
, §2.2]
for instance).

We also recall the Archimedean Hecke operators. (See also
[
BH24
, §4.1]
.) Suppose

\zeta=(\zeta_{\tau})_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}}

such that
\zeta_{\tau}=\pm 1
for each
\tau
. Let
t_{\zeta}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\zeta&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in G(\mathbb{R})
. The element
t_{\zeta}
normalizes
K_{\infty}^{+}
, so
T_{\zeta}:=r_{t_{\zeta}}^{\ast}
defines an automorphism of
H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{K},\underline{M})
. It acts by involution and
T_{\zeta\zeta^{\prime}}=T_{\zeta}\circ T_{\zeta^{\prime}}
. Given a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
, define a character
\widehat{\varepsilon}:\{\pm 1\}\rightarrow\{\pm 1\}
by
\widehat{\varepsilon}(-1)=\varepsilon
. The map
\varepsilon\mapsto\widehat{\varepsilon}
extends to a group isomorphism between
\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and its dual group. For each collection of signs
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
, we let

H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{K},\underline{M})^{\varepsilon}=\{\phi\in H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\mid T_{\zeta}\phi=\widehat{\varepsilon}(\zeta)\phi\}.

If
M
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space, then each
H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{K},\underline{M})^{\varepsilon}
is a direct summand of
H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{K},\underline{M})
. It is stable by any Hecke operator
[KgK]
because
g\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
commutes with
t_{\zeta}\in G(\mathbb{R})
.

2.6.
Levels

We recall special level subgroups and Hecke operators. Fix an ideal
\mathfrak{n}\subseteq\mathscr{O}_{F}
. Define

K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in G(\widehat{\mathbb{Z}})\mid c\in\mathfrak{n}\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F}\right\}\subseteq G(\mathbbm{A}_{f}).

Write
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})=Y_{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})}
for the corresponding Hilbert modular variety. Let

\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})\cap M_{2}(\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F})\mid c\in\mathfrak{n}\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F}\text{ and }d\in\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}\text{ for each $v\mid\mathfrak{n}$}\right\}.

The pair
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})\subseteq\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})
satisfies the formalism of §
2.5
. For
v
a finite place of
F
, we write
\varpi_{v}\in F_{v}
for a uniformizer. Then, we define Hecke operators

•

If
v\nmid\mathfrak{n}
, then
T_{v}=[K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\varpi_{v}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})]
and
S_{v}=[K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\varpi_{v}&0\\
0&\varpi_{v}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})]
.

•

If
v\mid\mathfrak{n}
, then
U_{v}=[K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\varpi_{v}&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})]
.

The operators are independent of the choice of
\varpi_{v}
. They commute with one another, in the cohomology of local systems defined by
\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})
-modules.

2.7.
Polynomial representations

For the remainder of §
2
, we let
E\subseteq\mathbb{C}
be a field such that
\tau(F)\subseteq E
for all
\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}
. We also fix an algebraic weight
\lambda
, a notion that we recall now. Let
\lambda_{1}=(\lambda_{1,\tau})_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}}
and
\lambda_{2}=(\lambda_{2,\tau})_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}}
be two
\Sigma_{\infty}
-tuples of integers. We call the pair
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
a (cohomological)
algebraic weight
if:

(a)

\lambda
is
dominant
:
\lambda_{1,\tau}\geq\lambda_{2,\tau}
for all
\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}
;

(b)

\lambda
is
pure
:
\lambda_{1,\tau}+\lambda_{2,\tau}
is independent of
\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}
.

Given an algebraic weight
\lambda
and
\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}
, let
n_{\tau}=\lambda_{1,\tau}-\lambda_{2,\tau}
and
w=\lambda_{1,\tau}+\lambda_{2,\tau}
(independent of
\tau
). Set
n=(n_{\tau})_{\tau}
. The weight
\lambda
is determined by
n
and
w
because
\lambda_{1,\tau}=\frac{1}{2}(n_{\tau}+w)
and
\lambda_{2,\tau}=\frac{1}{2}(w-n_{\tau})
. So, we often write
\lambda=(n,w)
as well and rely on the context to clarify whether a pair
(-,-)
is the
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
-style or the
(n,w)
-style.

Fix
\lambda=(n,w)
. Let
E[z_{\tau}]^{\leq n_{\tau}}
be the
E
-linear polynomials of degree at most
n_{\tau}
in
z_{\tau}
. Define

\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}=\bigotimes_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}}E[z_{\tau}]^{\leq n_{\tau}}\otimes{\det}^{\lambda_{2,\tau}}.

We endow
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
with a
right

E
-linear representation of
G(E)
, as follows. Let
z=(z_{\tau})_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}}
. We use multi-index notation such as

\frac{az+b}{cz+d}=\left(\frac{a_{\tau}z_{\tau}+b_{\tau}}{c_{\tau}z_{\tau}+d_{\tau}}\right)_{\tau},

and if
j=(j_{\tau})_{\tau}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and
u=(u_{\tau})_{\tau}
, then
u^{j}=\prod u_{\tau}^{j_{\tau}}
. For
\alpha=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in G(E)\simeq\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E)^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and
P\in\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
, the right action of
\alpha
on
P
is given by:

(2.7.1)

P|_{\alpha}(z)=\det(\alpha)^{\lambda_{2}}(cz+d)^{n}P\left(\frac{az+b}{cz+d}\right).

We emphasize two points on this action. First, by assumption on
E
we have
G(\mathbb{Q})\simeq\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F)\hookrightarrow G(E)
via
\gamma\mapsto(\tau(\gamma))_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}}
for
\gamma\in G(\mathbb{Q})
. So,
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
is also a right
G(\mathbb{Q})
-representation. Second,
Z(E)\simeq(E^{\times})^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and
Z(E)
acts on
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
through the character
w_{\lambda}:Z(E)\rightarrow E^{\times}
given by

w_{\lambda}(x)=x^{w}=\prod_{\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}}x_{\tau}^{w}.

Let
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}=\mathrm{Hom}_{E}(\mathscr{L}_{\lambda},E)
be the
E
-linear dual. We make
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
a
left

E
-linear representation of
G(E)
by

(\alpha\ell)(P)=\ell(P|_{\alpha}),

for
\ell\in\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
,
P\in\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
, and
\alpha\in G(E)
. The pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}:\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\otimes_{E}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}\rightarrow E
given by

\langle\ell,P\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}=\ell(P)

is called the
canonical pairing
. If
\alpha\in G(E)
, then
\langle\alpha\ell,P\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}=\langle\ell,P|_{\alpha}\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}
.

There is a second relationship between
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
. For any
2\times 2
matrix
g
, we write
g^{\prime}=\det(g)g^{-1}
. Then, define
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}=\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
as
E
-vector spaces, but we make
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
a
left

G(E)
-representation via the action

\alpha P=P|_{\alpha^{\prime}}=w_{\lambda}(\det\alpha)P|_{\alpha^{-1}}.

With this definition,
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
as left
G(E)
-representations, as we now explain.

Let
\{z^{r}\}
, with
0\leq r\leq n
, be the standard monomial
E
-linear basis of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}=\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
. Let
\{\ell_{r}\}_{0\leq r\leq n}
be the dual basis under the canonical pairing. Then,

(2.7.2)

\theta_{\lambda}(\ell_{r})=(-1)^{r}\binom{n}{r}z^{n-r}

defines an isomorphism
\theta_{\lambda}:\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
of left
E
-linear
G(E)
-representations. Let
w_{\lambda,\det}=w_{\lambda}\circ{\det}
as a character
G(E)\rightarrow E^{\times}
. Then, the canonical pairing becomes a
G(E)
-equivariant pairing

(2.7.3)

\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\otimes_{E}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}E(w_{\lambda,\det}).

Pre-composing with
\theta_{\lambda}
, one finds a self-pairing on
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
valued in the one-dimensional representation
E(w_{\lambda,\det})
. See also
[
Hid94
, (3.1b)]
or
[
Dim05
, §1.14]
(where the
G(E)
-equivariant properties are not fully emphasized).

2.8.
The twisted Poincaré pairing

Now fix an integral ideal
\mathfrak{n}\subseteq\mathscr{O}_{F}
. We let
G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
act on
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
via the inclusion
G(\mathbb{Q})\subseteq G(E)
and let
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
act trivially. Therefore,
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
is a
(G(\mathbb{Q})^{+},K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}))
-bimodule and it defines an
E
-linear local system
\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}
on
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
. Our goal in this section is the definition and Hecke-theoretic properties of a certain pairing

H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\xrightarrow{[-,-]_{\lambda}}E

called the
twisted Poincaré pairing
.

There are two main steps in the construction. First, the canonical pairing (
2.7.3
) is used to create a scalar-valued pairing between the
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
- and
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
-valued cohomology spaces. Second,
\theta_{\lambda}
is combined with a conjugation operation (“Atkin–Lehner”) in order to create a scalar-valued pairing on the
\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}
-cohomology. The details now follow.

The canonical pairing (
2.7.3
) is an equivariant pairing

\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}:\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\otimes_{E}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(w_{\lambda,\det}^{-1})\xrightarrow{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}E

of left
G(E)
-representations. Therefore, applying cup product we find a natural pairing

(2.8.1)

H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(w_{\lambda,\det}^{-1}))\xrightarrow{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),E)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{PD}}E

that we abusively call
\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}
as well.

The central character of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
is
w_{\lambda}
. Recall from §
2.4
that
\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(w_{\lambda,\det}^{-1})
is the pullback of
\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
along
\mathrm{d}:Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})\rightarrow Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
. Therefore, we define a new canonical pairing
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}
as the composition

(2.8.2)

\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\mathrm{d}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(w_{\lambda,\det}^{-1}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}
\textstyle{E.}

Remark 2.8.1
.

The adelic determinant twist was clarified, for the authors, by
[
Dim13
, §3.9]
. A version of
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}
is also found in
[
Hid94
, §5]
and
[
Gha02
, §3.3]
. In those sources, a central character is fixed. Let us discuss the compatibility of these two approaches.

Choose a Hecke character
\chi
such that
\chi|_{F_{\infty,+}^{\times}}=w_{\lambda}|_{F_{\infty,+}^{\times}}
. Then,

\chi_{\det}(g):=\chi(\det g)\chi(\det g_{\infty})^{-1}

defines a canonical class
\chi_{\det}\in H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{E}(w_{\lambda,\det}^{-1}))
. We define
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can},\chi}
as the composition

H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{E}(w_{\lambda,\det}))\xrightarrow{\cdot\cup\chi_{\det}}H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{E})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{PD}}E.

The pairings
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}
and
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can},\chi}
agree as long as the second argument is restricted to the part of cohomology with central character
\chi
. (See also
[
GG12
, §7.5]
.)

The second step to define
[-,-]_{\lambda}
is to apply
\theta_{\lambda}
and an Atkin–Lehner twist. We already explained
\theta_{\lambda}
. To explain the twist, choose
\nu\in\mathbbm{A}_{F,f}^{\times}
such that
\nu\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F}=\mathfrak{n}\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F}
.
Define
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
by
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}=1
if
v\nmid\mathfrak{n}
and, if
v\mid\mathfrak{n}
, then

(2.8.3)

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-\nu_{v}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Since
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}){\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{-1}=K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
, we have the right multiplication map
r_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}:Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})\rightarrow Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
. Recalling §
2.4
, the action of
G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
on
\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}
is through
G(\mathbb{R})
, so
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}=\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}
. Therefore, we get an
E
-linear automorphism
r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}:H^{\ast}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\rightarrow H^{\ast}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})
. We then introduce the following:

Definition 2.8.2
.

The
twisted Poincaré pairing
is the pairing
[-,-]_{\lambda}
given by:

(2.8.4)

\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\theta_{\lambda}r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}}
\textstyle{E.}

We summarize the features of twisted Poincaré pairings in the next theorem. We consider the Hecke actions of
[K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})gK_{0}(\mathfrak{n})]
for
g\in\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})
, as well as the Archimedean Hecke operators.

Proposition 2.8.3
.

The pairing
[-,-]_{\lambda}
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

If
g\in\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})
is a diagonal matrix, then
[K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})gK_{0}(\mathfrak{n})]
is self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{\lambda}
. In particular, for each finite place
v
of
F
, the Hecke operators
T_{v}
,
S_{v}
, and
U_{v}
are self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{\lambda}
.

(ii)

If
\varepsilon,\eta\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and
\varepsilon\neq-\eta
, then
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}
is orthogonal to
H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\eta}
under
[-,-]_{\lambda}
.

(iii)

If
\varepsilon=-\eta
, then
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}
is dual to
H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\eta}
under
[-,-]_{\lambda}
.

No formal proof is given here. The pairing
[-,-]_{\lambda}
we defined agrees with the one defined in
[
Dim13
, §3.9]
. The explanations there suffice to prove parts
(i)
and
(ii)
, even if only
(i)
is directly argued for. For
(iii)
, each of
\theta_{\lambda}
,
r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}
, and
\mathrm{d}^{\ast}
are isomorphisms on cohomology. Moreover, (
2.8.1
) is perfect since it is a perfect duality combined with Poincaré duality. (See also
[
Gha02
, Proposition 2]
.) So, part
(iii)
then follows from part
(ii)
.

We will also prove
(i)
and
(ii)
in Appendix
A
. Specifically,
(i)
and
(ii)
here are equivalent to parts
(i)
and
(ii)
in Proposition
4.1.2
, which is proven as Corollary
A.4.1
in Appendix
A
. The reason the argument is arranged this way is that the Hecke-theoretic properties of these pairings are formal to establish, so the logic applies to the local systems in §
5
, which one defines using locally analytic distributions.

Remark 2.8.4
.

If
H^{\ast}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})
is non-zero,
\lambda=(n,w)
must have an even value of
w
. Twisting by the adelic norm allows the calculation of
[-,-]_{\lambda}
to be reduced to the case
w=0
, as we explain now.

Let
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\lambda=(n,w)
. Write
\lambda_{r}=(n,w+2r)
. Write
\chi_{r,\det}\in H^{0}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),E({\det}^{-r}))
for the twisting class associated with
|-|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}^{r}
, as in Remark
2.8.1
. For each
r
, we have the following pullback formulas:

(2.8.5)

\displaystyle r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}\chi_{r,\det}

\displaystyle=|\det({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}})|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}^{r}\chi_{r,\det}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{\ast}\chi_{r,\det}

\displaystyle=\chi_{-r,\det}.

Define
\mathrm{tw}_{r}(-)=-\cup\chi_{r,\det}
, as an operator on cohomology. Thus
\mathrm{tw}_{r}
defines an
E
-linear isomorphism
H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda_{r}})\rightarrow H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})
. Using the definitions and (
2.8.5
), one finds that
[-,-]_{\lambda_{r}}
is related to
[-,-]_{\lambda}
via the commuting diagram

\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda_{r}})\otimes_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda_{r}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\lambda_{r}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{tw}_{r}\otimes\mathrm{tw}_{r}}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|\det({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}})|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}^{-r}[-,-]_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{E.}

3.
The twisted Poincaré pairing and adjoint
L
-values

In this section, we explain a relationship between the value of a twisted Poincaré pairing and an
imprimitive
adjoint
L
-value. Namely, we will consider cuspidal automorphic representations
\pi
of cohomological weights
\lambda
, together with the choice of a
p
-refinement

(\pi,\alpha)
. The definition of refinement is recalled in §
3.4
. To each
(\pi,\alpha)
and sign
\varepsilon
we will associate a cohomology class
\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon}
. The main theorem (Theorem
3.8.2
and Corollary
3.8.4
) is an expression

[\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon},\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{-\varepsilon}]_{\lambda}=\frac{\omega_{\pi}(c)e_{0}(\pi)e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})}{|c|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}^{1-w}\Omega_{F}\Omega_{\infty}(\pi)\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(\pi)},

where
L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
is the target adjoint
L
-value; each of the terms in the previous formula will be carefully explained in the body of the section, but for the moment we simply remark that the terms denoted by
\Omega
are considered periods, the factors
e(-)
are considered interpolation factors, and the factors with a
c
result from technical issues with rationality at places where where
F
ramifies. (Note, the adjoint
L
-value will be an imprimitive
L
-value, as long as
\pi_{v}
is a principal series at some
p
-adic place. Therefore, our calculation is a little different from
[
Gha02
]
and
[
Dim05
]
, where primitive adjoint
L
-values are dealt with.)

Later in the article, in §
5
-
6
we will explain that
p
-refined automorphic representations comprise the classical points on eigenvarieties for Hilbert modular forms and that the pairings
[-,-]_{\lambda}
interpolate over such an eigenvariety. Therefore, the utility of the present section is that it allows us to interpret the
p
-adic interpolation of the Poincaré pairings as a
p
-adic interpolation of the adjoint
L
-value at
s=1
.

As a rough outline of this section’s contents, §
3.1
-
3.3
explains broadly how to relate
L
-values to cohomological pairings via Whittaker models and Whittaker functions, in the tradition of Shimura, Manin, and others, who first connected integral representations to cohomology. Then,
p
-adic refinements are recalled in §
3.4
. Rationality issues, including the definitions of each
\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon}
, are explained in §
3.5
. The subsections §
3.6
-
3.7
are calculations related to certain zeta integrals. In §
3.2
-
3.3
, we assume that
\pi
is unitary, i.e.
w=0
, though it is not a major loss of generality. The assumption’s impact is to make
\pi
and its contragredient have the same weight. Although, the main result in §
3.8
is only initially proven in the unitary case, we quickly remove the unitary assumption after the fact.

We set aside some notations that remain in force through this section. If
v
is any place of
F
, we let
\mathfrak{p}_{v}
be the corresponding prime ideal. Denote by
q_{v}=|\mathscr{O}_{F}/\mathfrak{p}_{v}|
the size of its residue field and
e_{v}
the ramification index. Naturally, we fix a rational prime
p
. Write
\mathbf{p}=\prod_{v\mid p}\mathfrak{p}_{v}
. Finally, fix an integral ideal
\mathfrak{n}_{0}\subseteq\mathscr{O}_{F}
that is prime to
p
. Set
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0}\mathbf{p}
.

Finally,
\lambda
will denote an algebraic weight, and
E
will denote a number field
E\subseteq\mathbb{C}
such that
\tau(F)\subseteq E
for all
\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}
, as in §
2.7
-
2.8
. The field
E
will quickly be assumed to contain the Hecke eigenvalues for a fixed automorphic representation of weight
\lambda
.

The letter
\pi
will be reserved for an irreducible cohomological cuspidal automorphic representation of
G(\mathbbm{A})
, with weight
\lambda
. Eventually we will assume that
\pi
has level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pi})
where
\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
is a factor of
\mathfrak{n}
. The central character of
\pi
is written
\omega_{\pi}
. We write
\pi^{\prime}=\pi\otimes\omega_{\pi}^{-1}
, so that
\pi^{\prime}
is the contragredient (or dual) representation attached to
\pi
.

3.1.
Whittaker models and cohomology

In this section we recall Whittaker models associated with cuspidal automorphic representations and their relation to the cohomology of Hilbert modular varieties. To start, we recall standard results on cuspidal automorphic representations and Whittaker models (see
[
Bum97
, Chapers 3-4]
and
[
RT11
]
).

Let
\pi
be as above, an irreducible, cohomological, cuspidal automorphic representation of
G(\mathbbm{A})
of weight
\lambda
. We express it as a restricted tensor product
\pi=\bigotimes_{v}\pi_{v}
, for local representations
\pi_{v}
of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F_{v})
. Let
\psi
be a non-trivial additive character
\psi:\mathbbm{A}_{F}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Its local components are denoted by
\psi_{v}:F_{v}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. (To discuss rational structures later, we will specify a choice of
\psi
.)

The local Whittaker model is the unique
\mathbb{C}
-vector space
\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\psi_{v})
of smooth functions on
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F_{v})
such that if
W\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\psi_{v})
, then

W\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
&1\end{pmatrix}g\right)=\psi_{v}(x)W(g)\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;{\text{($x\in F_{v}$ and $g\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F_{v})$)},}

and for which
\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\psi_{v})
is isomorphic to
\pi_{v}
as a left
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F_{v})
-representation. Let
V_{\pi}
be the space underlying
\pi
. For
\varphi\in V_{\pi}
we define

(3.1.1)

W_{\varphi}(g):=\int_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}/F}\varphi\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
&1\end{pmatrix}g\right)\overline{\psi(x)}\,dx\;\;\;\;\;\;\;(g\in\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbbm{A}_{F})).

The space
\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)
is the vector space obtained by taking finite linear combinations of the
W_{\varphi}
. The map
\varphi\mapsto W_{\varphi}
in fact defines an isomorphism
V_{\pi}\simeq\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)
. This is the global Whittaker model. It decomposes as a restricted tensor product of local Whittaker models
\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)=\bigotimes_{v}\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\psi_{v})
. As notation, we define
\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)=\bigotimes_{v\nmid\infty}\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\psi_{v})
.

For a level
K\subseteq G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
, let
\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K}
denote subspace of
K
-fixed vectors. Let
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}}
denote
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
with
\mathbb{C}
-linear coefficients, as in §
2.7
, and let
\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}}
denote the associated local system over
Y_{K}
. Let
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
be a collection of signs as in §
2.5
. We let
H^{d}_{?}(Y_{K},\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}[\pi_{f}^{K}]
denote the
\pi_{f}^{K}
-isotypic component. Then, we have an isomorphism

(3.1.2)

\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi):\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K}\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{d}_{!}(Y_{K},\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}[\pi_{f}^{K}],

obtained as the composition of the following isomorphisms:

(3.1.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi_{f})^{K}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K}\otimes H^{d}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty},K_{\infty}^{+};\pi_{\infty}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K}\otimes H^{d}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty},K_{\infty}^{+};\mathcal{W}(\pi_{\infty},\psi_{\infty})\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{d}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty},K_{\infty}^{+};\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)^{K}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{d}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty},K_{\infty}^{+};V_{\pi}^{K}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{d}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty},K_{\infty}^{+};C_{0}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A}))^{K}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}[\pi_{f}^{K}]

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{d}_{!}(Y_{K},\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}[\pi_{f}^{K}].

Here
C_{0}^{\infty}(G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A}))
denotes the space of cuspidal automorphic forms on
G
. The first isomorphism in (
3.1.3
) is obtained by tacking on a basis vector
[\pi_{\infty}]^{\varepsilon}=\otimes_{v|\infty}[\pi_{v}]^{\varepsilon_{v}}
for the
1
-dimensional space

H^{d}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty},K_{\infty}^{+};\pi_{\infty}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}=\bigotimes_{v\mid\infty}H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{v},K_{v}^{+};\pi_{v}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda_{v},\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon_{v}}.

The fourth isomorphism is induced by
\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)\simeq V_{\pi}
. In particular,
\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}
depends on
\psi
, even if
\psi
is removed from the notation. The maps
\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}
are compatible with Hecke actions and Atkin–Lehner operators (see (
3.2.2
), later). To explain, we must expand on the choice of
[\pi_{\infty}]^{\varepsilon}
. (See also
[
RT11
, §3.1.12]
.)

We may identify
[\pi_{\infty}]^{\varepsilon}
with its image in
H^{d}(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty},K_{\infty}^{+};\mathcal{W}(\pi_{\infty},\psi_{\infty})\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}
. The relative Lie algebra cohomology is

H^{1}(\mathfrak{g}_{v},K_{v}^{+};\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\psi_{v})\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda_{v},\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon_{v}}=\left[(\mathfrak{g}_{v}/\mathfrak{k}_{v})^{*}\otimes\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\psi_{v})\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda_{v},\mathbb{C}}\right]^{K_{v}^{+}},

where
\mathfrak{k}_{v}
is the Lie algebra of
K_{v}^{+}
. Let
X_{1,v},X_{2,v}
be a basis for
\mathfrak{g}_{v}/\mathfrak{k}_{v}
, and write
\{\ell_{r,v}\}_{r}
for the basis of
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda_{v},\mathbb{C}}
dual to the standard polynomial basis on
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda_{v},\mathbb{C}}
, as in §
2.7
. Then,

[\pi_{v}]^{\varepsilon_{v}}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\sum_{r=0}^{n_{v}}X_{i,v}^{*}\otimes W_{i,r,\varepsilon_{v},v}\otimes\ell_{r,v},

for some Whittaker functions
W_{i,r,\varepsilon_{v},v}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\psi_{v})
. Here
X_{i,v}^{*}\in(\mathfrak{g}_{v}/\mathfrak{k}_{v})^{*}
are dual vectors to
X_{i,v}
. Based on this, given
W\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)
we define a collection of automorphic forms

\varphi_{i,r,\varepsilon}=\varphi_{i,r,\varepsilon}(W)

by stipulating that
\varphi=\varphi_{i,r,\varepsilon}
satisfies

W_{\varphi}=W\otimes\bigotimes_{v\mid\infty}W_{i,r,\varepsilon_{v},v}.

(There is one such form, attached to
W
, for each
i=1,2
and
0\leq r\leq n
, and
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{v})\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
).

3.2.
Pairings on cohomology and Whittaker functions

Denote by
\pi^{\prime}
the contragredient representation of
\pi
, which in the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
case is

\pi^{\prime}=\pi\otimes\omega_{\pi}^{-1}
. We have the following Petersson pairing

\langle\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{Pet}}=\int_{Z(\mathbbm{A})G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A})}\varphi(g)\varphi^{\prime}(g)dg,

for
\varphi\in V_{\pi}
and
\varphi^{\prime}\in V_{\pi^{\prime}}
. In this section, we relate the Poincaré pairing
[-,-]_{\lambda}
to
\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{Pet}}
. The main result is (
3.2.4
), one of the key steps in using the Poincaré pairing to calculate adjoint
L
-values.

We
assume
for the rest of this subsection that
\pi
is unitary, i.e. that
\lambda=(n,w)
with
w=0
. This simplifies the duality we need to consider, since
\pi^{\prime}
now also has weight
\lambda=(n,0)
. Later, we also use the unitary assumption in analytic arguments. Finally, there is no real loss of generality because a general
\pi
is always unitary up to a twist, and we will, in Corollary
3.8.4
, integrate the easy-to-see impacts twisting has on
L
-values, cohomological pairings, and so on.

We first clarify the cohomological pairing. Let
K
be a level. Since
w=0
, the canonical pairing in (
2.7.3
) and the isomorphism
\theta_{\lambda}
gives us a
G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
-equivariant pairing

\langle-,-\rangle_{\lambda}=\langle-,\theta_{\lambda}(-)\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}:\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}}\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}.

Since
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
both contribute to cohomology in weight
\lambda
, we obtain a pairing on parabolic cohomology,

\langle-,-\rangle_{\lambda}:H^{d}_{!}(Y_{K},\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}[\pi_{f}^{K}]\otimes H^{d}_{!}(Y_{K},\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\eta}[(\pi^{\prime}_{f})^{K}]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},

which we denote by the same symbol. Note that
\langle-,-\rangle_{\lambda}
is
not
the twisted Poincaré pairing: see (
3.2.3
) below for the precise relation between the two pairings, which directly follows from the constructions.

Let
\psi^{\prime}(x)=\psi(-x)
. Given signs
\varepsilon,\eta\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and given
W\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K}
and
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f}^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})^{K}
, we define

\displaystyle\phi_{W}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi)(W)\in H^{d}_{!}(Y_{K},\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}});

\displaystyle\phi_{W^{\prime}}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}^{\eta}(\pi^{\prime})(W^{\prime})\in H^{d}_{!}(Y_{K},\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}}).

Note that one needs to make a choice of
[\pi^{\prime}_{\infty}]^{\eta}
in order to define
\mathcal{F}^{\eta}(\pi^{\prime})
. But since
\pi^{\prime}_{\infty}\simeq\pi_{\infty}
(due to the unitary assumption), we may choose
[\pi^{\prime}_{\infty}]^{\eta}=[\pi_{\infty}]^{\eta}
.
We now relate
\langle\phi^{\varepsilon}_{W},\phi^{\eta}_{W^{\prime}}\rangle_{\lambda}
to certain Petersson pairings.

In the identifications of the prior subsection, we expand the classes
\phi^{\varepsilon}_{W}
and
\phi^{\eta}_{W^{\prime}}
as:

\phi^{\varepsilon}_{W}=W\otimes\otimes_{v|\infty}\sum_{i_{v}=1}^{2}\sum_{r_{v}=0}^{n_{v}}X_{i_{v},v}^{*}\otimes W_{i_{v},r_{v},\varepsilon_{v},v}\otimes\ell_{r_{v},v}=\sum_{i=(i_{v})}\sum_{r=(r_{v})}\vartheta_{i,r,\varepsilon}\otimes\ell_{r},

and

\phi^{\eta}_{W^{\prime}}=W^{\prime}\otimes\otimes_{v|\infty}\sum_{j_{v}=1}^{2}\sum_{s_{v}=0}^{n_{v}}X_{j_{v},v}^{*}\otimes W^{\prime}_{j_{v},s_{v},\eta_{v},v}\otimes\ell_{s_{v},v}=\sum_{j=(j_{v})}\sum_{s=(s_{v})}\vartheta^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta}\otimes\ell_{s},

where we view
\vartheta_{i,r,\varepsilon}\otimes\ell_{r}
and
\vartheta^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta}\otimes\ell_{s}
as elements of

\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K}\otimes(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty}/\mathfrak{k}_{\infty})^{*}\otimes\mathcal{W}(\pi_{\infty},\psi_{\infty})\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}}

and

\mathcal{W}(\pi^{\prime}_{f},\psi^{\prime})^{K}\otimes(\mathfrak{g}_{\infty}/\mathfrak{k}_{\infty})^{*}\otimes\mathcal{W}(\pi^{\prime}_{\infty},\psi_{\infty}^{\prime})\otimes\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}}

respectively. Now let
\varphi_{i,r,\varepsilon}=\varphi_{i,r,\varepsilon}(W)\in V_{\pi}^{K}
and
\varphi^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta}=\varphi^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta}(W^{\prime})\in V^{K}_{\pi^{\prime}}
, respectively. Then, the pairing
\langle\phi^{\varepsilon}_{W},\phi^{\eta}_{W^{\prime}}\rangle_{\lambda}
is calculated as follows. We have:

\displaystyle\langle\phi^{\varepsilon}_{W},\phi^{\eta}_{W^{\prime}}\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi)(W),\mathcal{F}^{\eta}(\pi^{\prime})(W^{\prime})\rangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\int_{[Y_{K}]}\sum_{i,j,r,s}\langle\ell_{r},\ell_{s}\rangle_{\lambda}(\vartheta_{i,r,\varepsilon}\wedge\vartheta^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}(K)}\int_{G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A})/K_{\infty}^{+}K}\sum_{i,j,r,s}\langle\ell_{r},\ell_{s}\rangle_{\lambda}(\vartheta_{i,r,\varepsilon}\wedge\vartheta^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}(K)}\int_{Z_{\infty}^{+}G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A})/K}\sum_{i,j,r,s}\langle\ell_{r},\ell_{s}\rangle_{\lambda}(\vartheta_{i,r,\varepsilon}\wedge\vartheta^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta})

\displaystyle\hskip 108.405pt\left[\textrm{since the integrand is invariant under $K_{\infty}^{+}$}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon\eta(-1)}{2^{d}\mathrm{Vol}(K)}\int_{Z_{\infty}G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A})/K}\sum_{i,j,r,s}\langle\ell_{r},\ell_{s}\rangle_{\lambda}(\vartheta_{i,r,\varepsilon}\wedge\vartheta^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta})

\displaystyle\hskip 108.405pt\left[\textrm{since the action of $Z_{\infty}/Z_{\infty}^{+}$ is via $\varepsilon\eta$}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon\eta(-1)h_{K}}{2^{d}\mathrm{Vol}(K)}\int_{Z(\mathbbm{A})G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A})/K}\sum_{i,j,r,s}\langle\ell_{r},\ell_{s}\rangle_{\lambda}(\vartheta_{i,r,\varepsilon}\wedge\vartheta^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta})

\displaystyle\hskip 108.405pt\left[\textrm{where $h_{K}=\mathrm{Vol}(Z(\mathbb{Q})\backslash Z(\mathbbm{A}_{f})/K\cap Z(\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F})$}\right]
