\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon\eta(-1)h_{K}}{2^{d}}\int_{Z(\mathbbm{A})G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A})}\sum_{i,j,r,s}\langle\ell_{r},\ell_{s}\rangle_{\lambda}(\vartheta_{i,r,\varepsilon}\wedge\vartheta^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta})

\displaystyle\hskip 108.405pt\left[\textrm{since the integrand is $K$-invariant}\right].

Define
s(i,j)\in\{1,-1,0\}
according to
X_{i}^{\ast}\wedge X_{j}^{\ast}=s(i,j)X_{1}^{\ast}\wedge X_{2}^{\ast}
.
Interchanging the integral and the summation on the right-hand side of the last equation, we have

(3.2.1)

\langle\phi^{\varepsilon}_{W},\phi^{\eta}_{W^{\prime}}\rangle_{\lambda}=\frac{\varepsilon\eta(-1)h_{K}}{2^{d}}\sum_{i,j,r,s}s(i,j)\langle\ell_{r},\ell_{s}\rangle_{\lambda}\langle\varphi_{i,r,\varepsilon},\varphi^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta}\rangle_{\mathrm{Pet}}.

Let us discuss the Atkin–Lehner operators, now. Assume, for the remainder of this subsection, that
K=K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
is chosen as in §
2.8
. For
W\in\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}}\mathcal{W}(\pi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})

\displaystyle R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}(W)(g)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\pi}^{-1}(\det g)W\left(\begin{pmatrix}-1&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}g{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\right).

The operator
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}
satisfies basic properties. First, since
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}
normalizes
K
, the map
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}
maps
K
-fixed vectors to
K
-fixed vectors. Second, if
W
is expressed as a pure tensor
W=\bigotimes_{v}W_{v}
, then
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}(W)
would be expressed as a pure tensor
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}(W)=\bigotimes_{v}R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{v})
for natural local analogues of the
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}
-construction. Then, for each sign
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
, it is straightforward from the definitions to show the diagram

(3.2.2)

\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi)}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{!}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}[(\pi_{f})^{K}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}^{*}r_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(\pi^{\prime}_{f},\psi^{\prime})^{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi^{\prime})}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{!}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}[(\pi^{\prime}_{f})^{K}].}

is commuting. Indeed, the purpose of left multiplication by
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
in the definition of
R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}
is only to change the additive character from
\psi
to
\psi^{\prime}
.
More precisely, the map
\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi^{\prime})
depends on our fixed isomorphism between
\mathcal{W}(\pi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\simeq V_{\pi^{\prime}}
, which factors as a composition

\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(\pi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{V_{\pi^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(\pi^{\prime},\psi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

where the map
\mathcal{W}(\pi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{W}(\pi^{\prime},\psi)
is given by
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
. As a consequence,
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
does not appear in the right vertical map of (
3.2.2
).

Finally, let
W\in\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)
and set
W^{\prime}=R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}(W)
. The relationship between
\langle-,-\rangle_{\lambda}
and
[-,-]_{\lambda}
is given by

(3.2.3)

[\phi^{\varepsilon},\phi^{\eta}]_{\lambda}=\langle\phi^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\ast}r_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\phi^{\eta}\rangle_{\lambda}\;\;\;\;\;\;\left(\phi^{?}\in H^{d}_{!}(Y_{K},\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{?}\right),

and so we can now calculate the cohomological pairing
[\phi_{W}^{\varepsilon},\phi_{W}^{\eta}]_{\lambda}
in terms of Petersson products:

(3.2.4)

\displaystyle[\phi_{W}^{\varepsilon},\phi_{W}^{\eta}]_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\langle\phi_{W}^{\varepsilon},\mathrm{d}^{\ast}r_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\phi_{W}^{\eta}\rangle_{\lambda}

(by (
3.2.3
))

\displaystyle=\langle\phi_{W}^{\varepsilon},\phi^{\eta}_{R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}(W)}\rangle_{\lambda}

(by (
3.2.2
))

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon\eta(-1)h_{K}}{2^{d}}\sum_{i,j,r,s}s(i,j)\langle\ell_{r},\ell_{s}\rangle_{\lambda}\langle\varphi_{i,r,\varepsilon},\varphi^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta}\rangle_{\mathrm{Pet}}

\displaystyle\text{(by \eqref{eqn:Poincare-pairing-Petersson-pairing})},

where
\varphi_{i,r,\varepsilon}=\varphi_{i,r,\varepsilon}(W)
and
\varphi^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta}=\varphi^{\prime}_{j,s,\eta}(R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}(W))
.

3.3.
Eulerian integral expression for Rankin–Selberg convolutions

In this subsection, we continue to assume that
\pi
is unitary. The goal is relating Petersson products to adjoint
L
-values and thus by the prior section (see (
3.2.4
)) we have conceptually related the Poincaré product to adjoint
L
-values.

Let
\Phi
be a Bruhat–Schwartz function on
\mathbbm{A}_{F}^{2}
and
E(s,g,\Phi)
be the Epstein–Eisenstein series associated with
\Phi
and the trivial character. For
\varphi\in V_{\pi}
and
\varphi^{\prime}\in V_{\pi^{\prime}}
, define

I(s,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},\Phi)=\int_{G(\mathbb{Q})Z(\mathbbm{A})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A})}\varphi(g)\varphi^{\prime}(g)E(s,g,\Phi)dg.

This integral has a meromorphic continuation to all of
s\in\mathbb{C}
with a simple pole at
s=1
. The relationship with the Petersson product is

(3.3.1)

\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}I(s,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},\Phi)=\frac{\mathrm{vol}(F^{\times}\backslash\mathbbm{A}_{F}^{1})}{2}\widehat{\Phi}(0)\langle\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{Pet}},

where
\widehat{\Phi}
is the Fourier transform of
\Phi
. (See
[
BR17
, Eqn. (2.1.1)]
.) If
\widehat{\Phi}(0)\neq 0
, we rewrite this as

(3.3.2)

\langle\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{Pet}}=\frac{2}{\mathrm{vol}(F^{\times}\backslash\mathbbm{A}_{F}^{1})\widehat{\Phi}(0)}\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}I(s,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},\Phi).

We now find a second expression for this residue in a favorable situation. Let
W_{\varphi}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi,\psi)
and
W^{\prime}_{\varphi^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
be the Whittaker functions associated with
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
respectively. Let
N\subseteq G
be the restriction of scalars of the upper-triangular unipotent matrices in
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
. A standard unfolding argument gives us

(3.3.3)

I(s,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},\Phi)=\Psi(s,W_{\varphi},W_{\varphi}^{\prime},\Phi):=\int_{N(\mathbbm{A})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A})}\Phi(eg)W_{\varphi}(g)W_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{\prime}(g)|\det g|^{s}dg,

where
e=(0\ 1)\in\mathbbm{A}_{F}^{2}
. Either side has meromorphic continuation to
s\in\mathbb{C}
, and the equality holds for
\Re(s)
large enough, see
[
JS81
, §4.5]
.

Now assume that the
W_{\varphi}
,
W_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{\prime}
, and
\Phi
are pure tensors, with factors
W_{v}
,
W_{v}^{\prime}
, and
\Phi_{v}
at places
v
of
F
, respectively. The zeta integral
\Psi(s,W_{\varphi},W^{\prime}_{\varphi^{\prime}},\Phi)
then factors

\Psi(s,W_{\varphi},W_{\varphi^{\prime}}^{\prime},\Phi)=\prod_{v}\Psi_{v}(s,W_{v},W_{v}^{\prime},\Phi_{v}),

where

\Psi_{v}(s,W_{v},W_{v}^{\prime},\Phi_{v})=\int_{N(F_{v})\backslash G(F_{v})}\Phi_{v}(eg)W_{v}(g)W_{v}^{\prime}(g)|\det g|^{s}dg

are the local zeta integrals. This is the Eulerian expression for
I
.

We will use this Eulerian expression in the following context. Assume that
K=K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
and let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}
once more be the Atkin–Lehner element. In §
3.4
-
3.5
we will specify specific choices of
W
, a pure tensor, and then define
W^{\prime}=R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}(W)
. For these choices, the computations in §
3.6
-
3.7
will result in
\Psi_{v}(s,W_{v},W_{v}^{\prime},\Phi_{v})=L(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime})
for almost all
v
. Assuming this for the moment, we find a natural formal expression

(3.3.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}I(s,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},\Phi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}\left(L(s,\pi\times\pi^{\prime})\right)\prod_{v}\frac{\Psi_{v}(1,W_{v},W_{v}^{\prime},\Phi_{v})}{L(1,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}(\zeta_{F}(s))L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})\prod_{v}\frac{\Psi_{v}(1,W_{v},W_{v}^{\prime},\Phi_{v})}{L(1,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime})},

where the products are in fact finite. (Here
\zeta_{F}(s)
is the Dedekind zeta function for
F
.) Therefore, up to making the specific choices we plan to make, the series of equations (
3.2.4
), (
3.3.2
), and (
3.3.4
) link the values of the twisted Poincaré pairing to the adjoint
L
-value at
s=1
, at least in the case where
\pi
is unitary.

3.4.
Recollection on refined automorphic representations

In this section, we begin to clarify the choices we make in applying the theory of the previous three sections. We start by recalling the notion of a
p
-refinement and a multiplicity one statement. In the next subsection, we will make choices of (
p
-refined) Whittaker functions to apply to the previous theory.

Unlike the prior two sections, we allow
\pi
to have weight
\lambda=(n,w)
with any
w
now. We assume that
\pi
has level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pi})
, where
\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}/\mathfrak{n}_{0}
is a divisor of
\mathbf{p}
. We then say
\pi
has
tame level

K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0})
. For each finite place, we use
m_{v}
for the conductor of
\pi_{v}
, which is to say
\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}\mathscr{O}_{v}=\varpi_{v}^{m_{v}}\mathscr{O}_{v}
. Denote by
K_{0}(\varpi_{v}^{m_{v}})
the corresponding local factor of
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pi})
. If
v
is a finite place of
F
, we denote by
a_{\pi}(v)
the
v
-th Hecke eigenvalue associated with
\pi
, which is to say the eigenvalue of
T_{v}
(or
U_{v}
if
v\mid\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
) acting on the one-dimensional space
\pi_{v}^{K_{0}(\varpi_{v}^{m_{v}})}
of new vectors (see Casselman
[
Cas73
]
for more details). We let
E\subseteq\mathbb{C}
be large enough to contain each
a_{\pi}(v)
and
\omega_{\pi}(\varpi_{v})
, where
\varpi_{v}
is a local uniformizer at
v
.

The representation
\pi
is
p
-refineable
(see
[
BH24
, Definition 3.4.2]
) if for each
v\mid p
, the local factor
\pi_{v}
is either an unramified twist of the Steinberg representation, or
\pi_{v}
is an unramified principal series. To set normalizations, we explicitly have the following.

(a)

Suppose
\pi_{v}
is an unramified principal series. Then, there exists unramified characters
\{\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\}
on
F_{v}^{\times}
such that
\chi_{1}\chi_{2}^{-1}\neq|\cdot|^{\pm 1}
and
\pi_{v}
is the representation induced by right translation on the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space

V_{\pi_{v}}=\pi(\chi_{1},\chi_{2}):=\left\{f:\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F_{v})\xrightarrow{\text{smooth}}\mathbb{C}\mid f\left(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
&d\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)g\right)=\chi_{1}(a)\chi_{2}(d)|a/d|^{1/2}f(g)\right\}.

We have
a_{\pi}(v)=q^{1/2}(\chi_{1}(\varpi_{v})+\chi_{2}(\varpi_{v}))
, and the Hecke polynomial
P_{v}(X)
factors

P_{v}(X)=X^{2}-a_{\pi}(v)X+\omega_{\pi}(\varpi_{v})q_{v}=(X-\alpha_{v})(X-\beta_{v})

over
E
(enlarge
E
if we have to).

(b)

If
\pi
is the twist of Steinberg by an unramified character
\chi
, then
\pi_{v}
is the representation induced by right translation
on a co-dimension one subspace
V_{\pi_{v}}\hookrightarrow\pi(\chi|\cdot|^{1/2},\chi|\cdot|^{-1/2})
. The
U_{v}
-eigenvalue acting on
\pi
is equal to
a_{\pi}(v)=\chi(\varpi_{v})
.

In the Steinberg case, we
define

\alpha_{v}=a_{\pi}(v)
. In the principal series case, we make a choice of the root
\alpha_{v}
of
P_{v}(X)
. A
p
-refinement
of
\pi
is then the choice of
\alpha=(\alpha_{v})_{v\mid p}
. We refer to
(\pi,\alpha)
as a
p
-refined
automorphic representation of tame level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0})
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be the
E
-algebra generated by symbols
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
if
v\nmid\mathfrak{n}
and
U_{v}
if
v\mid p
. Thus
\mathbb{T}
acts by
E
-linear endomorphism on
H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}
, for each sign
\varepsilon\in\{-1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and
?=\emptyset,c
. Let
\psi_{\pi,\alpha}:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow E
be the
E
-algebra determined by:

\psi_{\pi,\alpha}(T)=\begin{cases}a_{v}(\pi)&\text{if $T=T_{v}$ for $v\nmid\mathfrak{n}$;}\\
\omega_{\pi}(\varpi_{v})&\text{if $T=S_{v}$ for $v\nmid\mathfrak{n}$;}\\
\alpha_{v}&\text{if $T=U_{v}$ for $v\mid p$.}\end{cases}

(The notation of
\psi
here should not conflict conceptually with the additive character
\psi
in the prior sections.) Let
H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}
be the
generalized
eigenspace for the action of
\mathbb{T}
with respect to
\psi_{\pi,\alpha}
, while
H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}[\psi_{\pi,\alpha}]
denotes the eigenspace. In a similar fashion,
\pi_{f}^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})}
is equipped with a natural action of
\mathbb{T}
, and we write
\pi_{f}^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})}[\psi_{\pi,\alpha}]
or
(\pi_{f}^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})})_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}
for the eigenspace and generalized eigenspace, respectively.

Proposition 3.4.1
.

Assume that if
v\mid p
and
\pi_{v}
is an unramified principal series, then
\alpha_{v}\neq\beta_{v}
. Let
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
. Then the following hold.

(i)

The natural map
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}\longrightarrow H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}
is an isomorphism.

(ii)

\dim_{E}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}=\dim_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}=1
.

In particular, the inclusions

H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}[\psi_{\pi,\alpha}]\subseteq H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}

are equalities of one-dimensional vector spaces.

Proof.

For part
(i)
, the natural map
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}\rightarrow H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}
is
\mathbb{T}
-equivariant, so induces a map

(3.4.1)

H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}\longrightarrow H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}.

We also have
H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}=H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}[(\pi_{f}^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})})_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}]
. Since
\pi
is cuspidal, and by a result of Harder
[
Har87
]
the map

H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\rightarrow H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})
has Eisenstein kernel and cokernel, we conclude that the map (
3.4.1
) is an isomorphism.

For part
(ii)
, it suffices to replace
E
by
\mathbb{C}
.
Let
H^{d}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})
denote the cuspidal cohomology group of
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
with coefficients in
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}}
; the reader is referred to
[
Bor83
]
or
[
RT11
, §3.1.1]
for a precise definition.
Then, we first observe that the composition of maps

H^{d}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})\longrightarrow H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})\longrightarrow H^{d}_{!}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})

is an injection (see
[
Clo90
, p. 129]
) with Eisenstein cokernel (by Harder’s work).
Hence we have a sequence of isomorphisms

H^{d}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}\simeq H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}\simeq H^{d}_{!}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}=H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}.

We are thus reduced to show that the generalized eigenspace

H^{d}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}
has dimension
1
. From the explicit description of cuspidal cohomology (see
[
RT11
, eqn. (3.1)]
for e.g.), we have
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{d}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\pi_{f}^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})})_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}
and we are reduced to an analysis of automorphic forms. If
v\nmid\mathfrak{n}
or
v\mid p
and
\pi_{v}
is an unramified twist of Steinberg, then
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{v}^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})_{v}}=1
by the theory of the new vector. If
v\mid p
and
\pi_{v}
is an unramified principal series, then
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\pi_{v}^{K_{0}(\varpi_{v})}=2
. The Hecke operator
U_{v}
acts on
\pi_{v}^{K_{0}(\varpi_{v})}
with characteristic polynomial
P_{v}(X)
, which we have assumed has distinct roots. By choosing one of those roots,
\alpha
, we are forced to conclude
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\pi_{f}^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})})_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}=1
.

We finally deduce that the generalized eigenspaces

H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}
is equal to the eigenspaces

H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}[\psi_{\pi,\alpha}]
, completing the proof of the proposition.

∎

Proposition 3.4.2
.

Choose, for each
\varepsilon
, any non-zero vector
\phi^{\varepsilon}\in H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}[\psi_{\pi,\alpha}]
. Then
[\phi^{\varepsilon},\phi^{\eta}]\neq 0
if and only if
\eta=-\varepsilon
.

Proof.

It is clear from part
(ii)
of Proposition
2.8.3
that if
\eta\neq-\varepsilon
, then
[\phi^{\varepsilon},\phi^{\eta}]_{\lambda}=0
. Therefore, by parts
(i)
and
(iii)
of Proposition
2.8.3
, the pairing
[-,-]_{\lambda}
induces a
perfect
pairing

H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}^{\varepsilon}\otimes_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})_{\psi_{\pi,\alpha}}^{-\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{[-,-]_{\lambda}}E.

By part
(ii)
of Proposition
3.4.1
, each of these vector spaces is one-dimensional and spanned by the chosen non-zero vectors
\phi^{\pm\varepsilon}
. The result follows.
∎

3.5.
Rational structures and new vectors for
p
-refined automorphic representations

In this subsection, we fix a
p
-refined automorphic representation
(\pi,\alpha)
as in the prior subsection. (In particular, we still do
not
assume
w_{\lambda}=0
.) Our goal is to make special choices of Whittaker functions associated with
(\pi,\alpha)
. The initial discussion is independent of
\alpha
.

Both sides of (
3.1.2
) have canonical
E
-structures. The rational structure on Whittaker models is given in
[
RT11
, §3.2.3-3.2.4]
. Namely, the
E
-rational structure on
\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)
is the largest
E
-linear
G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
-representation containing the vector
W_{\pi}
that we define below. (Technically, this is the content of
[
RT11
, Proposition 3.14]
.)
On the cohomological side, we have the
E
-linear representation
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
, so that there is an isomorphism
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\otimes_{E}\mathbb{C}\simeq\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}}
. In this way,
H^{d}_{!}(Y_{K},\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}
gives a rational structure for
H^{d}_{!}(Y_{K},\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}
. The map
\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi)
does
not
respect these rational structures on both sides. However, there exists a period
\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that for any level
K\subseteq G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
, the normalized map

\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon,\circ}(\pi):=\frac{1}{\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)}\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi):\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K}\longrightarrow H^{d}_{!}(Y_{K},\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,\mathbb{C}})^{\varepsilon}[\pi_{f}^{K}]

does
respect rational structures on both sides. Indeed, if
K=K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pi})
, then left-hand side is one-dimensional and
\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)
is chosen to rationalize
\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}
on that one-dimensional space. The case of a general
K
follows, since the
E
-rational structure on
\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pi})}
generates the
E
-rational structure on
\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)
as a
G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
-representation (with the action preserving the rational structure). We refer to
\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)
as a
period
for
\pi
.

We choose the rational vectors in the Whittaker model that are chosen in
[
RT11
, Section 3.3.1]
. We will calculate with them, so let us specify them by formulas. First, we fix the specific additive character
\psi
of
[
RT11
, Section 2.8]
. Namely, for
\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}
being Tate’s choice of additive character on
\mathbbm{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}
we choose
\psi=\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}\circ\operatorname{tr}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}
. Let
\mathfrak{D}_{F}
be the different ideal. The local components
\psi_{v}
of
\psi
thus have conductor ideal
\mathfrak{D}_{F}^{-1}\mathscr{O}_{v}=\varpi_{v}^{-\delta_{v}}\mathscr{O}_{v}
. Set
c_{v}=\varpi_{v}^{\delta_{v}}\in\mathscr{O}_{v}
, and
c=(c_{v})_{v}\in\mathbbm{A}_{F,f}
. Now define

\widetilde{\psi}(x)=\psi(c^{-1}x).

Thus
\widetilde{\psi}
is a non-trivial additive character with local conductor
\mathscr{O}_{v}
at each finite place. (The character
\widetilde{\psi}
is written
\psi^{\prime}
in
[
RT11
, Section 3.3.1]
, but we already used that notation for the scalar
\psi^{\prime}(x)=\psi(-x)
.) For each finite place
v
, we have a natural
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(F_{v})
-equivariant isomorphism

\displaystyle L_{c_{v}}:\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\psi_{v})

\displaystyle(L_{c_{v}}W)(g)

\displaystyle=W\left(\begin{pmatrix}c_{v}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}g\right).

The rational vectors first depend on the normalized new vectors in
\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})
. Recall the conductor of
\pi_{v}
is denoted by
m_{v}
. We choose
W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})^{K_{0}(\varpi_{v}^{m_{v}})}
to be the unique non-zero vector such that
W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}(1)=1
. As explained in
[
RT11
, §3.3.1]
, we have the following.

(I)

If
\pi_{v}=\pi(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})
with
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
unramified characters such that
\chi_{1}\chi_{2}^{-1}\neq|\cdot|^{\pm 1}
, and
\{\alpha_{v},\beta_{v}\}=\{q_{v}^{1/2}\chi_{1}(\varpi_{v}),q_{v}^{1/2}\chi_{2}(\varpi_{v})\}
, then,

W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}\varpi_{v}^{m}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)=\begin{cases}q_{v}^{-m}\sum_{\ell=0}^{m}\alpha_{v}^{\ell}\beta_{v}^{m-\ell}&\text{if $m\geq 0$;}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(II)

If
\chi
is an unramified character, let
\pi_{v}\subseteq\pi(\chi|\cdot|^{1/2},\chi|\cdot|^{-1/2})
be the corresponding twist of Steinberg. Let
\alpha_{v}=\chi(\varpi_{v})
be the
U_{v}
-eigenvalue. Then,

W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}\varpi_{v}^{m}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)=\begin{cases}q_{v}^{-m}\alpha_{v}^{m}&\text{if $m\geq 0$;}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(III)

If
\pi_{v}
is supercuspidal, then

W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}\varpi_{v}^{m}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $m=0$;}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We now define:

W_{\pi_{v}}=L_{c_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}).

Thus, for each integer
m
, we have

W_{\pi_{v}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}\varpi^{m-\delta_{v}}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)=W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}\varpi^{m}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right).

The element
W_{\pi}=\bigotimes_{v}W_{\pi_{v}}
is a
rational
vector in
\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)
, according to
[
RT11
, Proposition 3.14]
. (The vector
W_{\pi}
is denoted
W^{\circ}_{\pi}
in
[
RT11
]
.)

We now apply the process of refinement to the rational vectors. Recall that we fixed a refinement
\alpha=(\alpha_{v})_{v\mid p}
. If
v\mid p
and
\pi_{v}
is a twist of Steinberg we
define

W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}=W_{\pi_{v}}
. If
v\mid p
but
\pi_{v}
is an unramified principal series, then we set

(3.5.1)

W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}(g)=W_{\pi_{v}}(g)-\beta_{v}q_{v}^{-1}W_{\pi_{v}}\left(g\begin{pmatrix}\varpi_{v}^{-1}\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right).

In both cases,
W_{\pi_{v},\alpha}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\psi_{v})^{K_{0}(\varpi_{v})}
is uniquely determined by

(3.5.2)

W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}\varpi_{v}^{m-\delta_{v}}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)=\begin{cases}q_{v}^{-m}\alpha_{v}^{m}&\text{if $m\geq 0$;}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

With these specifications made, we now define
W_{\pi,\alpha}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})}
by

(3.5.3)

W_{\pi,\alpha}=\bigotimes_{v\nmid p}W_{\pi_{v}}\otimes\bigotimes_{v\mid p}W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}.

Then,
W_{\pi,\alpha}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})}
is
E
-rational. Indeed,
W_{\pi,\alpha}
is in the
E
-linear
G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
-representation generated by
W_{\pi}
. Our next goal is calculating the local zeta integrals, associated with the
p
-refined rational vectors
W_{\pi,\alpha}
, that appeared at the end of §
3.3
. This is achieved in the next two sections.

3.6.
Atkin–Lehner actions on Whittaker functions

The goal of this subsection is to determine
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}})
for
v\mid\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
. Set
\widetilde{\psi}_{v}^{\prime}(x)=\widetilde{\psi}_{v}(-x)=\psi_{v}(-c_{v}^{-1}x)
and for
v\mid\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
set

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}=\begin{pmatrix}&1\\
-\varpi_{v}^{m_{v}}\end{pmatrix}.

Recall that
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}:\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})\rightarrow\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v}^{\prime})
is defined as:

(R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}W)(g)=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(\det g)^{-1}W\left(\begin{pmatrix}-1&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}g{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}\right).

Proposition 3.6.1
.

Let
v\mid\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
.

(i)

If
\pi_{v}=\operatorname{St}\otimes\chi
is an unramified twist of Steinberg, then

R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}})=-\alpha_{v}W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}}.

(ii)

If
\pi_{v}
is supercuspidal and
\epsilon(s,\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})
is the
\epsilon
-factor associated with
\pi_{v}
and
\widetilde{\psi}_{v}
, then

R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}})=\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}}.

Proof.

In either case,
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}
normalizes
K_{0}(\varpi_{v}^{m_{v}})
and therefore
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}})=CW_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}}
for some
C\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
that we need to find. In this proof, we introduce
w=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}&-1\\
1&\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
for the Weyl element. (It should not clash with the weight.) We will use
[
Sch02
]
as a convenient reference for these calculations, though the theory goes back at least to Jacquet and Langlands
[
JL70
]
.

For
(i)
, let
\pi_{v}=\operatorname{St}\otimes\chi
, with
\chi
unramified. We use the Whittaker model
\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})
given in
[
Sch02
]
. Namely,
\pi_{v}\subseteq\pi(\chi|\cdot|^{1/2},\chi|\cdot|^{-1/2})
and an isomorphism
\xi_{\widetilde{\psi}_{v}}:\pi_{\pi_{v}}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})
is given by, for
\varphi\in V_{\pi_{v}}
,

(\xi_{\widetilde{\psi}_{v}}\varphi)(g)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\varpi_{v}^{-N}\mathscr{O}_{v}}\varphi\left(w\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
&1\end{pmatrix}g\right)\widetilde{\psi}_{v}(-x)\;dx.

(See
[
Sch02
, Eq. (27)]
for the description of the Whittaker functional associated with this Whittaker model.) For
\varphi\in V_{\pi_{v}}
, define
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(\varphi)(g)=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(\varpi_{v})^{-1}\varphi(g{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v})
. Then, one checks that the diagram

\textstyle{{\pi_{v}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{\widetilde{\psi}_{v}}}
\scriptstyle{R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}}
\textstyle{{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{\widetilde{\psi}_{v}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v}^{\prime},\widetilde{\psi}_{v}^{\prime}),}

commutes, using crucially that
\varphi
is associated with an
unramified
twist in Steinberg and so left multiplication by
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
does not impact the values of
\varphi
lying in the principal series
\pi(\chi|\cdot|^{1/2},\chi|\cdot|^{-1/2})
.

Now let
\varphi_{\pi_{v}}=\xi_{\widetilde{\psi}_{v}}^{-1}(W_{\pi}^{\mathrm{new}})
and
\varphi_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}=\xi_{\widetilde{\psi}_{v}^{\prime}}^{-1}(W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}})
. Then,
[
Sch02
, Eq. (37)]
implies that
\varphi_{\pi_{v}}
and
\varphi_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}
are the unique
K_{0}(\varpi_{v})
-fixed vector in the respective representation spaces
such that, letting
\varphi
denote any of the two vectors
\varphi_{\pi_{v}}
and
\varphi_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}
,
we have

\varphi(1)=\frac{-q}{1+q^{-1}},\;\;\;\;\;\varphi(w)=\frac{1}{1+q^{-1}}.

Therefore, part
(i)
is reduced to showing:

(3.6.1)

R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(\varphi_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}})=-\alpha_{v}\varphi_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}}=-\chi(\varpi_{v})\varphi_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}},

where now
\varphi_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}
is normalized by
\varphi_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}(1)=q
and
\varphi_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}(w)=-1
(and similar for
\varphi_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}})
. The proof of (
3.6.1
) is now a straightforward calculation by evaluating both sides at
g=1
. See the proof of
[
Sch02
, Proposition 3.1.2(i)]
for the relevant details. (The assumption in
loc. cit.
that
\omega_{\pi_{v}}
is trivial is immaterial to justifying (
3.6.1
). Its role there is in interpreting
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}
as an automorphism of
\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})
, removing the need to be as careful with normalizations as we have been here.)

Now we prove
(ii)
. In this case, we also refer to calculations of Schmidt. Since
W_{\ast}^{\mathrm{new}}(1)=1
, our goal is to calculate
C=(R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}})(1)
. Thus

C=W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}&1\\
-\varpi_{v}^{m_{v}}\end{pmatrix}\right)=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(\varpi_{v})^{m_{v}}W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}-\varpi_{v}^{-m_{v}}\\
&1\end{pmatrix}w\right).

Here, we used that
\omega_{\pi_{v}}
is unramified (since
\pi_{v}
has level
K_{0}(-)
). Now,
wW_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}
is calculated in the proof of
[
Sch02
, Theorem 3.2.2]
. Namely,
loc. cit.
proves a formula for
wW_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}
assuming
\omega_{\pi_{v}}
is trivial (using that
\widetilde{\psi}_{v}
has conductor
\mathscr{O}_{v}
). The argument, however, can be modified to see that

\displaystyle wW_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}-\varpi^{-m_{v}}\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\varpi_{\pi_{v}}(\varpi_{v})^{-m_{v}}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(\varpi_{v})^{-m_{v}}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})

in general. Putting this together, we find
C=\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})
.
∎

Remark 3.6.2
.

The statement of Proposition
3.6.1
(ii)
also holds in the Steinberg case
(i)
. (To see this, one examines the proofs in
[
Sch02
]
that we referenced.) We prefer the expression in
(i)
as it highlights a Hecke eigenvalue. Separately, the relationship between
\epsilon
-factors and the action of
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}
, for Steinberg representations of general linear groups of higher rank, is also explained in
[
KY12
]
.

3.7.
Calculations of local zeta integrals

This subsection is dedicated entirely to calculating local zeta integrals. Those integrals were defined in §
3.3
under the assumption that
\pi
is unitary, but we do
not
need that assumption at this point. The propositions are presented as a pair, since the majority of the calculation occurs in the proof of the second proposition, covering the most interesting case where
v\mid p
and
\pi_{v}
is an unramified principal series.

We start discussing the cases when
v\nmid\mathfrak{n}
or
v\mid\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
in the next Proposition
3.7.1
.
See Proposition
3.7.3
below for the remaining calculations at
v\mid p
.

Proposition 3.7.1
.

We have the following.

(i)

Suppose
v\nmid\mathfrak{n}
. Let
\Phi_{v}
denote the characteristic function of
\mathscr{O}^{2}_{F,v}
. Then,

\Psi_{v}(1,W_{\pi_{v}},R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}),\Phi_{v})=\omega_{\pi}(c_{v})|c_{v}|^{-1}L(1,\pi_{v}\times\pi^{\prime}_{v}).

(ii)

Suppose that
v\mid\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
and
\pi_{v}
is Steinberg. Let
\Phi_{v}
be the characteristic function of
\varpi_{v}\mathscr{O}_{v}\times\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}
. Then,

\Psi_{v}(1,W_{\pi_{v}},R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}),\Phi_{v})=\frac{-\alpha_{v}\omega_{\pi_{v}}(c_{v})|c_{v}|^{-1}}{1-q_{v}^{-2}}.

(iii)

Suppose
v\mid\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
and
\pi_{v}
is supercuspidal. Let
\Phi_{v}
denote the characteristic function of
\varpi_{v}^{m_{v}}\mathscr{O}_{F,v}\times\mathscr{O}^{\times}_{F,v}
. Then,

\Psi_{v}(1,W_{\pi_{v}},R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}),\Phi_{v})=|c_{v}|^{-1}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi_{v}).

Proof.

We start with two observations. First, if
W\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})
, then
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}L_{c_{v}}(W)=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(c_{v})L_{c_{v}}R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W)
. Second, if also
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v}^{\prime})
, then

\Psi_{v}(s,L_{c_{v}}W,L_{c_{v}}W^{\prime},\Phi)=|c_{v}|^{-s}\Psi_{v}(s,W,W^{\prime},\Phi).

Therefore, in all three cases, we find:

(3.7.1)

\displaystyle\Psi_{v}(s,W_{\pi_{v}},R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}),\Phi_{v})

\displaystyle=\Psi_{v}(s,L_{c_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}),R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}L_{c_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}),\Phi_{v})

\displaystyle=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(c_{v})|c_{v}|^{-s}\Psi_{v}(s,W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}},R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}),\Phi_{v}).

We now argue the proposition case-by-case after replacing
W_{\pi_{v}}
by
W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}
.

First suppose
v\nmid\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
. Then,
R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}})=W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}}
and we have the well-known calculation

\Psi_{v}(s,W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}},W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}},\Phi_{v})=L(s,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime}).

This proves part
(i)
, following (
3.7.1
).

In case
(ii)
we assume
\pi_{v}
is Steinberg. By part
(i)
of Proposition
3.6.1
we have

R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}})=-\alpha_{v}W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}}.

Thus,
(ii)
is reduced to

\Psi_{v}(s,W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}},W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}},\Phi_{v})=\frac{1}{1-q_{v}^{-s-1}},

which is shown in
[
Gre03
, p. 306]
. (We have been careful to make sure the normalizations all match up.)

We take a similar path for
(iii)
. Namely, part
(ii)
of Proposition
3.6.1
shows that

R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}})=\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}},

while
[
Gre03
, p. 306]
shows

\Psi(s,W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}},W_{\pi_{v}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{new}},\Phi_{v})=1.

Finally note that
\psi_{v}(x)=\widetilde{\psi}_{v}(c_{v}x)
, and thus
[
Sch02
, Eq. (10)]
implies that

\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi_{v})=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(c_{v})\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v}).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.7.2
.

If
v\mid p
in part
(ii)
, then we could have also written
W_{\pi_{v}}=W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}
.

The remaining local zeta integrals are calculated in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.7.3
.

Let
v\mid p
such that
\pi_{v}
is an unramified principal series and assume that
\alpha_{v}\neq\beta_{v}
. Let
\Phi_{v}
be chosen such that
\widehat{\Phi}_{v}(0)\mathrm{vol}(\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times})=1
. Then,

\Psi_{v}(1,W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}},R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}),\Phi_{v})=\frac{\omega_{\pi_{v}}(c_{v})|c_{v}|^{-1}(\alpha_{v}-\beta_{v})q_{v}^{-1}}{(1-\alpha_{v}\beta_{v}^{-1}q_{v}^{-1})(1-q_{v}^{-1})}.

Proof.

First, the logic behind (
3.7.1
) still shows

(3.7.2)

\Psi_{v}(s,W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}},R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}),\Phi_{v})=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(c_{v})|c_{v}|^{-s}\Psi_{v}(s,W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}},R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}),\Phi_{v}),

where
W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}
is defined in the apparent way from (
3.5.1
). Second, we note an observation from
[
Zha14
, §3.1]
. For
W\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v},\widetilde{\psi}_{v})
and
W^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{v}^{\prime},\widetilde{\psi}_{v}^{\prime})
, define

\Theta(W,W^{\prime})=\int_{F_{v}^{\times}}W\begin{pmatrix}y&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}W^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}y&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}d^{\times}y.

Then, Zhang’s observation is that

\Psi_{v}(1,W,W^{\prime},\Phi_{v})=\widehat{\Phi}_{v}(0)\Theta(W,W^{\prime}).

Based on (
3.7.2
) and the choice we make for
\Phi_{v}
, we can now completely focus on proving:

(3.7.3)

\Theta(W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}},R_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}(W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}))\overset{?}{=}\frac{\mathrm{vol}(\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times})(\alpha_{v}-\beta_{v})q_{v}^{-1}}{(1-\alpha_{v}\beta_{v}^{-1}q_{v}^{-1})(1-q_{v}^{-1})}.

Although this calculation is fairly straightforward, we include a fair amount of detail to aid the reader.

To start, note that for
y\in F_{v}^{\times}
we have

\displaystyle(R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}})\left(\begin{pmatrix}y\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(y)^{-1}W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}y\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}&1\\
-\varpi_{v}&\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(y)^{-1}W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}&y\\
\varpi_{v}\end{pmatrix}\right).

(We used that
\omega_{\pi_{v}}(-1)=1
, as
\pi_{v}
is unramified.) From the definition of the
p
-refinement (
3.5.1
), we have:

\displaystyle W_{\pi_{v},\alpha}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}&y\\
\varpi_{v}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}&y\\
\varpi_{v}\end{pmatrix}\right)-\beta_{v}q_{v}^{-1}W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}&y\\
1\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(\varpi_{v})W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}&y/\varpi_{v}\\
1\end{pmatrix}\right)-\beta_{v}q_{v}^{-1}W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}&y\\
1\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(\varpi_{v})W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}y/\varpi_{v}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)-\beta_{v}q_{v}^{-1}W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}y&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right).

In the final equality, we used that
W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}
is a
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathscr{O}_{v})
-fixed vector and
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}x&\\
&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}&x\\
1&\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}&1\\
1&\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
for
x\in F_{v}^{\times}
. In summary, these elementary manipulations have so far given us:

\Theta(W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}},R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{v}}(W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}))=\underset{I_{1}}{\underbrace{\omega_{\pi_{v}}(\varpi_{v})\int_{F_{v}^{\times}}\omega_{\pi_{v}}(y)^{-1}W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}y\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}y/\varpi_{v}&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\;d^{\times}y}}\\
-\underset{I_{2}}{\underbrace{\beta_{v}q_{v}^{-1}\int_{F_{v}^{\times}}\omega_{\pi_{v}}(y)^{-1}W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}y\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}y&\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\;d^{\times}y}}.

Now, we claim that:

I_{1}=\frac{\mathrm{vol}(\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times})\alpha_{v}q_{v}^{-1}}{(1-\alpha_{v}\beta_{v}^{-1}q_{v}^{-1})(1-q_{v}^{-1})}\;\;\;\;\;I_{2}=\frac{\mathrm{vol}(\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times})\beta_{v}q_{v}^{-1}}{(1-\alpha_{v}\beta_{v}^{-1}q_{v}^{-1})(1-q_{v}^{-1})}.

This being granted, it clearly implies (
3.7.3
) and therefore completes the proof. So, we now indicate how to calculate
I_{2}
and leave
I_{1}
for the reader.

To calculate
I_{2}
, we use the explicit formulas in case
(I)
on page
(I)
, and (
3.5.2
). Those formulas show that the integrand for
I_{2}
is constant on each set
\varpi_{v}^{m}\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}
, with
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. More specifically, the integrand vanishes if
m<0
, and for
m\geq 0
and
y\in\varpi_{v}^{m}\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}
we have

\displaystyle\omega_{\pi_{v}}(y)^{-1}W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}y\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}y\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(\varpi_{v})^{-m}\cdot q_{v}^{-m}\alpha_{v}^{m}\cdot q_{v}^{-m}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\alpha_{v}^{j}\beta_{v}^{m-j}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{q_{v}}{\alpha_{v}\beta_{v}}\right)^{m}q_{v}^{-2m}\alpha^{2m}\sum_{j=0}^{m}\alpha_{v}^{j-m}\beta_{v}^{m-j}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\alpha_{v}}{q_{v}\beta_{v}}\right)^{m}\frac{1-(\beta_{v}/\alpha_{v})^{m+1}}{1-\beta_{v}/\alpha_{v}}.

In the last evaluation of the geometric series, we used that
\alpha_{v}\neq\beta_{v}
.
From this, we now see that:

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\beta_{v}q_{v}^{-1}\sum_{m\geq 0}\int_{\varpi^{m}\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}}\omega_{\pi_{v}}(y)^{-1}W_{\pi_{v},\alpha_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}y\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}y\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\;d^{\times}y

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{vol}(\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times})\beta_{v}q_{v}^{-1}}{1-\beta_{v}/\alpha_{v}}\left(\sum_{m\geq 0}\left(\frac{\alpha_{v}}{q_{v}\beta_{v}}\right)^{m}-\frac{\beta_{v}}{\alpha_{v}}\left(\frac{1}{q_{v}}\right)^{m}\right).

This difference of geometric series is easily evaluated and seen to equal to the claimed value of
I_{2}
.
∎

3.8.
Cohomological interpretation of adjoint
L
-values

We are now able to state and prove the main theorem of Section
3
. We first recap our running assumptions.

Fix a
p
-refined cohomological cuspidal automorphic representation
(\pi,\alpha)
of level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{\pi})
. Let
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
be the prime-to-
p
part of
\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
, and we assume
\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}/\mathfrak{n}_{0}
is a divisor of
\mathbf{p}
. We set
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0}\mathbf{p}
. Let
\lambda=(n,w)
be the weight of
\pi
. (We clearly state in the results below where we assume
\pi
is unitary, i.e.
w=0
.) We will
assume
below that
\alpha_{v}\neq\beta_{v}
at all places
v\mid p
where
\pi_{v}
is an unramified principal series.
We fix rational Whittaker vectors
W_{\pi,\alpha}
as in §
3.5
, and we fix periods
\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)
for each sign
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
, allowing us to define the normalized maps
\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon,\circ}(\pi)
. Now define

\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon,\circ}(\pi)(W_{\pi,\alpha})=\frac{\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi)(W_{\pi,\alpha})}{\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)}\in H^{d}_{!}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}.

We are going to determine
[\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon},\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{-\varepsilon}]_{\lambda}
in terms of an adjoint
L
-value. To do this, introduce the following notations.

•

If
\pi_{v}
is Steinberg, let
\alpha_{v}
be the
U_{v}
-eigenvalue and set

e_{v}(\pi)=e_{v}(\pi,\alpha)=\frac{-\alpha_{v}}{1+q_{v}^{-1}}.

(When
v\mid p
we include
\alpha
in the notation, and if
v\nmid p
, then we do not.)

•

If
\pi_{v}
is supercuspidal, set

e_{v}(\pi)=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(c_{v}^{-1})\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi_{v}).

•

If
v\mid\mathfrak{n}
, but
v\nmid\mathfrak{n}_{\pi}
, then
v\mid p
and
\pi_{v}
is a principal series. Set

e_{v}(\pi,\alpha)=(1-q_{v}^{-1})(1-\beta_{v}\alpha_{v}^{-1}q_{v}^{-1})(\alpha_{v}-\beta_{v})q_{v}^{-1}.

Given these notations, we then define

e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)=\prod_{v\mid p}e_{v}(\pi,\alpha),\;\;\;\;e_{0}(\pi,\alpha)=\prod_{v\mid\mathfrak{n}_{0}}e_{v}(\pi).

Finally, we fix a Bruhat–Schwarz function
\Phi
on
\mathbbm{A}_{F}^{2}
such that

•

\widehat{\Phi}(0)\neq 0
, and

•

\Phi_{v}
is specified at
v\nmid\infty
according to Propositions
3.7.1
and
3.7.3
.

The utility of these choices is the following expression for the product of local zeta integrals at finite places.

Lemma 3.8.1
.

Let
W^{\prime}_{\pi,\alpha}=R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}(W_{\pi,\alpha})
. Then,

\prod_{v\nmid\infty}\frac{\Psi_{v}(1,(W_{\pi,\alpha})_{v},(W_{\pi,\alpha}^{\prime})_{v},\Phi_{v})}{L(1,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime})}=\omega_{\pi}(c)|c|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}^{-1}e_{0}(\pi)e_{p}(\pi,\alpha).

Proof.

We first recall calculations of the local
L
-factors:

•

If
\pi_{v}
is a twist of Steinberg, then
L(1,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime})=(1-q_{v}^{-1})^{-1}
.

•

If
\pi_{v}
is supercuspidal, then
L(1,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime})=1
.

•

If
\pi_{v}
is an unramified principal series (with corresponding
\alpha_{v},\beta_{v}
), then

L(1,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{(1-q_{v}^{-1})^{2}(1-\alpha_{v}\beta_{v}^{-1}q_{v}^{-1})(1-\beta_{v}\alpha_{v}^{-1}q_{v}^{-1})}.

With this knowledge, the formula in the lemma follows from Proposition
3.7.1
and Proposition
3.7.3
.
∎

Continuing on, we define

\Omega_{F}=\frac{-2^{d-1}\mathrm{vol}(F^{\times}\backslash\mathbbm{A}_{F}^{1})\widehat{\Phi}(0)}{h_{K}\mathrm{Res}_{s=1}\zeta_{F}(s)}.

This constant is independent of
\pi
. Recall as well, from §
3.1
, that in order to define the maps
\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi)
and
\mathcal{F}^{-\varepsilon}(\pi^{\prime})
, we fixed
[\pi_{\infty}]^{\varepsilon}
and
[\pi^{\prime}_{\infty}]^{-\varepsilon}
and thus the related Whittaker functions
W_{i,r,\varepsilon,v}
and
W^{\prime}_{j,s,-\varepsilon,v}
at
v\mid\infty
. We now define
\Omega_{\infty}(\pi)\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
according to:

\Omega_{\infty}(\pi)^{-1}=\sum_{i,j,r,s}s(i,j)\langle\ell_{r},\ell_{s}\rangle_{\lambda}\prod_{v\mid\infty}\frac{\Psi_{v}(1,W_{i,r,\varepsilon,\alpha,v},W_{j,s,-\varepsilon,\alpha,v}^{\prime},\Phi_{v})}{L(1,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime})}.

(The notation is as in (
3.2.1
).) This is well-defined, i.e.
\Omega_{\infty}(\pi)
is non-zero, by
[
BR17
, Proposition 3.3.8]
(proven in §5 of
op. cit.
). Here is our main theorem in this section:

Theorem 3.8.2
.

Suppose that
\pi
is unitary (i.e.
w=0
). Let
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
. Then,

(3.8.1)

[\phi^{\varepsilon}_{\pi,\alpha},\phi^{-\varepsilon}_{\pi,\alpha}]_{\lambda}=\frac{\omega_{\pi}(c)e_{0}(\pi)e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})}{|c|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}\Omega_{F}\Omega_{\infty}(\pi)\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(\pi)}.

Proof.

Set
W=W_{\pi,\alpha}\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f},\psi)^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})}
and
W^{\prime}=R_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}(W_{\pi,\alpha})\in\mathcal{W}(\pi_{f}^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})^{K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})}
. Since
\pi
is unitary, we have

\displaystyle[\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon},\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{-\varepsilon}]_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(\pi)}[\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi)(W),\mathcal{F}^{-\varepsilon}(\pi)(W)]_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(\pi)}\langle\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}(\pi)(W),\mathcal{F}^{-\varepsilon}(\pi^{\prime})(W^{\prime})\rangle_{\lambda},

as in (
3.2.4
). In fact, from (
3.2.4
), (
3.3.2
), and (
3.3.4
), we can continue and see:

[\phi^{\varepsilon}_{\pi,\alpha},\phi^{-\varepsilon}_{\pi,\alpha}]_{\lambda}=\left(\prod_{v\nmid\infty}\frac{\Psi_{v}(1,W_{v},W_{v}^{\prime},\Phi_{v})}{L(1,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime})}\right)\times\frac{L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})}{\Omega_{F}\Omega_{\infty}(\pi)\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(\pi)}.

Finally, from Lemma
3.8.1
we have

\prod_{v\nmid\infty}\frac{\Psi_{v}(1,W_{v},W_{v}^{\prime},\Phi_{v})}{L(1,\pi_{v}\times\pi_{v}^{\prime})}=\omega_{\pi}(c)|c|^{-1}_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}e_{0}(\pi)e_{p}(\pi,\alpha).

Therefore, we have shown

[\phi^{\varepsilon}_{\pi,\alpha},\phi^{-\varepsilon}_{\pi,\alpha}]_{\lambda}=\frac{\omega_{\pi}(c)e_{0}(\pi)e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})}{|c|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}\Omega_{F}\Omega_{\infty}(\pi)\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(\pi)},

as promised.
∎

In the prior argument, we used that
\pi
is unitary so that
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
have the same algebraic weight. We end by stating essentially Theorem
3.8.2
in the non-unitary case as well. So, assume that
\pi
has a general weight
\lambda=(n,w)
. Set
\Pi=\pi|\det|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}^{w/2}
, so
\Pi
is unitary of weight
\lambda_{0}=(n,0)
. If
v
is a finite place then the Hecke eigenvalues of
\Pi
are related to those of
\pi
by

(3.8.2)

a_{v}(\Pi)=a_{v}(\pi)q_{v}^{-w/2}.

In particular, the
p
-refinement
(\pi,\alpha)
induces a
p
-refinement
(\Pi,A)
where
A_{v}=\alpha_{v}q_{v}^{-w/2}
. (The
A
stands for the capital
\alpha
.) We have the following relationship on the local factors
e(-)
at
v\mid\mathfrak{n}
.

Lemma 3.8.3
.

Suppose
v\mid\mathfrak{n}
is a finite place.

(1)

If
v\mid p
, then
e_{v}(\pi,\alpha)=q_{v}^{w/2}e_{v}(\Pi,A)
.

(2)

If
v\mid\mathfrak{n}_{0}
, then
e_{v}(\pi)=q_{v}^{m_{v}w/2}e_{v}(\Pi)
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward calculation from (
3.8.2
) whenever
\pi_{v}
is a twist of Steinberg or an unramified principal series. The remaining case is where
v\mid\mathfrak{n}_{0}
and
\pi_{v}
is supercuspidal. For that, we apply
[
Sch02
, Eq. (10)]
. Namely, the conductor of
\psi_{v}
is
-\delta_{v}
by construction and
|c_{v}^{-1}|=q_{v}^{\delta_{v}}
, so

\displaystyle q_{v}^{m_{v}w/2}\omega_{\Pi_{v}}(c_{v}^{-1})\epsilon(1/2,\Pi_{v},\psi_{v})

\displaystyle=q_{v}^{m_{v}w/2}\cdot\omega_{\pi_{v}}(c_{v}^{-1})|c_{v}|^{-w}\cdot q_{v}^{(-2\delta_{v}-m_{v})w/2}\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi_{v})

\displaystyle=\omega_{\pi_{v}}(c_{v}^{-1})\epsilon(1/2,\pi_{v},\psi_{v}).

See
[
Sch02
, Eq. (10)]
for the first equality. This completes the proof.
∎

The next corollary is a version of Theorem
3.8.2
that does not assume
\pi
is unitary. For each sign
\varepsilon
, we choose
[\Pi_{\infty}]^{\varepsilon}
to be equal to
[\pi_{\infty}]^{\varepsilon}
. By examination, they are equal up to scalar already, so this is a matter of choosing reasonable normalizations. Along the same lines, twisting by the adelic norm is a
E
-rational operation
(in the sense that takes
E
-rational vectors to
E
-rational vectors)
on Whittaker models and cohomology. Therefore, we also assume to have chosen
\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)=\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\Pi)
. In terms of the Whittaker models, in fact for each finite place
v
it is clear that
W_{\Pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}=W_{\pi_{v}}^{\mathrm{new}}|{\det}|^{w/2}
, since both are new vectors and normalized to take the value of
1
on the identity matrix. Therefore, based on these choices we certainly have

\mathrm{tw}_{w/2}(\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\pm\varepsilon})=\phi_{\Pi,A}^{\pm\varepsilon},

where
\mathrm{tw}_{w/2}
is the twisting operator

\mathrm{tw}_{w/2}:H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\longrightarrow H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda_{0}})

discussed in Remark
2.8.1
and Remark
2.8.4
. Finally, we
define

\Omega_{\infty}(\pi):=\Omega_{\infty}(\Pi)
.

Corollary 3.8.4
.

Let
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
. Then,

[\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon},\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{-\varepsilon}]_{\lambda}=\frac{\omega_{\pi}(c)e_{0}(\pi)e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})}{|c|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}^{1-w}\Omega_{F}\Omega_{\infty}(\pi)\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(\pi)}.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{tw}_{w/2}(\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\pm\varepsilon})=\phi_{\Pi,A}^{\pm\varepsilon}
, from Remark
2.8.4
we have

[\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon},\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{-\varepsilon}]_{\lambda}=|\det({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}})|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}^{-w/2}[\phi_{\Pi,A}^{\varepsilon},\phi_{\Pi,A}^{-\varepsilon}]_{\lambda_{0}}.

By Theorem
3.8.2
, we have

[\phi_{\Pi,A}^{\varepsilon},\phi_{\Pi,A}^{-\varepsilon}]_{\lambda_{0}}=\frac{\omega_{\Pi}(c)e_{0}(\Pi)e_{p}(\Pi,A)L(1,\Pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})}{|c|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}\Omega_{F}\Omega_{\infty}(\Pi)\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\Pi)\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(\Pi)}.

Now we combine these results and make the following translations:

•

We have
\omega_{\Pi}(c)=\omega_{\pi}(c)|c|^{w}
, and thus
\frac{\omega_{\Pi}(c)}{|c|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}}=\frac{\omega_{\pi}(c)}{|c|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}^{1-w}}
.

•

By Lemma
3.8.3
we have

\displaystyle|\det({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}})|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}^{-w/2}e_{0}(\Pi)e_{p}(\Pi,A)=\left(\prod_{v\mid\mathfrak{n}_{0}}q_{v}^{m_{v}w/2}e_{v}(\Pi)\right)\times\left(\prod_{v\mid p}q_{v}^{w/2}e_{v}(\pi_{v},\alpha_{v})\right)=e_{0}(\pi)e_{p}(\pi,\alpha).

•

We clearly have
L(1,\Pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})=L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})
.

•

Finally, the periods in the denominator of either expression are equal.

The end result of these translations is precisely

[\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{\varepsilon},\phi_{\pi,\alpha}^{-\varepsilon}]_{\lambda}=\frac{\omega_{\pi}(c)e_{0}(\pi)e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})}{|c|_{\mathbbm{A}_{F}}^{1-w}\Omega_{F}\Omega_{\infty}(\pi)\Omega^{\varepsilon}(\pi)\Omega^{-\varepsilon}(\pi)}

as promised.
∎

Remark 3.8.5
.

We did not seriously pursue calculating the constants
\Omega_{\infty}(-)
, but it would be interesting to better understand the benefits and drawbacks of choosing the normalization that
\Omega_{\infty}(\pi)=\Omega_{\infty}(\Pi)
.

4.
Calculating the
p
-adic twisted Poincaré pairing

The goal of this section is to define the twisted Poincaré pairing
[-,-]_{\lambda}
in a
p
-adic setting. The explicit calculation of §
4.2
is compared to the interpolation in §
5
(specifically §
5.5
).

For the
rest of the paper
we fix an algebraic closure
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Let
\Sigma_{p}
denote the set of embeddings
\tau:F\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. We assume that
L\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
is a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
such that
\tau(F)\subseteq L
for all
\tau\in\Sigma_{p}
. We furthermore fix a field isomorphism
\iota:\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. For
v\mid p
, let
\Sigma_{v}
be the embeddings of
F_{v}
into
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. Thus we have an identification
\Sigma_{\infty}\overset{\iota}{\leftrightarrow}\Sigma_{p}=\bigsqcup_{v\mid p}\Sigma_{v}
. We assume as well that
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0}\mathbf{p}
as in §
3
. We write
\varpi_{p}=(\varpi_{v})_{v\mid p}
for the tuple of uniformizers at
p
-adic places. We choose
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
from §
2.8
, such that

(4.0.1)

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-\varpi_{p}&0\end{pmatrix}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p}).

4.1.
The twisted Poincaré pairing, II

Fix an algebraic weight
\lambda=(n,w)
. Define
L[z_{\tau}]^{\leq n_{\tau}}
as in §
2.7
, except with
L
-coefficients. We re-define

\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}=\bigotimes_{\tau\in\Sigma_{p}}L[z_{\tau}]^{\leq n_{\tau}}\otimes{\det}^{\lambda_{2,\tau}}=\bigotimes_{v\mid p}\bigotimes_{\tau\in\Sigma_{v}}L[z_{\tau}]^{\leq n_{\tau}}\otimes{\det}^{\lambda_{2,\tau}}.

This is a right
L
-linear representation of
G(L)\simeq\prod_{v\mid p}\mathrm{GL}_{2}(L)^{\Sigma_{v}}
as before, via

P|_{g}(z)=\det(g)^{\lambda_{2}}(cz+d)^{n}P\left(\frac{az+b}{cz+d}\right)

for
g\in G(L)
and
P\in\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
. We write
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}=\mathrm{Hom}_{L}(\mathscr{L}_{\lambda},L)
for the left
G(L)
-representation
(g\ell)(P)=\ell(P|_{g})
and
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
for the left
G(L)
-representation
gP=P|_{g^{\prime}}
. All three representations have

w_{\lambda}(x)=x^{w}=\prod_{v\mid p}\prod_{\tau\in\Sigma_{v}}x_{\tau}^{w}

as their central character
Z(L)\rightarrow L^{\times}
. Let
w_{\lambda,\det}=w_{\lambda}\circ{\det}
, a character on
G(L)
. We also define the canonical pairing

(4.1.1)

\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\otimes_{L}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}L(w_{\lambda,\det}),

along with the isomorphism

\theta_{\lambda}:\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}

of left
G(L)
-representations, as in §
2.7
.

Each of
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
define a local system on
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
, as we recall. For
k\in K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
write
k_{p}\in G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\subseteq G(L)
for its factor at
p
. For
v\in\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
or
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
, we define a right action of
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
by
v|_{k}=k_{p}^{-1}v
. We endow either with the structure of a trivial
G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
-module. This defines the local systems
\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}
and
\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
. Note that at the level of local systems, defined by the right
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-actions, the pairing (
4.1.1
) becomes a pairing

(4.1.2)

\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}\otimes_{L}\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}\underline{L}(w_{\lambda,\det}^{-1}).

For the sake of completeness, we recall the relationship with the local systems in §
2.8
. Let
E
be as in §
2.7
and assume
L=\mathbb{Q}_{p}(\iota(E))
. Use subscripts to distinguish the
E
-linear from the
L
-linear representations. Thus
\iota
induces an
L
-linear isomorphism
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,E}\otimes_{E,\iota}L\simeq\mathscr{V}_{\lambda,L}
that lifts to an isomorphism

(4.1.3)

\displaystyle\iota:\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,E}\otimes_{E,\iota}L

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,L}

\displaystyle(z,g_{f},\ell)

\displaystyle\overset{\iota}{\mapsto}(z,g_{f},g_{p}^{-1}\iota(\ell))

of local systems on
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
. Then,
\iota
induces a natural isomorphism in cohomology

(4.1.4)

\iota:H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,E})\otimes_{E,\iota}L\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,L}).

Natural means, at least, that Hecke operators commute with
\iota
. The same comments apply to
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
.

We
could
simply transfer the twisted Poincaré pairing from the
E
-linear case to the
L
-linear case, via
\iota
. In practice, we need to re-collect the definitions. From now on, if no subscripts are used, the vector spaces and local systems are their
L
-linear versions. First, we define
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}
in analogy with §
2.4
. Namely, the central character of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
as a right
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
-representation is
w_{\lambda}^{-1}
. Thus, based on §
2.4
and (
4.1.2
), we define
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}
by the commuting diagram

\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{L}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\mathrm{d}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{L}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(w_{\lambda,\det}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}
\textstyle{L.}

Second, we apply the Atkin–Lehner twist in the
p
-adic context. Since
\mathfrak{n}
is divisible by
p
, the pullback
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}
is not isomorphic to
\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}
via the identity map. Nonetheless,
\widetilde{r}_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}((g,\ell))=(g{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}},{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}^{-1}\ell)
lifts
r_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}
to an automorphism

\textstyle{\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{r}_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}
\textstyle{Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})}

of
\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}
. This is the transfer of
r_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}
from the
E
-linear to
L
-linear setting in the sense that
\widetilde{r}_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}\iota=\iota r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}
on cohomology.

Definition 4.1.1
.

The
p
-adic twisted Poincaré pairing

[-,-]_{\lambda}
is the composition

(4.1.5)

\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{L}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\theta_{\lambda}\widetilde{r}_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{L}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}}
\textstyle{L.}

The
E
-linear and
L
-linear pairings agree, in the sense that

(4.1.6)

\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,E})\otimes_{E}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\lambda,E}}
\scriptstyle{\iota\otimes\iota}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,L})\otimes_{L}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda,L})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\lambda,L}}
\textstyle{L}

is a commuting diagram. Therefore, Proposition
2.8.3
holds for the
L
-bilinear pairing
[-,-]_{\lambda}
in Definition
4.1.1
. We record this as the next result, but note that we give a complete proof in Appendix
A
. (Which, incidentally, provides a proof of Proposition
2.8.3
.)

Proposition 4.1.2
.

The pairing
[-,-]_{\lambda}
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

If
g\in\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})
is a diagonal matrix, then
[K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})gK_{0}(\mathfrak{n})]
is self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{\lambda}
. In particular, for each finite place
v
of
F
, the Hecke operators
T_{v}
,
S_{v}
, and
U_{v}
are self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{\lambda}
.

(ii)

If
\varepsilon,\eta\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and
\varepsilon\neq-\eta
, then
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}
is orthogonal to
H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\eta}
under
[-,-]_{\lambda}
.

(iii)

If
\varepsilon=-\eta
, then
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}
is dual to
H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})^{\eta}
under
[-,-]_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Parts
(i)
and
(ii)
are proven in Corollary
A.4.1
within Appendix
A
. The argument for
(iii)
from
(ii)
is the same one explained following Proposition
2.8.3
.
∎

Remark 4.1.3
.

There is another way to calculate
\theta_{\lambda}\widetilde{r}_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}
. The map
\widetilde{r}_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}
is really a two-stage composition

\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}\xrightarrow{(g,\ell)\mapsto(g,{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}^{-1}\ell)}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}\xrightarrow{(g,\ell)\mapsto(g{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}},\ell)}\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}.

The second map lies over
r_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}
, while the first lies over the identity.

Recall,
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
is the
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-representation where
g_{p}
acts via
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}^{-1}g_{p}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}
. The map
\alpha:{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
given by
\alpha(\ell)=\theta_{\lambda}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}\ell)
is
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-equivariant because

\alpha({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}^{-1}g_{p}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}\ell)=\theta_{\lambda}(g_{p}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}\ell)=g_{p}^{\prime}\theta_{\lambda}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}\ell)=g_{p}^{\prime}\alpha(\ell),

for all
g_{p}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
\ell\in\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
. (Note that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}
acting on
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
is the same
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}
acting on
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
. So, there is no ambiguity in the meaning of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}\ell
.)

Combining the prior two paragraphs, the map
\alpha
induces a map in cohomology that satisfies

\theta_{\lambda}\widetilde{r}_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}=\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}

as
L
-linear morphisms
H^{\ast}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\rightarrow H^{\ast}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime})
. Therefore, in Definition
4.1.1
, we may also write
\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}
, rather than
\theta\widetilde{r}_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}
, though that may have made the connection with the Archimedean setting less clear.

4.2.
Explicit calculation

The goal of this section to calculate the operator
\theta_{\lambda}\widetilde{r}_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}
used in Definition
4.1.1
. As noted in Remark
4.1.3
, we have
\theta_{\lambda}\widetilde{r}_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}=\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}
where
\alpha=\theta_{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}
. We therefore focus on calculating
\theta_{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}:{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
. The key formula is the following lemma. Recall,
\{\ell_{r}\}_{0\leq r\leq n}
is the basis in
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
that is dual to the polynomial basis
\{z^{r}\}_{0\leq r\leq n}
in
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
. Recall
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}
acts on
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
via the same formula as on
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
.

Lemma 4.2.1
.

For each
0\leq r\leq n
we have
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}\ell_{r}=\varpi_{p}^{\lambda_{2}}(-1)^{r}\varpi_{p}^{r}\ell_{n-r}
.

Proof.

We evaluate
({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}\ell_{r})(z^{m})
with
0\leq m\leq n
. By the definition, we have:

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}\ell_{r})(z^{m})

\displaystyle=\ell_{r}(z^{m}|_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}})

\displaystyle=\ell_{r}(\varpi_{p}^{\lambda_{2}}(-\varpi_{p}z)^{n}(-1/\varpi_{p}z)^{m})

\displaystyle=\ell_{r}(\varpi_{p}^{\lambda_{2}}(-1)^{n-m}\varpi_{p}^{n-m}z^{n-m}).

The result follows, since
\ell_{r}(z^{n-m})=0
unless
n-m=r
, and
\ell_{n-m}(z^{n-m})=1
.
∎

Proposition 4.2.2
.

The map
\alpha=\theta_{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}:{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
is given by

\alpha(\ell)=(-1)^{n}\varpi_{p}^{\lambda_{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{n}\ell(z^{r})\varpi_{p}^{r}\binom{n}{r}z^{r}.

Proof.

Let
\ell\in{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
. Write
\ell=\sum_{r}\ell(z^{r})\ell_{r}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\theta_{\lambda}({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}\ell)

\displaystyle=\theta_{\lambda}\left(\varpi_{p}^{\lambda_{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{n}\ell(z^{r})(-1)^{r}\varpi_{p}^{r}\ell_{n-r}\right)

(by Lemma
4.2.1
)

\displaystyle=\varpi_{p}^{\lambda_{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{n}\ell(z^{r})(-1)^{r}\varpi_{p}^{r}(-1)^{n-r}\binom{n}{n-r}z^{r}

(by definition of
\theta_{\lambda}
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\varpi_{p}^{\lambda_{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{n}\ell(z^{r})\varpi_{p}^{r}\binom{n}{r}z^{r}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2.3
.

For comparison with §
5.5
, we re-write the formula in Proposition
4.2.2
. Introduce a second variable
x
alongside
z
. Consider the two-variable polynomial expansion

(1+\varpi_{p}xz)^{n}=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\binom{n}{r}\varpi_{p}^{r}x^{r}z^{r}.

For
\ell\in\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
, we may consider
\ell
operating only in the
z
-variable. Then, we see that

\ell((1+\varpi_{p}xz)^{n})=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\ell(z^{r})\binom{n}{r}\varpi_{p}^{r}x^{r}.

If we perceive elements of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
as functions of
x
, then Proposition
4.2.2
says that
\alpha
is given by

\alpha(\ell)(x)=(-1)^{n}\varpi_{p}^{\lambda_{2}}\ell\left(z\mapsto(1+\varpi_{p}xz)^{n}\right).

5.
The
p
-adic analytic twisted Poincaré pairing

There are two goals in this section. First, we recall algebras of locally analytic functions and distributions equipped with monoid actions. Second, we generalize the twisted Poincaré pairing from the algebraic situation studied in §
4
to affinoid
p
-adic weights.

The notations of §
4
are enforced, and we introduce a few more. If
\Gamma
is a compact and abelian
p
-adic Lie group, we write
\mathscr{X}(\Gamma)
for the rigid analytic space, over
L
, parametrizing continuous characters on
\Gamma
. See
[
BH24
, §5.1]
, for instance. The dimension of
\mathscr{X}(\Gamma)
is the dimension of
\Gamma
as a
p
-adic Lie group. If
\chi\in\mathscr{X}(\Gamma)(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, we implicitly understand that
\chi
is defined over its residue field
L_{\chi}
, which is a finite extension of
L
. We also deal with some matrix groups and monoids. The Iwahori subgroup is

I=\left\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\mid c\in\varpi_{p}\mathscr{O}_{p}\right\}.

We will also consider two submonoids of
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. They are

(5.0.1)

\Delta^{+}=\left\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cap M_{2}(\mathscr{O}_{p})\mid c\in\varpi_{p}\mathscr{O}_{p}\text{ and }d\in\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}\right\},

(5.0.2)

\Delta^{-}=\left\{g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\cap M_{2}(\mathscr{O}_{p})\mid c\in\varpi_{p}\mathscr{O}_{p}\text{ and }a\in\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}\right\}.

The Iwahori group is the local factor of
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
above
p
, while
\Delta^{+}
is the local factor of
\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})
. The monoid
\Delta^{-}
also has a global analogue, which is called
\Delta^{-}_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
in §
5.4
.

5.1.
Locally analytic functions and distributions

In this section, we fix an
L
-affinoid algebra
A
equipped with Banach norm
|-|_{A}
. We briefly recall Tate algebras. Suppose
n=(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d})
is a
d
-tuple of integers
n_{j}\geq 0
. Write
|n|=\sum n_{i}
. Given variables
y_{1},\dotsc,y_{d}
, we define
y^{n}=y_{1}^{n_{1}}\dotsb y_{d}^{n_{d}}
. The
d
-variable Tate algebra over
A
is

A\langle y_{1},\dotsc,y_{d}\rangle=\left\{\sum_{n}c_{n}y^{n}\in A[[y_{1},\dotsc,y_{n}]]\mid\lim_{|n|\rightarrow\infty}|c_{n}|_{A}=0\right\}.

It is a Banach
A
-algebra for the supremum norm
|\sum c_{n}y^{n}|_{\sup}=\max_{n}|c_{n}|_{A}
.

A chart for
\mathscr{O}_{p}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear isomorphism
\nu:\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathscr{O}_{p}
. Let
s=(s_{v})_{v\mid p}
be a
\Sigma_{p}
-tuple of integers
s_{v}\geq 0
. Write
|s|=\sum_{v\mid p}s_{v}
. A function
f:\mathscr{O}_{p}\rightarrow A
is called
s
-analytic if, for all
x_{0}\in\mathscr{O}_{p}
, the function

y\mapsto f(x_{0}+\varpi_{p}^{s}\nu(y)):\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\longrightarrow A

extends to an element of
A\langle y_{1},\dotsc,y_{d}\rangle
. The
s
-analytic functions form an
A
-algebra
\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
. Given
f\in\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
one sets
|f|_{s}=\max_{x_{0}}|f(x_{0}+\varpi_{p}^{s}\nu(y))|_{\sup}
, with the maximum depending only on finite set of a coset representatives for
\mathscr{O}_{p}/\varpi_{p}^{s}\mathscr{O}_{p}
. In this way,
\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
is a Banach
A
-algebra with norm
|-|_{s}
. If
s^{\prime}>s
, meaning
s_{v}^{\prime}>s_{v}
for all
v\mid p
, then the inclusion
\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{s^{\prime}}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
is compact with dense image. Each definition we have made depends on
\nu
, but the compact type space

\mathscr{A}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)=\mathop{\varinjlim}\limits_{|s|\rightarrow\infty}\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)

is independent of
\nu
. It is the
A
-algebra of
locally analytic functions
on
\mathscr{O}_{p}
.

We define
\mathscr{D}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
as the Banach
A
-module dual to
\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
. The topology is induced by the operator norm, which we also denote by
|-|_{s}
. We will only need to use the following inequality:
for
\mu\in\mathscr{D}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
and
f\in\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
, we have

(5.1.1)

|\mu(f)|_{A}\leq|\mu|_{s}|f|_{s}.

The natural restriction maps
\mathscr{D}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{s^{\prime}}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
are compact inclusions, dual to the dense and compact inclusions for functions. The projective limit

\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)=\mathop{\varprojlim}\limits_{|s|\rightarrow\infty}\mathscr{D}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)

is known as the
A
-valued
locally analytic distributions
on
\mathscr{O}_{p}
. It is an
A
-algebra for the convolution product, but we will not use this fact.

We recalled some details of the construction of
\mathscr{A}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
and
\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
in order to justify the following proposition, which we were unable to find a reference for. It will be used in the proof of Proposition
5.3.1
.

Proposition 5.1.1
.

Let
g\in\mathscr{A}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
.

(i)

For each
x\in\mathscr{O}_{p}
, the function
z\mapsto g(xz)
belongs to
\mathscr{A}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
.

(ii)

If
\mu\in\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
, then
\alpha_{\mu}(x)=\mu(z\mapsto g(xz))
belongs to
\mathscr{A}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
.

Proof.

Multiplication by
x
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear function on
\mathscr{O}_{p}
. Since the composition of a linear function with a locally analytic one is also locally analytic, part
(i)
is clear.

We will only sketch part
(ii)
. Namely, we will show that
\alpha_{\mu}
is rigid analytic on a ball
\varpi_{p}^{s}\mathscr{O}_{p}
where
s
depends on
g
but not
\mu
. The general case is left for the reader. The partial proof is, actually, enough for our application of Proposition
5.1.1
. See Remark
5.3.2
.

Fix a chart
\nu
for
\mathscr{O}_{p}
. Suppose
g\in\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
with respect to
\nu
. For
x,z\in\mathscr{O}_{p}
, we write
\nu(y)=z
and
\nu(w)=x
with
y,w\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}
. Since
(x,z)\mapsto xz
is
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-bilinear, we see that

\nu^{-1}(xz)=A_{y}w

where
A_{y}\in M_{d}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
y\mapsto A_{y}
is
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear. Since
g\in\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
, there exists a sequence
(c_{n})_{n}
in
A
such that
|c_{n}|_{A}\rightarrow 0
as
|n|\rightarrow\infty
and

(5.1.2)

g(\varpi_{p}^{s}xz)=\sum_{n}c_{n}(A_{y}w)^{n}.

Recall that, if
A_{y}w=(u_{1},\dotsc,u_{n})
, then
(A_{y}w)^{n}=u_{1}^{n_{1}}\dotsb u_{d}^{n_{d}}
. Writing the
u_{i}
in terms of entries of
A_{y}
(which are linear functions of
y
) and the components of
w
, we find that there exists homogeneous polynomials
b_{m}(y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[y_{1},\dotsc,y_{d}]
, depending on
n
and of degree
|m|=|n|
, such that

(A_{y}w)^{n}=\sum_{|m|=|n|}b_{m}(y)w^{m}.

Now calculate the value of
\mu
on (
5.1.2
), acting as a distribution with respect to the
z
-variable. Write
\mu_{\nu}
for the pullback of
\mu
along
\nu
, which acts as a distribution with respect to the
y
-variable. For each coordinate function
y_{i}\in\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d},A)
, we have
|y_{i}^{n}|_{s}\leq 1
for all
n
. Since each
b_{m}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-polynomial in the
y
-variables, we find

(5.1.3)

|\mu_{\nu}(b_{m}(y))|_{A}\leq|\mu_{\nu}|_{s}|b_{m}(y)|_{s}\leq|\mu_{\nu}|_{s}

by (
5.1.1
). Applying
\mu
to (
5.1.2
), we find

\alpha_{\mu}(\varpi^{s}x)=\mu(z\mapsto g(\varpi_{p}^{s}xz))=\sum_{m}d_{m}w^{m}

where
d_{m}\in A
is a sum of terms of the form
\mu_{\nu}(b_{m}(y))c_{n}
with
|n|=|m|
. By the ultrametric inequality and (
5.1.3
), we find that

|d_{m}|_{A}\leq|\mu_{\nu}|_{s}\max_{|n|=|m|}|c_{n}|_{A}.

Since
|\mu_{\nu}|_{s}
is a constant and
|c_{n}|_{A}\rightarrow 0
as
|n|\rightarrow\infty
, we deduce that
|d_{m}|_{A}\rightarrow 0
as
|m|\rightarrow\infty
. Therefore,
\sum_{m}d_{m}w^{m}
extends to an element of
A\langle w_{1},\dotsc,w_{d}\rangle
, making
\alpha_{\mu}
rigid analytic on
\varpi_{p}^{s}\mathscr{O}_{p}
. By examination, the value of
s
depends only on the radius of convergence for
g
. This completes the partial proof of part
(ii)
.∎

5.2.
p
-adic weights

The goal of this section is to introduce
p
-adic weights for
F
. Write

j:\mathscr{O}_{F}^{\times}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}\simeq\prod_{v\mid p}\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}

for the diagonal embedding of the global units into the local units.
We embed
\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}
into the center of
T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
via the diagonal map
x\mapsto\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
0&x\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
.
We denote by
H_{p}
the
p
-adic closure of
j(\mathscr{O}_{F,+}^{\times})
inside
\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}\subseteq T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. The quotient groups
\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}/H_{p}\subseteq T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})/H_{p}
are compact and abelian
p
-adic Lie groups.

Definition 5.2.1
.

The
p
-adic weight space
is
\mathscr{W}=\mathscr{X}\left(T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})/H_{p}\right)
.

By definition, if
A
is an
L
-affinoid algebra, then each
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}(A)
is a continuous character
T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\rightarrow A^{\times}
that is trivial on the image of
\mathscr{O}_{F,+}^{\times}
embedded via
j
. The rigid analytic space
\mathscr{W}
is a disjoint union of polydiscs of dimension
\dim(\mathscr{W})=d+1+\delta_{F,p}
where
\delta_{F,p}
is Leopoldt’s defect. Algebraic weights are
p
-adic weights as follows. For
\lambda=((\lambda_{1,\tau},\lambda_{2,\tau}))_{\tau\in\Sigma_{p}}
we consider
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}(L)
by

\lambda\left(\begin{pmatrix}x&0\\
0&y\end{pmatrix}\right)=x^{\lambda_{1}}y^{\lambda_{2}}=\prod_{v\mid p}\prod_{\tau\in\Sigma_{v}}\tau(x_{v})^{\lambda_{1,\tau}}\tau(y_{v})^{\lambda_{2,\tau}}.

The weights arising this way are called
classical weights
. They are denoted by
\mathscr{W}_{\mathrm{cl}}\subseteq\mathscr{W}(L)
. Based on this, we introduce the notation
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
for
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}
. Namely,
\lambda_{1}
is the restriction to
\{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
