0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\}\subseteq T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
\lambda_{2}
is the restriction to
\{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&y\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\}\subseteq T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. The diagonal character is denoted by
w_{\lambda}=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}
. Unlike the classical case,
w_{\lambda}
is only a character of
\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}
, rather than
F_{p}^{\times}
. So, we get a matrix group character
w_{\lambda,\det}={\det}\circ w_{\lambda}
on
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
, rather than
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

The Zariski closure, in
\mathscr{W}
, of the classical weights has dimension
d+1
. So, unless Leopoldt’s conjecture is established for
F
, the set
\mathscr{W}_{\mathrm{cl}}
is not Zariski dense in
\mathscr{W}
. However, the classical weights and their twists are dense, as we now recall. Let
{\Gamma}_{p}=\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}/H_{p}
. For
\theta\in\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{p})
and
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mathscr{W}
, we define

\theta\lambda:=(\theta\lambda_{1},\theta\lambda_{2}).

This defines a rigid analytic group action of
\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{p})
on
\mathscr{W}
.

Definition 5.2.2
.

A weight
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
is
twist classical
if
\lambda=\theta\lambda_{\mathrm{cl}}
for some
\theta\in\mathscr{X}({\Gamma}_{p})(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{cl}}\in\mathscr{W}_{\mathrm{cl}}
.

The next proposition summarizes features of
\mathscr{W}
and twist classical points that will be used in the coming sections. The arguments are well-studied, but we include details if there is no direct reference. In part
(ii)
, recall that if
X
is a rigid analytic space, then a subset
Z\subseteq X(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
is called
accumulating
if every
z\in Z
admits a neighborhood basis of affinoid opens
U
for which
U\cap Z
is Zariski dense in
U
.

Proposition 5.2.3
.

(i)

Suppose
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}
is twist classical. Write
\lambda=\theta\lambda_{\mathrm{cl}}
where
\theta\in\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{p})(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{cl}}\in\mathscr{W}_{\mathrm{cl}}
. Write
\lambda_{\mathrm{cl}}=(n,w)
. Then,
\lambda\mapsto n
is well-defined.

(ii)

The set of twist classical weights is Zariski dense and accumulating in
\mathscr{W}
.

Proof.

Part
(i)
is straightforward from direct examination. (See
[
BH24
, Lemma 6.1.3]
for instance.)

We sketch the argument for
(ii)
, which is claimed without proof in
[
BH24
, Lemma 6.1.4]
. Start by writing
\Gamma=T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})/H_{p}
. Let
\Gamma_{\mathrm{tors}}\subseteq\Gamma
be the torsion subgroup. If
\chi
is a character on
\Gamma_{\mathrm{tors}}
, we write
\mathscr{W}_{\chi}
for those
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}
such that
\lambda|_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{tors}}}=\chi
. Let
B
be the open rigid analytic
L
-ball of radius one centered at
1
. Then,
\mathscr{W}_{\chi}\simeq B^{1+d+\delta_{F,p}}
, and
\mathscr{W}
is the disjoint union of the
\mathscr{W}_{\chi}
. It suffices to show the following.

(a)

Each
\mathscr{W}_{\chi}
contains a classical weight.

(b)

The Zariski closure of the twist classical weights has maximal dimension and twist classical weights accumulate at a classical weight.

To prove
(a)
, start by noting that the inclusion
\Gamma_{p}\subseteq\Gamma
is split by
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
0&y\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\mapsto y
. The complementary group is
\{\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\mid x\in\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}\}\simeq\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}
. Therefore, we get a rigid analytic splitting

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathscr{X}(\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times})\times\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{p})

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}).

The action of
\theta\in\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{p})
under this splitting is
(\theta\lambda_{1},\theta^{2}\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})
. Therefore, up to twist we may assume that
\chi|_{(\Gamma_{p})_{\mathrm{tors}}}
is either trivial or the norm character
x:\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow L^{\times}
. In the first case,
\chi
extends to an algebraic weight
\lambda=(n,w)=(2m,0)
for some integer
m
, and in the second case it extends to
\lambda=(2m+1,1)
.

The argument for
(b)
is well-studied when
\delta_{F,p}=0
. See
[
Bir19
, Proposition 5.1.4]
, for instance. Moreover, if
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathscr{W}_{\mathrm{cl}}
then
\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{p})\lambda_{1}\cap\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{p})\lambda_{2}
has at most a one-dimensional Zariski closure, by part
(i)
. Therefore, the Zariski closure of the twist classical weights has dimension at least
1+d+\dim\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{p})-1=1+d+\delta_{F,p}
. Since
\dim(\mathscr{W})=1+d+\delta_{F,p}
, we see the twist classical weights are Zariski-dense. It is clear the twist classical weights accumulate upon themselves.
∎

5.3.
Monoid actions

In this section, locally analytic functions and distributions are equipped with weighted actions of monoids. Throughout, we fix an affinoid
L
-algebra
A
and a
p
-adic weight
\lambda:T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\rightarrow A^{\times}
. To shorten notations, we write
\kappa=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{-1}
. Recall the monoids
\Delta^{\pm}
defined in (
5.0.1
) and (
5.0.2
). We will repeatedly use that continuous characters on
\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}
are locally analytic on
\mathscr{O}_{p}
(once extended by zero). See
[
Urb11
, Lemma 3.4.6]
. So, if
d\in\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}
and
c\in\varpi_{p}\mathscr{O}_{p}
, then
x\mapsto\kappa(cx+d)
is locally analytic on
\mathscr{O}_{p}
.

For
f\in\mathscr{A}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
and
g\in\Delta^{+}
, we define

(5.3.1)

f|_{g}(x)=\lambda_{2}(\det(g)\varpi_{p}^{-v(\det g)})\kappa(cx+d)f\left(\frac{ax+b}{cx+d}\right).

In (
5.3.1
), we explicitly mean

\varpi_{p}^{-v(\det g)}=\prod_{v\mid p}\varpi_{v}^{-v(\det g_{v})},

where
v(-)
is the valuation on
\mathscr{O}_{v}
normalized such that
v(\varpi_{v})=1
. In particular, if
g\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, then
\det(g)\varpi_{p}^{-v(\det g)}\in\mathscr{O}_{p}^{\times}
and so
\lambda_{2}(\det(g)\varpi_{p}^{-v(\det g)})
is well-defined. We write
\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}
for
\mathscr{A}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
equipped with the right
A
-linear
\Delta^{+}
-action given in (
5.3.1
). Dualizing, we get a left
A
-linear action of
\Delta^{+}
on
\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
. Namely, for
\mu\in\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
and
g\in\Delta^{+}
we set

(g\mu)(f)=\mu(f|_{g}).

We write
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
for the
A
-algebra
\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
equipped with this action.

There are two further actions to consider, based on two relationships between
\Delta^{+}
and
\Delta^{-}
. First, the anti-automorphism
g\mapsto g^{\prime}=\det(g)g^{-1}
on
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
induces an anti-isomorphism
\Delta^{+}\xrightarrow{g\mapsto g^{\prime}}\Delta^{-}
. We write
\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
for
\mathscr{A}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
equipped with the left
\Delta^{-}
-action

hf=f|_{h^{\prime}},

for
h\in\Delta^{-}
and
f\in\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}
.

Second, let
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
-\varpi_{p}&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
as in (
4.0.1
). For
g=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
we have

(5.3.2)

g_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}}:={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}g{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}d&-c/\varpi_{p}\\
-b\varpi_{p}&a\end{pmatrix}.

Thus
g\mapsto g_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}}
defines an isomorphism
\Delta^{+}\xrightarrow{g\mapsto g_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}}}\Delta^{-}
. The inverse map
h\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}^{-1}h{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}
is given by the same formula as (
5.3.2
). If
h\in\Delta^{-}
and
\mu\in\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
, we then define

h\cdot_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}\mu=({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}^{-1}h{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p})\mu.

We write
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
for
\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
equipped with this left
\Delta^{-}
-action. (We write
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}
rather than
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}^{\ast}
in order to align with the next section and §
2.4
.)

If
g\in I=\Delta^{+}\cap\Delta^{-}
and
\mu\in\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
and
f\in\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
, then

(g\mu)(gf)=\mu(f|_{g^{\prime}g})=\mu(f|_{\det g})=w_{\lambda}(\det g)\mu(f).

Therefore, as
right

I
-representations we have a canonical pairing

(5.3.3)

\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}\otimes_{A}\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}A(w_{\lambda,\det}^{-1}).

This connection is complemented by a connection between
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
with
\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}
as left
\Delta^{-}
-modules.

Proposition 5.3.1
.

(i)

If
\mu\in\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
, then
\alpha_{\mu}(x)=\mu(z\mapsto\kappa(-1)\kappa(1+\varpi_{p}xz))
belongs to
\mathscr{A}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
.

(ii)

The assignment
\alpha(\mu)=\alpha_{\mu}
defines an
A
-linear left
\Delta^{-}
-module morphism
\alpha:{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Part
(i)
holds by applying Proposition
5.1.1
(ii)
to
g(z)=\kappa(-1)\kappa(1+\varpi_{p}z)\in\mathscr{A}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
. Part
(ii)
is a direct calculation. We want to show that if
h\in\Delta^{-}
and
\mu\in\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
, then

(5.3.4)

\alpha_{h\cdot_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\mu}=(\alpha_{\mu})|_{h^{\prime}}.

Let
h=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
. Note that
h^{\prime}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}d&-b\\
-c&a\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\det(h)=\det(h^{\prime})
. Therefore, if
x\in\mathscr{O}_{p}
, then

(5.3.5)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mu}|_{h^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{2}(\det(h)\varpi_{p}^{-v(\det h)})\kappa(a-cx)\alpha_{\mu}\left(\frac{dx-b}{a-cx}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{2}(\det(h)\varpi_{p}^{-v(\det h)})\kappa(a-cx)\mu\left(z\mapsto\kappa(-1)\kappa\left(1+\varpi_{p}\frac{dx-b}{a-cx}z\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{2}(\det(h)\varpi_{p}^{-v(\det h)})\kappa(-1)\mu\left(z\mapsto\kappa(a-cx+\varpi_{p}(dx-b)z)\right).

The third equality is justified because
\mu
is
A
-linear and
\kappa
is a character.

Similarly, we note that
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}^{-1}h{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}d&-c/\varpi_{p}\\
-b\varpi_{p}&a\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\det({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}^{-1}h{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p})=\det(h)
. Therefore,

(5.3.6)

\displaystyle\alpha_{h\cdot_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=(h\cdot_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\mu)\left(z\mapsto\kappa(-1)\kappa(1+\varpi_{p}xz)\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\left[z\mapsto\kappa(-1)\kappa(1+\varpi_{p}xz)\right]|_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}^{-1}h{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu\left(z\mapsto\lambda_{2}(\det(h)\varpi_{p}^{-v(\det h)})\kappa(-b\varpi_{p}z+a)\kappa(-1)\kappa\left(1+\varpi_{p}x\frac{dz-c/\varpi_{p}}{a-b\varpi_{p}z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{2}(\det(h)\varpi_{p}^{-v(\det h)})\kappa(-1)\mu\left(z\mapsto\kappa(a-b\varpi_{p}z+\varpi_{p}x(dz-c/\varpi_{p})\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{2}(\det(h)\varpi_{p}^{-v(\det h)})\kappa(-1)\mu(z\mapsto(a-cx+\varpi_{p}(dx-b)z).

The third equality is justified because
\mu
is
A
-linear and the fourth equality because
\kappa
is a character. The final formulas of (
5.3.6
) and (
5.3.5
) agree. Therefore, (
5.3.4
) holds and part
(ii)
is proven.
∎

Remark 5.3.2
.

Recall that Proposition
5.1.1
cited in the proof of part
(i)
was not fully proven, but the proof we gave is
enough
for our application in Proposition
5.3.1
. Indeed, let
x_{0}\in\mathscr{O}_{p}
, let
\mu_{0}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&x_{0}\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\cdot_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\mu
. Then, we have

\alpha_{\mu}(x-x_{0})=\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&x_{0}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\alpha_{\mu}\right)(x)=\alpha_{\mu_{0}}(x).

The proof of Proposition
5.1.1
shows that
\alpha_{\mu_{0}}
is rigid analytic on
\varpi_{p}^{s}\mathscr{O}_{p}
, where
s
depends only on
\kappa
, which means
\alpha_{\mu}
is also rigid analytic on
x_{0}+\varpi_{p}^{s}\mathscr{O}_{p}
.

5.4.
The analytic twisted Poincaré pairing

Finally, we introduce and analyze an analytic twisted Poincaré pairing on distribution-valued cohomology. Fix an affinoid
L
-algebra
A
and
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}(A)
. Recall,
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{0}\mathbf{p}
with
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
co-prime to
\mathbf{p}
. Write
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
for the choice of an Atkin–Lehner element as in §
4.1
.

We have global analogues of
\Delta^{\pm}
. Namely, we already defined
\Delta^{+}_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
in §
2.6
, and now we also define

\Delta_{0}^{-}(\mathfrak{n})=\{g=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in M_{2}(\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F})\cap G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})\mid c\in\mathfrak{n}\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F}\text{ and }a_{v}\in\mathscr{O}_{v}^{\times}\text{ if $v\mid\mathfrak{n}$}\}.

The relationships between
\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})
and
\Delta_{0}^{-}(\mathfrak{n})
are analogous to those between
\Delta^{+}
and
\Delta^{-}
. Namely,
g\mapsto g^{\prime}=\det(g)g^{-1}
is an anti-isomorphism between them, and
g\mapsto g_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}={\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}g{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{-1}
is an isomorphism of
\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})
onto
\Delta_{0}^{-}(\mathfrak{n})
. Since
\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})\cap\Delta_{0}^{-}(\mathfrak{n})=K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
, left
\Delta_{0}^{\pm}(\mathfrak{n})
-modules define local systems on
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
as in §
2.5
, by taking the induced right
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
-actions and letting
G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
act trivially.

We consider
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
,
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
, and
\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
as left
\Delta_{0}^{\pm}(\mathfrak{n})
-modules via the weight
\lambda
-action of the local factors at
p
. The central character of
I
acting on
\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
is, therefore,
w_{\lambda}^{-1}
. By §
2.4
, we have a natural pullback

\mathrm{d}^{\ast}:H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\lambda}^{\prime})\longrightarrow H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(w_{\lambda,\det})).

By (
5.3.3
), we once again define the canonical pairing
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}
by

(5.4.1)

\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{A}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\lambda}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\mathrm{d}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{A}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\lambda}^{\prime}(w_{\lambda,\det}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}
\textstyle{A.}

The map
\alpha:{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
is
\Delta_{0}^{-}(\mathfrak{n})
-equivariant. We also have the natural pullback map on cohomology

(5.4.2)

H^{\ast}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\xrightarrow{r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}}H^{\ast}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda}).

Thus
\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}
is an
A
-linear map
H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\rightarrow H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\lambda}^{\prime})
.

Definition 5.4.1
.

The
analytic twisted Poincaré pairing

[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
is given by the following diagram

\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{A}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{A}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{A}}_{\lambda}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}}
\textstyle{A.}

The relationship between the analytic twisted Poincaré pairing and its classical cousin is given in §
5.5
. Before that, we record the Hecke-theoretic properties of
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
.

Proposition 5.4.2
.

The pairing
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

If
g\in\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})
is a diagonal matrix, then
[K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})gK_{0}(\mathfrak{n})]
is self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
. In particular, for each finite place
v
of
F
, the Hecke operators
T_{v}
,
S_{v}
, and
U_{v}
are self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{\lambda}
.

(ii)

If
\varepsilon,\eta\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and
\varepsilon\neq-\eta
, then
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})^{\varepsilon}
is orthogonal to
H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})^{\eta}
under
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
.

(iii)

Assume
A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
is a morphism of affinoid
L
-algebras and write
\lambda^{\prime}\in\mathscr{W}(A^{\prime})
for the corresponding
A^{\prime}
-valued weight. Then, we have a natural commuting diagram

\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\otimes_{A}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda^{\prime}})\otimes_{A^{\prime}}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda^{\prime}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{an}}}
\textstyle{A^{\prime}.}

Proof.

The proof of parts
(i)
and
(ii)
are as formal as their corresponding statements in Proposition
4.1.2
. The proof is explained in Appendix
A
, with the end result being Corollary
A.4.1
. Finally,
(iii)
is clear from the constructions. Indeed, the canonical pairings, the morphism
\alpha
, the geometric pullback maps, and Poincaré duality are all clearly functorial in the weight
\lambda
.
∎

Remark 5.4.3
.

The Hecke-theoretic parts
(i)
and
(ii)
of Proposition
5.4.2
are analogues of the same statements in the classical setting Proposition
4.1.2
. The duality statement in Proposition
4.1.2
is false for
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
. For instance if
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}_{\mathrm{cl}}
then the theory in the next section shows that the pairing
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
factors through the classical pairing. So, any cohomology class that vanishes under classical specialization ruins the chance to have a perfect pairing.

5.5.
Specialization

In this section, we fix
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}_{\mathrm{cl}}
. Our goal is to clarify the relationship between
[-,-]_{\lambda}
in Definition
4.1.1
and
[-,-]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}
in Definition
5.4.1
(both considered as
L
-valued pairings).

Viewing
\lambda
as a
p
-adic weight, the action (
2.7.1
) of
\Delta^{+}\subseteq G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
on
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
is

P|_{g}(x)=\lambda_{2}(\det g)\kappa(cx+d)f\left(\frac{ax+b}{cx+d}\right),

for
P\in\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
and
g\in\Delta^{+}
. Comparing with (
5.3.1
), the inclusion
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}
is
I
-equivariant but not
\Delta^{+}
-equivariant. Therefore, we introduce a twist. Define
\chi_{\lambda}:F_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow L^{\times}
by

(5.5.1)

\chi_{\lambda}(x)=\varpi_{p}^{-v(x)\lambda_{2}}=\prod_{v\mid p}\prod_{\tau\in\Sigma_{v}}\tau(\varpi_{v})^{-v(x_{v})\lambda_{2,\tau}}.

Adopting a notation similar to the one introduced in §
4.1
, we denote by
\chi_{\lambda,\det}:G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\rightarrow L^{\times}
the character

\chi_{\lambda,\det}=\chi_{\lambda}\circ\det
.
Write
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\chi_{\lambda,\det})
for the twist of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
by
\chi_{\lambda,\det}
. Then, the natural inclusion

(5.5.2)

j_{\lambda}:\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\chi_{\lambda,\det})\mbox{$\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow$}\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}

is equivariant for the right
\Delta^{+}
-actions. On the dual side, the surjection
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
is
\Delta^{+}
-equivariant if we replace the target with
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(\chi_{\lambda,\det})
, defined as the left
G(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
-representation gotten by twisting by
\chi_{\lambda,\det}
. (Note,
\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(\chi_{\lambda,\det})
is also the left representation dual to
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\chi_{\lambda,\det})
.) The corresponding surjection

(5.5.3)

\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}:\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}\mbox{$\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow$}\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}(\chi_{\lambda,\det})

is called the
specialization map
. To explicitly describe it, for
\mu\in\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
and
P\in\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\chi_{\lambda,\det})
, we have

(5.5.4)

\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\mu)(P)=\mu(j_{\lambda}(P)).

Note that
\chi_{\lambda,\det}
is trivial on
G(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. So, the identity map induces an isomorphism

H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}(\chi_{\lambda,\det}))\simeq H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})

of
L
-vector spaces that is Hecke equivariant, except the
U_{v}
-action is twisted if
v\mid p
. Therefore, we view
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}
on cohomology as an
L
-linear map

\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}:H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\longrightarrow H^{d}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}).

Proposition 5.5.1
.

If
\Phi\in H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})
and
\Psi\in H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})
, then

[\Phi,\Psi]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}=\varpi_{p}^{-\lambda_{2}}[\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\Phi),\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\Psi)]_{\lambda}.

Proof.

Let
\Phi\in H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})
and
\psi^{\prime}\in H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}^{\prime})
. Then, we have
\langle\Phi,j_{\lambda}(\psi^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}=\langle\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\Phi),\psi^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}
by the definitions. Since
j_{\lambda}
commutes with the geometric pullback
\mathrm{d}^{\ast}
, we conclude that

(5.5.5)

[\Phi,j_{\lambda}(\psi^{\prime})]_{\mathrm{can}}=[\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\Phi),\psi^{\prime}]_{\mathrm{can}}.

Now write
\lambda=(n,w)
. Then,
\kappa=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{-1}
is the
n
-th power map. The map
\alpha:{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}^{\prime}
is given by

\displaystyle\alpha(\mu)(x)=\kappa(-1)\mu(\kappa(1+\varpi_{p}xz))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\mu((1+\varpi_{p}xz)^{n})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\mu)\left(j_{\lambda}(1+\varpi_{p}xz)^{n})\right).

By Proposition
4.2.2
and Remark
4.2.3
, we find a commuting diagram

\textstyle{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}^{\ast}\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varpi_{p}^{-\lambda_{2}}\theta_{\lambda}{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}_{p}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\lambda}}

of left
I
-representations. Like
\mathrm{d}^{\ast}
, the pullback
r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}
is geometric. Therefore, we find a commuting diagram

(5.5.6)

\textstyle{H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}^{\prime})}
\textstyle{H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varpi_{p}^{-\lambda_{2}}\theta_{\lambda}{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}_{p}r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{\prime}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\lambda}}

Putting this together, we conclude

\displaystyle[\Phi,\Psi]_{\lambda}^{\mathrm{an}}=[\Phi,\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}\Psi]_{\mathrm{can}}

\displaystyle=\varpi_{p}^{-\lambda_{2}}[\Phi,j_{\lambda}\theta_{\lambda}{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}_{p}r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\Psi)]_{\mathrm{can}}

(by (
5.5.6
))

\displaystyle=\varpi_{p}^{-\lambda_{2}}[\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\Phi),\theta_{\lambda}{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}_{p}r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\Psi)]_{\mathrm{can}}

(by (
5.5.5
) with
\psi^{\prime}=\theta_{\lambda}{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}_{p}r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}\mathrm{Sp}(\Psi)
)

\displaystyle=\varpi_{p}^{-\lambda_{2}}[\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\Phi),\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}(\Psi)]_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\text{(by Remark \ref{remark:alpha-calculation})}.

This completes the proof.
∎

6.
The eigenvariety

The aim of this section is to discuss an eigenvariety
\mathscr{E}
for (finite slope) Hecke eigensystems appearing in the cohomology
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})
as
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}
varies. The eigenvariety is a rigid analytic variety over a weight space. In §
7
, we extend the Poincaré pairing to a pairing on coherent sheaves on
\mathscr{E}
, and we relate the extended pairing to the ramification of the eigenvariety over its weight space. Therefore, the target result in this section is Theorem
6.4.7
, describing geometrical properties of the eigenvariety.

There are different mechanisms for constructing eigenvarieties. For instance, an eigenvariety for cuspidal Hilbert
modular forms
is constructed in
[
AIP16
]
. Naturally, we use distribution-valued cohomology as in
[
BH24
,
BDJ22
]
. Eigenvarieties were developed in this style for more general reductive groups by Ash and Stevens, Urban, and Hansen
[
AS97
,
Urb11
,
Han17
]
. We work with a specific sub-eigenvariety
\mathscr{E}
called the
middle-degree
eigenvariety, coming out of the general constructions.

We maintain the notations of §
5
. We also introduce
\mathbb{T}
as the
L
-algebra generated by formal symbols
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
with
v\nmid\mathfrak{n}
and
U_{v}
with
v\mid p
. This algebra acts naturally on the cohomology
H^{\ast}_{?}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),-)
via Hecke operators. We also make a shift in notation when compared with §
5
. Namely, if
A
is an affinoid
L
-algebra and
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}(A)
, then
\lambda
also corresponds to a point
\Omega=\mathrm{Sp}(A)\rightarrow\mathscr{W}
. We choose to write
\mathscr{D}_{\Omega}
rather than
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
to describe
\mathscr{D}(\mathscr{O}_{p},A)
with the weight-
\lambda
action of
\Delta^{+}
.
If
\Omega=\{\lambda\}
where
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, then we still write
\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}
, as there should be no confusion.
Finally, if
X
is a rigid analytic space over
L
and
x\in X(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, we write
L_{x}
for its residue field. This is a finite extension of
L
.

6.1.
The total eigenvariety

Let
U_{p}\in\mathbb{T}
be
U_{p}:=\prod_{v\mid p}U_{v}^{e_{v}}
. Let
\Omega=\mathrm{Sp}(A)\rightarrow\mathscr{W}
denote an affinoid point of the weight space. Let
\nu
be a non-negative real number. Recall
\phi\in H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})
is said to have slope-
\leq\nu
if
U_{p}^{\deg Q}Q(U_{p}^{-1})\phi=0
for all
Q=a_{n}u^{n}+a_{n-1}u^{n-1}+\dotsb\in A[u]
such that
a_{n}\in A^{\times}
and the Newton polygon of
Q
has only slopes-
\leq\nu
. (Recall that
A
is an
L
-Banach algebra, hence comes equipped with a natural norm used to calculate the Newton polygon.) The slope-
\leq\nu
elements form an
A
-submodule
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})_{\leq\nu}
. A slope-
\leq\nu
factorization is a decomposition

H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})\overset{?}{\simeq}H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})_{\leq\nu}\oplus H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})_{>\nu}

as
A[U_{p}]
-modules such that the slope-
\leq\nu
submodule is finitely generated over
A
and
U_{p}^{\deg Q}Q(U_{p}^{-1})
acts by isomorphism on the complement, for the same collection of
Q
as before. (See
[
AS97
, §4]
or
[
Han17
, §2.3]
for further explanations of slope decompositions.) If
\Omega=\{\lambda\}
where
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, then a slope-
\leq\nu
factorization always exists. If
\Omega\subseteq\mathscr{W}
is an open affinoid, and a slope-
\leq\nu
factorization exists, then we call
(\Omega,\nu)
a slope adapted pair. In that case, we further define
\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\nu}
to be the
A
-algebra

(6.1.1)

\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\nu}:=\mathrm{im}\left(\mathbb{T}\otimes_{L}A\rightarrow\mathrm{End}_{A}(H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\mathscr{D}_{\Omega})_{\leq\nu})\right).

By definition, the
A
-algebra
\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\nu}
acts faithfully on the finitely generated
A
-module
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})_{\leq\nu}
.

The
total eigenvariety

\mathscr{Y}
of tame level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0})
is a rigid analytic space over
L
that is locally built from these Hecke algebras. By construction, each slope adapted pair
(\Omega,\nu)
gives rise to an affinoid subdomain
\mathscr{Y}_{\Omega,\nu}
that is canonically identified as the rigid analytic spectrum
\mathscr{Y}_{\Omega,\nu}\simeq\mathrm{Sp}(\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\nu})
. The space
\mathscr{Y}
is obtained by gluing together the
\mathscr{Y}_{\Omega,\nu}
. The morphisms
\mathscr{Y}_{\Omega,\nu}\rightarrow\Omega
glue to a rigid analytic map
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}:\mathscr{Y}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}
called the
weight map
. The word
total
refers to allowing all degrees of cohomology.

Points of
\mathscr{Y}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
correspond to eigensystems for
\mathbb{T}
, as we now recall. A (Hecke)
eigensystem
is an
L
-algebra morphism
\psi:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. It is
finite slope
if
\psi(U_{v})\neq 0
for all
v\mid p
. If
\psi
is an eigensystem and
M
is a
\mathbb{T}
-module, we write
M[\psi]
for the
\psi
-eigenspace, which is the set of
m\in M
such that
tm=\psi(t)m
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
. We say
\psi

appears
in weight
\lambda\in\mathscr{W}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
if
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})[\psi]\neq 0
. By
[
Han17
, Theorem 4.3.3]
, the points
x\in\mathscr{Y}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
with weight
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(x)=\lambda_{x}
are in bijection with finite slope Hecke eigensystems appearing in weight
\lambda_{x}
. We write this bijection
x\leftrightarrow\psi_{x}
. If
x\in\mathscr{Y}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, define

\mathfrak{m}_{x}=\ker(\mathbb{T}\otimes_{L}L_{x}\xrightarrow{\psi_{x}}L_{x}).

Thus,
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})[\mathfrak{m}_{x}]=H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})[\psi_{x}]
, and
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}
is the
generalized

\psi
-eigenspace.

Any particular point of
\mathscr{Y}
belongs to some
\mathscr{Y}_{\Omega,\nu}
and thus to a connected component of some
\mathscr{Y}_{\Omega,\nu}
. We isolate out terminology to refer to such a component.

Definition 6.1.1
.

A
good neighborhood
of
\mathscr{Y}
is (an affinoid open)
U\subseteq\mathscr{Y}
such that
U
is a connected component of
\mathscr{Y}_{\Omega,\nu}
for a slope adapted pair
(\Omega,\nu)
.

If
U
is a good neighborhood in Definition
6.1.1
, we say that
U

belongs
to
(\Omega,\nu)
. In that case, there is an idempotent
e_{U}\in\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\nu}
such that
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{Y}}(U)\simeq e_{U}\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\nu}
. The triple
(\Omega,\nu,e_{U})
is synonymous with
U
.

The eigenvariety
\mathscr{Y}
also comes equipped with a graded coherent sheaf
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{\ast}
. If
U
is a good neighborhood belonging to
(\Omega,\nu)
, the sections of
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{\ast}
over
U
are given by

\mathscr{M}_{c}^{\ast}(U)\simeq e_{U}H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})_{\leq\nu}.

This is a
\mathbb{T}_{\Omega,\nu}
-stable direct summand of
H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})_{\leq\nu}
on which
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{Y}}(U)
acts faithfully. The good neighborhoods are a co-final collection of admissible opens in
\mathscr{Y}
. See
[
BH24
, Proposition 6.2.9]
.

6.2.
The middle-degree eigenvariety

The middle-degree eigenvariety
\mathscr{E}\subseteq\mathscr{Y}
is defined in
[
BH24
, §6.4]
. As a warning,
op. cit.
writes
\mathscr{E}(\mathfrak{n}_{0})_{\mathrm{mid}}
. In particular, the
\mathfrak{n}
there is the
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
here. Conjecturally,
\mathscr{E}
is the largest Zariski open subspace of
\mathscr{Y}
containing all points corresponding to cohomological cuspidal automorphic representations (see §
6.3
). It is precisely defined as the complement in
\mathscr{Y}
of the supports of a finite number of coherent
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{Y}}
-modules. Those
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{Y}}
-modules, which involve Borel–Moore homology, have no role to play in this article. So, we omit the precise definition.
Instead, we describe the points
\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. Namely, by
[
BH24
, Proposition 6.4.3]
, if
x\in\mathscr{Y}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, then

(6.2.1)

x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\iff H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}=H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}.

The property (
6.2.1
) justifies the name “middle-degree eigenvariety”. The middle-degree support property remains true at the level of sheaves, in the sense that
loc. cit.
also proves
\mathscr{M}^{\ast}_{c}|_{\mathscr{E}}=\mathscr{M}^{d}_{c}|_{\mathscr{E}}
. Further properties are summarized in the next proposition.

Proposition 6.2.1
.

(i)

The rigid analytic space
\mathscr{E}
is reduced, equidimensional of
\dim(\mathscr{W})=1+d+\delta_{F,p}
, and the image of any irreducible component of
\mathscr{E}
is Zariski open in
\mathscr{W}
.

(ii)

The sheaf
\mathscr{M}^{d}_{c}|_{\mathscr{E}}
is flat over
\mathscr{W}
.

(iii)

If
U\subseteq\mathscr{E}
is a good neighborhood belonging to
(\Omega,\nu)
, then
\mathscr{M}^{d}_{c}(U)
is a finite projective
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
-module, and
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
acts faithfully on
\mathscr{M}^{d}_{c}(U)
.

(iv)

If
U\subseteq\mathscr{E}
is a good neighborhood belonging to
(\Omega,\nu)
and
x\in U(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, then the natural morphism

(6.2.2)

\mathscr{M}^{d}_{c}(U)_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}L_{\lambda_{x}}\longrightarrow H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

For part
(i)
, the reducedness is
[
BH24
, Theorem 6.4.9]
, the dimension statement is
[
BH24
, Proposition 6.4.7(3)]
, and the proof of
[
BH24
, Proposition 6.4.7(4)]
shows the claim on the irreducible components. Parts
(ii)
and
(iii)
are proven as
[
BH24
, Proposition 6.4.5]
. For part
(iv)
, we have

\mathscr{M}^{d}_{c}(U)_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}=(e_{U}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})_{\leq\nu})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}=\left[H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})_{\leq\nu}\right]_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}},

and it is explained in
[
BH24
, Remark 6.4.6]
that the natural map

\left[H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})_{\leq\nu}\right]_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}L_{\lambda_{x}}\rightarrow H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}

is an isomorphism.
∎

6.3.
Classical and twist classical points

The goals of this section are to recall the meaning of classical points on
\mathscr{Y}
and explain that these classical points are dense on
\mathscr{E}
, up to twists by Hecke characters.

Let
\lambda=(n,w)
be a classical algebraic weight. Assume that
(\pi,\alpha)
is a
p
-refined automorphic representation, as in §
3.4
, of weight
\lambda
and tame level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{\prime})
for some
\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{\prime}\mid\mathfrak{n}_{0}
. The reader may ignore the possibility that
\mathfrak{n}_{0}^{\prime}
is a proper divisor of
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
, but such considerations are required to make Proposition
6.3.3
accurate. Write
\omega_{\pi}
for the central character of
\pi
, and for
v
prime to
\mathfrak{n}
, let
a_{v}(\pi)
be the
T_{v}
-eigenvalue for
\pi
. Let
E\subseteq\mathbb{C}
be a number field that contains
\tau(F)
for all
\tau\in\Sigma_{\infty}
, along with
\alpha_{v}
for each
v\mid p
, and
a_{v}(\pi)
and
\omega_{\pi}(\varpi_{v})
for each
v
that is prime to
\mathfrak{n}
. Using the fixed isomorphism
\iota:\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, we view all of these numbers in
L=\iota(E)
. Then, define a finite slope Hecke eigensystem
\psi_{\pi,\alpha}:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow L
by

\psi_{\pi,\alpha}(T)=\begin{cases}a_{v}(\pi)&\text{if $T=T_{v}$ for $v\nmid\mathfrak{n}$;}\\
\omega_{\pi}(\varpi_{v})&\text{if $T=S_{v}$ for $v\nmid\mathfrak{n}$;}\\
\varpi_{v}^{\frac{n-w}{2}}\alpha_{v}&\text{if $T=U_{v}$ for $v\mid p$.}\end{cases}

Placing
\varpi_{v}^{\frac{n-w}{2}}
in the value of
\psi_{\pi,\alpha}(U_{v})
, for
v\mid p
, is designed to make
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}(\chi_{\lambda,\det}))^{\varepsilon}[\psi_{\pi,\alpha}]
non-zero for each sign
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
. Compare with the analogous discussion in §
3.4
.

Definition 6.3.1
.

Let
x\in\mathscr{Y}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.

(i)

We call
x

classical
if
\lambda_{x}
is classical and
\psi_{x}=\psi_{\pi,\alpha}
for some
(\pi,\alpha)
of weight
\lambda_{x}
.

(ii)

We call
x

non-critical
if
x
is classical and the specialization map

H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda_{x}}}H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda_{x}}(\chi_{\lambda_{x},\det}))_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}

is an isomorphism. We call
x

critical
if
x
is classical but not non-critical.

If
x
is classical, its tame level is defined to be the tame level of the underlying
p
-refined automorphic representation
(\pi,\alpha)
. We note that, in this article, classical points are limited to those arising from
p
-refinements of cohomological cuspidal automorphic representations. Moreover, non-critical points are
a priori
classical. Other authors may choose different conventions. The cohomological and cuspidal conditions imply, by results of Harder
[
Har87
]
, that if
x
is classical, then

H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda_{x}}(\chi_{\lambda_{x},\det}))_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}=H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda_{x}}(\chi_{\lambda_{x},\det}))_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}.

Therefore, if
x
is non-critical, then
x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.

We now recall the density, up to a twists, of non-critical points on
\mathscr{E}
. Let
\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F}^{(p)}
be the integral finite adeles supported away from
p
. Let
H
be the closure in
\mathbbm{A}_{F}^{\times}
of
\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F}^{(p)}F_{\infty,+}^{\times}
. Let
\Gamma_{F}
be the Galois group of the maximal abelian extension unramified away from
p
and
\infty
. The global Artin map provides an isomorphism
\Gamma_{F}\simeq F^{\times}\backslash\mathbbm{A}_{F}^{\times}/H
. In particular,
\Gamma_{F}
sits in a natural exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\Gamma_{p}\longrightarrow\Gamma_{F}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Cl}_{F}^{+}\longrightarrow 1.

So,
\Gamma_{F}
is a compact and abelian
p
-adic Lie group.

Now suppose
\vartheta\in\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{F})(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. Let
\psi:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
be an eigensystem. We define
\mathrm{tw}_{\vartheta}\psi
according to:

(\mathrm{tw}_{\vartheta}\psi)(T)=\begin{cases}\vartheta(\varpi_{v})\psi(T)&\text{if $T=T_{v}$ or $T=U_{v}$;}\\
\vartheta(\varpi_{v})^{2}\psi(T)&\text{if $T=S_{v}$}.\end{cases}

The operation
(\vartheta,\psi)\mapsto\mathrm{tw}_{\vartheta}\psi
on eigensystems induces a rigid analytic group action of
\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{F})
on
\mathscr{Y}
, under which
\mathscr{E}
is stable. For instance, suppose
\vartheta\in\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{F})(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, and let
\theta=\vartheta|_{\Gamma_{p}}
. Then, by
[
BH24
, Lemma 6.3.1]
, if
x\in\mathscr{Y}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, then there is a point
\mathrm{tw}_{\vartheta}(x)\in\mathscr{Y}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
of weight
\theta^{-1}\lambda_{x}
and a vector space isomorphism

(6.3.1)

\mathrm{tw}_{\vartheta}:H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\theta^{-1}\lambda})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\mathrm{tw}_{\vartheta}(x)}}.

(See
[
BH24
, Proposition 6.4.7(1)]
as well.)

Definition 6.3.2
.

Let
x\in\mathscr{Y}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.

(i)

We call
x

twist classical
if
x=\mathrm{tw}_{\vartheta}(x^{\prime})
for some
\vartheta\in\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{F})(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
and some classical
x^{\prime}\in\mathscr{Y}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.

(ii)

We call
x

twist non-critical
if
x=\mathrm{tw}_{\vartheta}(x^{\prime})
for some
\vartheta\in\mathscr{X}(\Gamma_{F})(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
and some non-critical
x^{\prime}\in\mathscr{Y}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.

If
x=\mathrm{tw}_{\vartheta}(x^{\prime})
with
x^{\prime}
classical, the tame level of
x
is defined to be the tame level of
x^{\prime}
. This is well-defined because each
\vartheta
is unramified away from
p
. (Compare with the proof of
[
BH24
, Lemma 6.5.7]
.) Additionally, if
x=\mathrm{tw}_{\vartheta}(x^{\prime})
with
x^{\prime}
classical, then whether or not
x^{\prime}
is non-critical is independent of
x^{\prime}
because of (
6.3.1
). (See also
[
BH24
, Lemma 6.3.5]
and the discussion after
[
BH24
, Definition 6.3.6]
.) Since twisting preserves
\mathscr{E}
, Definition
6.3.2
descends to
\mathscr{E}
. The twist non-critical points all belong to
\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. The next proposition implies that they are Zariski dense and accumulate (these notions were recalled in §
5.2
).

We will require a more precise density statement, in order to prove Theorem
6.4.7
(vi)
. Suppose
\lambda
is a twist classical weight. Then
\lambda=\theta\lambda^{\prime}
where
\lambda^{\prime}=(n,w)
is classical. The value of
n
is independent of
\lambda^{\prime}
by Proposition
5.2.3
(i)
. A twist classical
y\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
with weight
\lambda
is called
extremely non-critical
if

\sum_{v\mid p}e_{v}v_{p}(\psi_{y}(U_{v}))<\frac{1}{2}\min_{\tau\in\Sigma_{p}}(1+n_{\tau}).

By
[
BH24
, Proposition 6.3.8]
, extremely non-critical points are twist non-critical and thus twist classical. (This is an example of a classicality theorem, such as
[
Han17
, Theorem 3.2.5]
.)

Proposition 6.3.3
.

The twist non-critical points form a Zariski dense and accumulating subset of
\mathscr{E}
. More specifically, suppose
x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
is classical with tame level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0})
. Let
U
be a good neighborhood containing
x
. Then, there exists a subset
Z\subseteq{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(U)(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
with the following properties.

(i)

The set
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{-1}(Z)
is Zariski dense and accumulating in
U
.

(ii)

If
y\in{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{-1}(Z)
, then
y
is extremely non-critical of tame level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0})
.

Proof.

The first sentence of the proposition is deduced from
[
BH24
, Proposition 6.4.7(4)]
. The more specific statement is deduced from the proof of
loc. cit.
along with
[
BH24
, Lemma 6.5.7]
to handle the claim about the tame levels. (The proof of the tame level assertion relies on the variation of Galois representations, which we do not have a use for.)
∎

6.4.
Very decent points and geometrical theorems on eigenvarieties

The goal of this section is to explain theorems on the geometry of
\mathscr{E}
at
very decent
points. Bellaïche introduced decent points on the eigencurve
[
Bel12
]
. The second author and Hansen generalized the definition to Hilbert modular eigenvarieties
[
BH24
]
. Very decent points are
a priori
more specific than their precursors, but conjecturally there is no difference
and
conjecturally the definition simplifies just to a separability hypothesis on Hecke polynomials at
p
-adic places (assumption
(i)
in Definition
6.4.2
below). The main utility of the definition is that very decent points are provably non-singular on
\mathscr{E}
, which is part of Theorem
6.4.7
below.

Very decent points are classical by definition. We recall some relevant notions in order to make the definition. Suppose
(\pi,\alpha)
is a
p
-refined automorphic representation. Let
v\mid p
be a place such that
\pi_{v}
is a principal series representation. As in §
3.4
, the
v
-th Hecke polynomial

P_{v}(X)=X^{2}-a_{\pi}(v)X+\omega_{\pi}(\varpi_{v})q_{v}=(X-\alpha_{v})(X-\beta_{v})

has two roots
\alpha_{v}
and
\beta_{v}
. In general, we may or may not have
\alpha_{v}\neq\beta_{v}
. The first condition a very decent point satisfies is that
\alpha_{v}\neq\beta_{v}
for each such
v
. The remaining condition is that either the point on
\mathscr{Y}
corresponding to
(\pi,\alpha)
is non-critical, or two further assumptions, which we describe in the next paragraphs, are made related to the
p
-adic arithmetic of
(\pi,\alpha)
.

The choice of
\iota
allows one to attach a Galois representation
\rho_{\pi}
to
\pi
. Let
L
be as at the start of §
6.3
. Let
G_{F,\mathfrak{n}}
be the Galois group of the maximal extension of
F
unramified away from
\mathfrak{n}
. Then, there exists a two-dimensional, irreducible, representation

\rho_{\pi}:\mathrm{Gal}_{F,\mathfrak{n}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{2}(L)

that satisfies local-global compatibility at all finite places. The simplest version of this compatibility is that if
v
is a place away from
\mathfrak{n}
, then a (geometric) Frobenius element
\mathrm{Frob}_{v}
has characteristic polynomial

\det(X-\rho_{\pi}(\mathrm{Frob}_{v})I)=P_{v}(X)=X^{2}-a_{\pi}(v)X+\omega_{\pi}(\varpi_{v})q_{v}.

See
[
Car86
,
Wil88
,
BR93
,
Tay89
]
for the constructions and local-global statements away from
p
. The local-global statements at places above
p
are proven in
[
BR93
,
Sai09
,
Sai97
,
Ski09
]
. See
[
Ski09
, page 256]
for an argument to show that
\rho_{\pi}
is irreducible, using that
\pi
is cuspidal.

Let
\operatorname{ad}\rho_{\pi}
be the 4-dimensional adjoint representation. The first auxiliary condition for very decent
critical
points
x
is that the Bloch–Kato Selmer group
H^{1}_{f}(G_{F,\mathfrak{n}},\operatorname{ad}\rho_{\pi})
vanishes
[
BK90
]
. The Bloch–Kato conjectures in fact predicts this, and Newton and Thorne proved it as long as
\pi
does not have complex multiplication
[
NT23
, Theorem B(1)]
. The CM case is known if
F=\mathbb{Q}
(
[
Bel12
, Proposition 2.15(iv)]
), and further CM cases should follow from main conjectures for Hecke characters over CM fields.

Remark 6.4.1
.

Proving geometrical properties of
\mathscr{E}
at critical points relies on analyzing families of Galois representations over
\mathscr{E}
. Specifically, the hypothesis on the Selmer groups enters directly in the proof Theorem
6.4.7
(i)
below (and thus the other parts, which are a consequence of the first) in the case of critical points
x
.

The second auxiliary condition for very decent critical points involves companion weights and companion eigensystems. This condition was
not
previously included in the definition of decent critical points by the second author and Hansen. Assume
\lambda
is the weight of
(\pi,\alpha)
. Recall that as a
p
-adic weight, we view
\lambda
as

\lambda\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&b\end{pmatrix}\right)=\prod_{v\mid p}\prod_{\tau\in\Sigma_{v}}\tau(a_{v})^{\lambda_{1,\tau}}\tau(b_{v})^{\lambda_{2,\tau}}

where
\lambda_{1,\tau}\geq\lambda_{2,\tau}
for all
\tau
. For each
v\mid p
, let
S_{v}\subseteq\Sigma_{v}
be a (possibly empty) subset. Define
S=(S_{v})_{v\mid p}
and a new weight
\lambda_{S}
by

\lambda_{S}\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&b\end{pmatrix}\right)=\left(\prod_{v\mid p}\prod_{\tau\in S_{v}}\tau(a_{v})^{\lambda_{2,\tau}-1}\tau(b_{v})^{\lambda_{1,\tau}+1}\right)\times\left(\prod_{v\mid p}\prod_{\tau\not\in S_{v}}\tau(a_{v})^{\lambda_{1,\tau}}\tau(b_{v})^{\lambda_{2,\tau}}\right).

If
S_{v}=\emptyset
for all
v
, then
\lambda_{S}=\lambda
. The remaining
\lambda_{S}
are algebraic but non-dominant on
T(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.
They are the
companion weights
to
\lambda
.
For context, these new weights are the orbit of
\lambda
under the natural “dot”-action of the Weyl group of
G_{/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\simeq\prod_{v\mid p}\mathrm{Res}_{F_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\mathrm{GL}_{2}
(see
[
Urb11
, Section 3.2.9]

for relevant discussion). The Weyl group here is naturally isomorphic to
\prod_{v\mid p}S_{2}^{\Sigma_{v}}
with
S_{2}
being the permutation group on two letters. The
non-empty choices of
S=(S_{v})
are in bijection with the non-trivial elements of the Weyl group. Borrowing the
concept of length from the Lie theory, given
S
we define

\ell(S)=\sum_{v}|S_{v}|
. The BGG resolution (see
[
Urb11
, Theorem 3.3.10]
) is a natural resolution

(6.4.1)

0\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(S)=d}\mathscr{D}_{\lambda_{S}}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(S)=i}\mathscr{D}_{\lambda_{S}}\longrightarrow\dotsb\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(S)=2}\mathscr{D}_{\lambda_{S}}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(S)=1}\mathscr{D}_{\lambda_{S}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}_{\lambda}\overset{\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}\longrightarrow 0

of left
I
-representations. Each term in (
6.4.1
) is of course a left
\Delta^{+}
-module, and (
6.4.1
) is
\Delta^{+}
-equivariant up to some basic twists. We need the impact of these twists on cohomology. So, let
\psi:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
be an eigensystem. Given
S
, define
\psi_{S}:\mathbb{T}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
by

\psi_{S}(T)=\begin{cases}\psi(T)&\text{if $T=S_{v}$ or $T=T_{v}$ with $v\nmid\mathfrak{n}$;}\\
(\prod_{\tau\in S_{v}}\tau(\varpi_{v})^{1-n_{\tau}})\psi(T)&\text{if $T=U_{v}$ with $v\mid p$.}\\
\end{cases}

If
S_{v}
is non-empty for some
v\mid p
, then
\psi_{S}
is called a
companion eigensystem
to
\psi
. Let
\mathfrak{m}
be the maximal ideal corresponding to
\psi
and
\mathfrak{m}_{S}
the maximal ideal corresponding to
\psi_{S}
. Then, the twisting that makes (
6.4.1
)
\Delta^{+}
-equivariant implies there is a second quadrant spectral sequence

(6.4.2)

E_{1}^{i,j}=\bigoplus_{\ell(S)=-i}H^{j}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{S}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{S}}\implies H^{i+j}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}(\chi_{\lambda,\det}))_{\mathfrak{m}}.

Finally, the second auxiliary condition for very decent critical points is that the above localizations vanish away from their middle-degrees.

We now fully state the full definition.

Definition 6.4.2
.

A classical point
x\in\mathscr{Y}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, associated with
(\pi,\alpha)
, is called
very decent
if the following conditions hold.

(i)

For all
v\mid p
, if
\pi_{v}
is a principal series representation, then
\alpha_{v}\neq\beta_{v}
.

(ii)

Either
x
is non-critical or both of the following conditions hold:

(a)

H^{1}_{f}(G_{F,\mathfrak{n}},\operatorname{ad}\rho_{\pi})=(0)
, and

(b)

H^{\ast}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x,S}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x,S}}=H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x,S}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x,S}}
for all
S
.

Note that if
x
is very decent then
x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. Indeed, non-critical points lie in
\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
by the discussion following Definition
6.3.1
, while very decent critical points have their eigensystems supported in middle degree by condition
(ii)(b)
, taking
S_{v}=\emptyset
for all
v\mid p
.

Remark 6.4.3
.

For comparison with decent points defined
[
BH24
, Definition 1.5.2]
, we note that very decent points are decent ones that satisfy two extra assumptions. First, in Definition
6.4.2
we assume
(i)
holds for
x
, regardless of whether
x
is non-critical or critical, whereas the definition of decent requires
(i)
only if
x
is critical. Second, decent critical points were assumed to have
(ii)(b)
only when
S_{v}=\emptyset
for all
v\mid p
. Here, we have made the assumption more stringent, to also include any possible companion eigensystems. Note, we are not addressing whether companion eigensystems actually exist. For that, see Remark
6.4.4
. Rather, we simply assume that
if
they exist, then they lie on
\mathscr{E}
.

In our view, the inclusion of assumptions on companion points is not serious. Indeed, the middle-degree support condition for critical
x
is discussed in
[
BH24
, Appendix B]
, where the following result is shown. Let
\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
be the reduction modulo
p
of
\rho_{\pi}
. Then,
loc. cit.
proves that if
\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
contains
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
, then
x
lies in
\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. The proof begins with considerations of a vanishing theorem established by Caraiani and Tamiozzo for compactly supported cohomology, outside middle degrees, with constant torsion coefficients
[
CT
]
. Examining the proof, the passage from constant torsion coefficients to locally analytic distribution modules is insensitive to the weight. Moreover, the Galois representation associated with a companion eigensystem is identical to the one associated with the original eigensystem. Therefore, if the argument in
[
BH24
, Appendix B]
shows
x
is decent, then it also shows
x
is very decent.

Remark 6.4.4
.

The state of the art on companion points is the work of Breuil, Hellmann, and Schraen in the setting of definite unitary groups
[
BHS19
]
. Their theorems apply in principle to some settings with Hilbert modular forms. For instance, if
[F:\mathbb{Q}]
is even, then one can replicate the theory presented here with an analogous theory over definite quaternion algebras, where
op. cit.
applies.

The main reason to include the companion point assumption on very decent critical points is in order to deduce the following simple property of specialization.

Proposition 6.4.5
.

If
x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
is a very decent point , then the specialization map

H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda_{x}}}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda_{x}}(\chi_{\lambda_{x},\det}))_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}

is surjective.

Proof.

Suppose
x
is very decent. Set
\lambda=\lambda_{x}
. If
x
is non-critical, then
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}
is an isomorphism. Otherwise, condition
(ii)(b)
forces the spectral sequence (
6.4.2
) to collapse to a long exact sequence

\dotsb\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\ell(S)=1}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{S}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x,S}}\longrightarrow H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda}(\chi_{\lambda,\det}))_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\longrightarrow 0.

Either way,
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda}
is onto.
∎

The separability assumption in Definition
6.4.2
(i)
enters next.

Corollary 6.4.6
.

Let
x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
be very decent of tame level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0})
. Let
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
.

(a)

We have
\dim_{L_{x}}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda_{x}}(\chi_{\lambda_{x},\det}))^{\varepsilon}_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}=1
.

(b)

The point
x
is non-critical if and only if
\dim_{L_{x}}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})^{\varepsilon}_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}=1
.

Proof.

Since we assumed Definition
6.4.2
(i)
, we may apply Proposition
3.4.1
(ii)
. It directly implies part
(a)
, after the
\iota
-transfer described in §
4.1
and the renormalization due to the
\chi_{\lambda_{x},\det}
-twist.

For
(b)
, since
x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, we have that
x
is non-critical if and only
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda_{x}}
is an isomorphism in degree
d
. Therefore, part
(b)
an obvious consequence of Proposition
6.4.5
and part
(a)
.
∎

We are now in position to state the primary geometrical theorems on the middle-degree eigenvariety at very decent points. Part
(i)
and the consequences
(ii)
-
(v)
are proven in
[
BH24
]
. Let
x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
and
U
be a good neighborhood containing
x
and belonging to
(\Omega,\nu)
. We then define local rings
A_{\lambda_{x}}=\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)_{\mathfrak{m}_{\lambda_{x}}}
and
\mathbb{T}_{x}=\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}
. Given a sign
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
, set
\mathscr{M}_{c,x}^{d,\varepsilon}=\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d,\varepsilon}(U)_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}
. These definitions are apparently independent of the choice of
U
.

Theorem 6.4.7
.

Let
x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
be very decent of tame level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0})
. Let
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
. Then, the following conclusions hold.

(i)

The eigenvariety
\mathscr{E}
is non-singular at
x
.

(ii)

The natural map
A_{\lambda_{x}}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{x}
is flat and
\mathbb{T}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{\lambda_{x}}\mathbb{T}_{x}
is a local complete intersection ring.

(iii)

The
\mathbb{T}_{x}
-module
\mathscr{M}_{c,x}^{d,\varepsilon}
is free of rank one.

(iv)

The natural map

\mathbb{T}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{\lambda_{x}}\mathbb{T}_{x}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}_{L_{x}}(H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})^{\varepsilon}_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}})

is injective.

(v)

We have

\dim_{L_{x}}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})^{\varepsilon}[\mathfrak{m}_{x}]=1.

(vi)

The following are equivalent.

(a)

The point
x
is non-critical.

(b)

The map
A_{\lambda_{x}}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{x}
is unramified.

(c)

We have
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda_{x}}(H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})^{\varepsilon}[\mathfrak{m}_{x}])\neq\{0\}
.

Proof.

Part
(i)
is
[
BH24
, Theorem 6.6.3]
. More specifically, that theorem proves that
\mathscr{E}
is non-singular at
x
when it is decent and Definition
6.4.2
(i)
holds. Therefore, the theorem applies to any very decent
x
. This is where the assumption
(ii)(a)
in Definition
6.4.2
is crucially used in the case where
x
is critical.

Part
(ii)
is a consequence of part
(i)
and both parts of
[
BH24
, Lemma 8.1.1]
.

Parts
(iii)
,
(iv)
, and
(v)
are mostly proven in
[
BH24
, §8.1]
as well. Specifically
[
BH24
, Proposition 8.1.3]
shows
\mathscr{M}_{c,x}^{d,\varepsilon}
is free over
\mathbb{T}_{x}
, and it also shows part
(iv)
holds. The rank one assertion in
(iii)
and the dimension assertion in
(v)
are proven in
[
BH24
, Theorem 8.1.4]
.

Finally, we prove
(vi)
. After shrinking
U
, we may assume that the only point
y\in U(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
such that
\lambda_{y}=\lambda_{x}
is
y=x
. By Proposition
6.2.1
(iv)
and part
(iii)
we have isomorphisms of
L_{x}
-vector spaces

\mathbb{T}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{\lambda_{x}}\mathbb{T}_{x}\simeq\mathscr{M}_{c,x}^{d,\varepsilon}/\mathfrak{m}_{\lambda_{x}}\mathscr{M}_{c,x}^{d,\varepsilon}\simeq H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})^{\varepsilon}_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}.

By Corollary
6.4.6
(b)
we see that
x
is non-critical if and only if
\mathbb{T}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{\lambda_{x}}\mathbb{T}_{x}
is one-dimensional over
L_{x}
, which is equivalent to the flat map
A_{\lambda_{x}}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{x}
being unramified. Indeed,
\mathbb{T}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{\lambda_{x}}\mathbb{T}_{x}
is one-dimensional if and only if the natural map
\mathbb{T}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{\lambda_{x}}\mathbb{T}_{x}\rightarrow\mathbb{T}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}\mathbb{T}_{x}
is an isomorphism. So,
(vi)(a)
and
(vi)(b)
are equivalent. The equivalence of
(vi)(a)
and
(vi)(c)
is proven in
[
BH24
, Lemma 8.2.1]
, noting that the proof uses the fundamental properties established in parts
(ii)
-
(v)
.
∎

7.
The
p
-adic analytic twisted Poincaré pairing and ramification of the weight map

In this final section, we define a twisted Poincaré pairing over the eigenvariety
\mathscr{E}
and relate it to the ramification locus of the weight map. Combined with §
3
, we relate the adjoint
L
-value at
s=1
and the relative geometry of the eigenvariety of its weight space.

7.1.
Constructions with pairings

We first recall the
L
-ideal of a pairing following Bellaïche
[
Bel21
, §9.1.3]
. Let
A
be a noetherian ring and
T
an
A
-algebra. Let
\mathfrak{a}
be the kernel of the multiplication map on
T
, which is

\mathfrak{a}=\ker\left(T\otimes_{A}T\xrightarrow{(t,s)\mapsto ts}T\right)=\langle t\otimes 1-1\otimes t\mid t\in T\rangle.

Let
M
be a
T
-module. Then,
M\otimes_{A}T
has two
T
-module structures, one per tensor factor. The largest
A
-submodule
M_{T}\subseteq M\otimes_{A}T
on which there is an unambiguous
T
-module structure is

M_{T}:=(M\otimes_{A}T)[\mathfrak{a}]\subseteq M\otimes_{A}T.

Suppose
N
is a second
T
-module. Fix an
A
-bilinear pairing
[-,-]:M\otimes_{A}N\rightarrow A
that is
T
-equivariant in the sense that
[tm,n]=[m,tn]
for
t\in T
and
m\in M
and
n\in N
. We then extend
[-,-]
to a
T
-bilinear pairing

[-,-]:M_{T}\otimes_{T}N_{T}\rightarrow T.

The
L
-ideal
of
[-,-]
is the ideal
\mathfrak{L}\subseteq T
generated by

[\Phi,\Psi]
for
\Phi\in M_{T}
and
\Psi\in N_{T}
,
i.e.

\mathfrak{L}=\text{ the ideal generated by }\{[\Phi,\Psi]\mid\Phi\in M_{T}\text{ and }\Psi\in N_{T}\}.

The connection between
L
-ideals and properties of
A\rightarrow T
is explained in
[
Bel21
, §9.1]
. Here is a summary. (See
loc. cit.
for further discussion.)

•

Noether’s different
\mathfrak{d}_{\mathrm{N}}(T/A)
is the
L
-ideal of the multiplication map
A\otimes_{A}A\rightarrow A
.

•

For any pairing
[-,-]
, we have
\mathfrak{L}\subseteq\mathfrak{d}_{\mathrm{N}}(T/A)
by
[
Bel21
, Proposition 9.1.10]
.

•

If
T
is finite over
A
, then Auslander and Buchsbaum proved that the primes in
T
that ramify over
A
are the primes dividing
\mathfrak{d}_{\mathrm{N}}(T/A)

[
AB59
, Theorem 2.7]
. (Warning: Noether’s different is called the homological different in
op. cit.
)

Combining the second two points, if
T
is finite over
A
, the closed subscheme determined by an
L
-ideal always contains the ramification locus of
\mathrm{Spec}(T)\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(A)
, with its reduced subscheme structure. (Warning:
[
Bel21
, Theorem 9.4.7]
mistakenly reverses the containment.)

Later, in Proposition
7.4.1
, we will want to glue
L
-ideals in the context of eigenvarieties. So, we record here a formal lemma on base change and
L
-ideals. For terminology, let us refer to the
L
-ideal
\mathfrak{L}
as being associated with the data
(A,T,M,N,[-,-])
, as above.

Lemma 7.1.1
.

Suppose
\mathfrak{L}
is the
L
-ideal associated with the data
(A,T,M,N,[-,-])
. Let
\mathfrak{L}_{0}
be the
L
-ideal associated with the data
(A_{0},T_{0},M_{0},N_{0},[-,-]_{0})
in one of the two following cases.

(a)

We assume
A_{0}
is a flat
A
-algebra, and then define
T_{0}=T\otimes_{A}A_{0}
and
M_{0}=M\otimes_{A}A_{0}
and
N_{0}=N\otimes_{A}A_{0}
and finally
[-,-]_{0}
to be the scalar extension of
[-,-]
along
A\rightarrow A_{0}
.

(b)

We assume that
T_{0}\subseteq T
is an
A
-subalgebra that is a direct factor of
T
as an
A
-algebra, meaning that we can write
T=T_{0}\times T_{1}
as
A
-algebras for a suitable
A
-algebra
T_{1}
. Define
A_{0}=A
and
M_{0}=M\otimes_{T}T_{0}\subseteq M
and
N_{0}=N\otimes_{T}T_{0}\subseteq N
and
[-,-]_{0}
to be the restriction of
[-,-]
to
M_{0}\otimes_{A}N_{0}\subseteq M\otimes_{A}N

(the tensor products
-\otimes_{T}T_{0}
are taken with respect to the canonical projection
T\rightarrow T_{0}
).

Then, in either case, there is a canonical isomorphism

\mathfrak{L}\otimes_{T}T_{0}\simeq\mathfrak{L}_{0}.

Proof.

It is enough in either case to describe a canonical isomorphism
M_{T}\otimes_{T}T_{0}\simeq(M_{0})_{T_{0}}
, where
M
is any
T
-module and
T_{0}
and
M_{0}
depend on the context
(a)
or
(b)
.

(a)

Since annihilators commute with flat base change, we have natural isomorphisms

(7.1.1)

M_{T}\otimes_{T}T_{0}\simeq M_{T}\otimes_{A}A_{0}\simeq\left((M_{T}\otimes_{A}T)\otimes_{A}A_{0}\right)[\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{A}A_{0}].

Now let
\mathfrak{a}_{0}=\ker(T_{0}\otimes_{A_{0}}T_{0}\rightarrow T_{0})
. Multiplication commutes with all base change, and kernels commute with flat base change, so there is a canonical isomorphism

\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{A}A_{0}\simeq\mathfrak{a}_{0}.

Thus, the natural isomorphism

(M\otimes_{A}T)\otimes_{A}A_{0}\simeq M_{0}\otimes_{A_{0}}T_{0}

and (
7.1.1
) induces the claimed isomorphism
M_{T}\otimes_{T}T_{0}\simeq(M_{0})_{T_{0}}
.

(b)

Let
T=T_{0}\times T_{1}
be as in the statement and let
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
be the corresponding idempotents so that
e_{0}+e_{1}=1_{T}
(where
1_{T}
is the identity element of
T
),
e_{0}^{2}=e_{0}
,
e_{1}^{2}=e_{1}
,
e_{1}e_{2}=0
.
Write
M_{1}=e_{1}M=M\otimes_{T}T_{1}
(where the tensor product is taken with respect to the canonical projection
T\rightarrow T_{1}
), and note that
M\simeq M_{0}\times M_{1}
as
T
-modules, thanks to the equation
e_{0}+e_{1}=1_{T}
. Thus we have an isomorphism of
T\otimes_{A}T
-modules

(7.1.2)

\begin{split}M\otimes_{A}T&\simeq(M\otimes_{A}T_{0})\times(M\otimes_{A}T_{1})\\
&\simeq\left((M_{0}\otimes_{A}T_{0})\times(M_{1}\otimes_{A}T_{0})\right)\times\left((M_{0}\otimes_{A}T_{1})\times(M_{1}\otimes_{A}T_{1})\right)\end{split}

Let
t\in T
and write it as
t=t_{0}+t_{1}
with
t_{0}=te_{0}
and
t_{1}=te_{1}
.
Since
e_{0}e_{1}=0
, we see easily that the action of
t_{1}\otimes 1
is trivial on
M_{0}\otimes_{A}T_{1}
and
M_{0}\otimes_{A}T_{0}
, while
1\otimes t_{1}
acts trivially on
M_{0}\otimes_{A}T_{0}
and
M_{1}\otimes T_{0}
; similarly, the action of
t_{0}\otimes 1
is trivial on
M_{1}\otimes_{A}T_{0}
and
M_{1}\otimes_{A}T_{1}
, while
1\otimes t_{0}
acts trivially on
M_{1}\otimes_{A}T_{1}
and
M_{0}\otimes_{A}T_{1}
.
Using the decomposition (
7.1.2
), write
x\in M\otimes_{A}T
as
x=((x_{00},x_{10}),(x_{01},x_{11}))
with
x_{ij}=(e_{i}\otimes e_{j})x
for
i,j\in\{0,1\}
. Then we have

\begin{split}(t\otimes 1-1\otimes t)(x)&=(t\otimes 1-1\otimes t)((x_{00},x_{10}),(x_{01},x_{11}))\\
&=((t_{0}\otimes 1-1\otimes t_{0})(x_{00},0),(0,(t_{1}\otimes 1-1\otimes t_{1})x_{11}))\end{split}

and therefore we find that
M_{T}
is given by

M_{T}\simeq\left((M_{0})_{T_{0}}\times\{0\}\right)\times\left(\{0\}\times(M_{1})_{T_{1}}\right)\simeq(M_{0})_{T_{0}}\times(M_{1})_{T_{1}}.

Tracing through these isomorphisms, we see that
M_{T}\otimes_{T}T_{0}\simeq(M_{0})_{T_{0}}
.

The proofs are complete.
∎

7.2.
Calculating
L
-ideals

We now explain how to calculate
L
-ideals, in a favorable setting. The discussion we give is implicit in
[
Bel21
, §9.4]
and more explicit in
[
Bel12
, p. 53-56]
. We assume for the rest of §
7.2
that
T
is finite
flat
over
A
.

If
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq T
is a maximal ideal, we write
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\subseteq A
for its contraction. Define the residue fields
{k}=T/\mathfrak{m}
and
k^{\prime}=A/\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
. Let
\psi:T\twoheadrightarrow{k}
be the projection map. (We conceptualize
\psi
as an
{k}
-valued
T
-eigensystem.) Fix a
T
-module
M
and set
M_{k^{\prime}}=M\otimes_{A}k^{\prime}
. The
k^{\prime}
-vector space
M_{k^{\prime}}
is a
T
-module via the action of
T\twoheadrightarrow T/\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}T
. Moreover,
M_{k^{\prime}}\otimes_{k^{\prime}}{k}
is a
{k}
-vector space via the second tensor factor. The
\psi
-eigenspace is:

\displaystyle M_{k^{\prime}}[\mathfrak{m}]

\displaystyle=\{\Phi\in M_{k^{\prime}}\otimes_{k^{\prime}}{k}\mid t\Phi=\psi(t)\Phi\text{ for all $t\in T$}\}

\displaystyle=(M_{k^{\prime}}\otimes_{k^{\prime}}{k})[\mathfrak{a}].

We consider the natural reduction map induced by
\psi

M\otimes_{A}T\xrightarrow{\mathrm{red}_{\mathfrak{m}}}(M\otimes_{A}T)\otimes_{T}{k}.

Finally,
\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is defined as the composition

\textstyle{M\otimes_{A}T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{red}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
\textstyle{M_{k^{\prime}}\otimes_{k^{\prime}}{k}}
\textstyle{(M\otimes_{A}T)\otimes_{T}{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{M\otimes_{A}{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

The map
\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is
T\otimes_{A}T
-equivariant. Taking the
\mathfrak{a}
-torsion submodules, we deduce a
T
-module map

M_{T}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}}M_{k^{\prime}}[\mathfrak{m}].

We refer to
\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}
as an
evaluation map
.

The next lemma gives a criterion for
\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}
to be non-trivial on
M_{T}
. To state it, define

T^{\vee}=\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(T,A).

This is a
T
-module for the action
(t\cdot\ell)(s)=\ell(ts)
for all
\ell\in T^{\vee}
and
t,s\in T
. For context, the
A
-algebra
T
is called
Gorenstein
if
T^{\vee}
is flat of rank one over
T
. Note that since
T
is finite flat over
A
, the same is true for
T^{\vee}
, and there is a natural isomorphism

(7.2.1)

T^{\vee}\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}(T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}})^{\vee}

of
T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}}
-modules. (The target is the
{k^{\prime}}
-linear dual.)

Lemma 7.2.1
.

Assume
M\simeq T^{\vee}
as
T
-modules. Then, we have the following.

(i)

The
T
-module
M_{T}
is free of rank one.

(ii)

The
{k}
-vector space
M_{k^{\prime}}[\mathfrak{m}]
is one-dimensional.

(iii)

The evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}:M_{T}\otimes_{T}{k}\rightarrow M_{k^{\prime}}[\mathfrak{m}]
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We prove all three claims at once. Fix a
T
-module isomorphism
M\simeq T^{\vee}
. By (
7.2.1
), we have

M_{k^{\prime}}\simeq T^{\vee}\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}}\simeq(T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}})^{\vee}

as
T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}}
-modules. The evaluation map therefore fits into a commuting diagram

(7.2.2)

\textstyle{M\otimes_{A}T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(T,T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{k^{\prime}}\otimes_{k^{\prime}}{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{k^{\prime}}(T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}},{k}).}

The right-hand vertical arrow sends an
A
-linear endomorphism to the induced endomorphism on
T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}}
, composed with the projector
T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow{k}
. Taking
\mathfrak{a}
-torsion in the left-hand column of (
7.2.2
), we find the evaluation map
M_{T}\rightarrow M_{k^{\prime}}[\mathfrak{m}]
. By
[
Bel21
, Proposition 9.1.6]
, the
\mathfrak{a}
-torsion submodules in the right-hand column are

\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(T,T)[\mathfrak{a}]=\mathrm{Hom}_{T}(T,T)

and

\mathrm{Hom}_{k^{\prime}}(T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}},{k})[\mathfrak{a}]=\mathrm{Hom}_{T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}}}(T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}},{k}).

(Note,
loc. cit.
applies because
T
is finite flat over
A
.) From (
7.2.2
) we deduce a new commuting diagram

\textstyle{M_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{T}(T,T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{M_{k^{\prime}}[\mathfrak{m}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}}}(T\otimes_{A}{k^{\prime}},{k})}
\textstyle{{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

The isomorphisms across the top row prove part
(i)
, while those across the bottom row prove part
(ii)
. Part
(iii)
is also shown because
\psi
becomes an isomorphism after applying
-\otimes_{T}{k}
.
∎

We now return to
L
-ideals. Suppose
M
and
N
are two
T
-modules and fix an
A
-bilinear and
T
-equivariant pairing
[-,-]:M\otimes_{A}N\rightarrow A
. We use the same notation for the induced
{k^{\prime}}
-bilinear and
T/\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}T
-equivariant pairing
M_{k^{\prime}}\otimes_{k^{\prime}}N_{k^{\prime}}\rightarrow{k^{\prime}}
. The evaluation maps are compatible with these pairings, in the sense that for all
\Phi\in M_{T}
and
\Psi\in N_{T}
,

(7.2.3)

\psi([\Phi,\Psi])=[\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Phi),\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Psi)].

Proposition 7.2.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}\subseteq T
be the
L
-ideal of
[-,-]
. If
M\simeq T^{\vee}\simeq N
as
T
-modules, then we have the following.

(i)

The ideal
\mathfrak{L}
is principally generated.

(ii)

For each maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq T
, we have
\dim_{k}M_{k^{\prime}}[\mathfrak{m}]=1=\dim_{k}N_{k^{\prime}}[\mathfrak{m}]
.

(iii)

Let
\mathcal{L}\in\mathfrak{L}
be a
T
-generator (by part
(i)
). Then, for all maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq T
and any choice of non-zero vectors
\Phi_{\mathfrak{m}}\in M_{k^{\prime}}[\mathfrak{m}]
and
\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}\in N_{k^{\prime}}[\mathfrak{m}]
, there exists
c\in{k}^{\times}
such that

\psi(\mathcal{L})=c[\Phi_{\mathfrak{m}},\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}].

Proof.

By Lemma
7.2.1
(i)
,
M_{T}
and
N_{T}
are free of rank one over
T
. Choose
T
-module bases
\Phi
for
M_{T}
and
\Psi
for
N_{T}
. Then, part
(i)
holds because
\mathfrak{L}
is principally generated by
[\Phi,\Psi]
. (Compare with
[
Bel21
, Proposition 9.1.11]
.) Part
(ii)
follows from Lemma
7.2.1
(ii)
. We keep the choice of
\Phi
and
\Psi
to prove part
(iii)
. After scaling by
T^{\times}
, we may assume that
\mathcal{L}=[\Phi,\Psi]
. Then, by Lemma
7.2.1
(iii)
there exists
c_{i}\in{k}^{\times}
such that
\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Phi)=c_{1}\Phi_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\mathrm{ev}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Psi)=c_{2}\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Thus
\psi(\mathcal{L})=c[\Phi_{\mathfrak{m}},\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}]
by (
7.2.3
), for
c=c_{1}c_{2}
.
∎

7.3.
The analytic Poincaré pairing over
\mathscr{E}

In this section, we formally introduce the analytic Poincaré pairing over the middle-degree eigenvariety
\mathscr{E}
. Recall, if
U\subseteq\mathscr{E}
is a good neighborhood belonging to a slope adapted pair
(\Omega,\nu)
, then

\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)\simeq e_{U}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})_{\leq\nu}\subseteq H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega}).

We then consider the analytic Poincaré pairing given by Definition
5.4.1

[-,-]_{\Omega}^{\mathrm{an}}:H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})\rightarrow\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega).

(We follow the convention at the start §
6
, writing
[-,-]_{\Omega}
rather than
[-,-]_{\lambda_{\Omega}}
.)

Definition 7.3.1
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathscr{E}
be a good neighborhood belonging to a slope adapted pair
(\Omega,\nu)
. The
analytic Poincaré pairing over
U
is defined as the composition

\textstyle{\mathscr{M}^{d}_{c}(U)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}\mathscr{M}^{d}_{c}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}H^{d}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\Omega}^{\mathrm{an}}}

The bottom horizontal arrow emphasizes that
[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}
factors through a cup product. By construction,
[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}
is
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
-linear. The next two propositions establish properties analogous to Proposition
5.4.2
.

Proposition 7.3.2
.

(i)

The ring
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
acts on
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)
through operators that are self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}
.

(ii)

If
\varepsilon,\eta\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and
\varepsilon\neq-\eta
, then
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)^{\varepsilon}
is orthogonal to
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)^{\eta}
under
[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}
.

Proof.

By construction (
6.1.1
) and Proposition
6.2.1
(iii)
, we have that
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
is the
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
-algebra generated by the image of
\mathbb{T}
acting on
\mathscr{M}^{d}_{c}(U)
. By construction in Definition
7.3.1
and Proposition
5.4.2
(i)
, the operators defined by
\mathbb{T}
are self-adjoint for
[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}
. This proves part
(i)
. Part
(ii)
follows from the construction and Proposition
5.4.2
(ii)
.
∎

The next proposition establishes base change properties. The bulk of the explanation is describing their meanings. The results themselves follow from Proposition
5.4.2
(iii)
. Fix a good neighborhood
U
.

The first base change property is restriction to good neighborhoods. Suppose
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
is another good neighborhood. Say,
U
belongs to
(\Omega,\nu)
and
U^{\prime}
to
(\Omega^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
, where
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
and
\nu^{\prime}\leq\nu
. By
[
Han17
, Proposition 3.1.5]
, the natural base change map

{H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega})_{\leq\nu}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}(\Omega)}\mathscr{O}(\Omega^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega^{\prime}})_{\leq\nu},}

is an isomorphism. By the argument in
[
Bel21
, Lemma 3.6.1]
, we also have a natural isomorphism

(7.3.1)

H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega^{\prime}})_{\leq\nu}\otimes_{\mathbb{T}_{\Omega^{\prime},\nu}}\mathbb{T}_{\Omega^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}}\simeq H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega^{\prime}})_{\leq\nu^{\prime}}\subseteq H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega^{\prime}})_{\leq\nu},

and the inclusion is a direct summand. (The reference
loc. cit.
requires us to know
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\Omega^{\prime}})_{\leq\nu}
is finite projective over
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega^{\prime})
, which is justified by Proposition
6.2.1
(iii)
.) Passing to idempotents, we find

\mathscr{M}^{d}_{c}(U^{\prime})\simeq\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)}\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U^{\prime})\subseteq\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega^{\prime}).
