So, the pairing
[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}
on
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)
can be
restricted
to an
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega^{\prime})
-linear pairing on
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U^{\prime})
.

The second property is specialization to a weight
\lambda\in\Omega(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. For
\lambda\in\Omega(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, we define

\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}=\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}L_{\lambda}.

Let
\mathbb{T}_{\lambda}=\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}L_{\lambda}
. Thus,
\mathbb{T}_{\lambda}
is a finite-dimensional
L_{\lambda}
-algebra. As a
\mathbb{T}_{\lambda}
-module, hence also as a
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
-module, we have

\mathscr{M}_{\lambda}\simeq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in U\\
\lambda_{x}=\lambda\end{subarray}}(\mathscr{M}_{\lambda})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}.

Since
[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}
is
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
-linear it induces a natural pairing

[-,-]_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}:(\mathscr{M}_{\lambda})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\otimes_{L_{\lambda}}(\mathscr{M}_{\lambda})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\rightarrow L_{\lambda}

on each summand. By Proposition
6.2.1
(iv)
, the source of
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}
is identified as

(\mathscr{M}_{\lambda})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}\subseteq H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda}).

Proposition 7.3.3
.

Suppose
U
is a good neighborhood of
\mathscr{E}
.

(i)

If
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
is a second good neighborhood, then

[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an}}=[-,-]_{U^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{an}}
on
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U^{\prime})
.

(ii)

If
x\in U(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
then
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}=[-,-]_{\lambda_{x}}^{\mathrm{an}}
on
H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}
.

Proof.

Both parts follow from Proposition
5.4.2
(iii)
, following the identifications explained above.
∎

7.4.
The adjoint
L
-ideal sheaf and ramification on
\mathscr{E}

In this section, we apply the abstract theory of
L
-ideals in §
7.1
-
7.2
to the analytic Poincaré pairings over
\mathscr{E}
in §
7.3
. We first define the
adjoint
L
-ideal sheaf
on
\mathscr{E}
. (There should be no confusion with the local systems
\underline{\mathscr{L}}_{\lambda}
on
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
.) Technically the sheaf depends on the choice of a sign
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
, so we include that in the notation.

To define it, first suppose
U
is a good neighborhood on
\mathscr{E}
, belonging to a slope adapted pair
(\Omega,\nu)
. From Definition
7.3.1
, we have the analytic Poincaré pairing

[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an},\varepsilon}:\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)^{\varepsilon}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)}\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)^{-\varepsilon}\longrightarrow\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)

that is
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
-equivariant, by Proposition
7.3.2
(i)
. We then denote by
\mathfrak{L}_{U}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
the
L
-ideal of
[-,-]_{U}^{\mathrm{an},\varepsilon}
. This is an ideal in
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
.

Proposition 7.4.1
.

If
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
is a good neighborhood, then the natural map
\mathfrak{L}_{U}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)}\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U^{\prime})
is injective and defines an isomorphism
\mathfrak{L}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}_{U}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)}\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U^{\prime})\simeq\mathfrak{L}_{U^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

There are two basic cases to consider, to start. First,
U^{\prime}
may belong to
(\Omega^{\prime},\nu)
for some
\Omega^{\prime}
but the same
\nu
. Second,
U^{\prime}
may belong to
(\Omega,\nu^{\prime})
with
\nu^{\prime}<\nu
. In the first case,
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)\rightarrow\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega^{\prime})
is a flat base change and the
\mathfrak{L}
-ideal
\mathfrak{L}_{U^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ad},\varepsilon}
arises from base change by Lemma
7.1.1
(a)
. Similarly, in the second case,
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U^{\prime})
is a direct factor of
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
(as explained in the prior section) as an
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
-algebra. Thus the
\mathfrak{L}
-ideal
\mathfrak{L}_{U^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{ad},\varepsilon}
arises from base change by Lemma
7.1.1
(b)
. Finally, a general good
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
is a combination of these two basic cases.
∎

By Proposition
7.4.1
and the discussion following Definition
6.1.1
, the following definition is well-posed.

Definition 7.4.2
.

The adjoint
L
-ideal sheaf
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
is the unique ideal sheaf on
\mathscr{E}
for which
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}(U)=\mathfrak{L}_{U}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
for all good neighborhoods
U
of
\mathscr{E}
.

Now let
\mathscr{Z}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
be the reduced closed analytic subspace determined by
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
. The general properties of
L
-ideals in §
7.1
imply that
\mathscr{Z}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
contains every point of ramification of the weight map. Any kind of converse requires significant analysis of the analytic Poincaré pairing. For instance, Proposition
7.4.1
and Definition
7.4.2
are perfectly valid even if we use
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)^{\eta}
where
\eta\neq-\varepsilon
as the second tensor factor. But in those cases we of course end up with the zero ideal sheaf, by Proposition
7.3.2
(ii)
. By choosing the signs correctly and focusing on very decent points, we have the following theorem, which was the primary goal of this article.

Theorem 7.4.3
.

Let
x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
be a very decent point of tame level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0})
and
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
. Then,
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
is free of rank one in a neighborhood of
x
, and the following are equivalent.

(i)

The point
x
belongs to
\mathscr{Z}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
.

(ii)

The point
x
is critical.

(iii)

The weight map ramifies at
x
.

Proof.

We first prove that
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
is principally generated at
x
. Let
U
be a good neighborhood of
x
belonging to a slope adapted pair
(\Omega,\nu)
. Since
x
is very decent, we may shrink
U
in order to assume the following.

(a)

Both
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
and
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
are regular and
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
is finite flat over
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
.

(b)

Both
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)^{-\varepsilon}
are free of rank one as
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
-modules.

Indeed,
(a)
follows from Theorem
6.4.7
(i)
-
(ii)
and
(b)
follows from Theorem
6.4.7
(iii)
. We conclude from
(a)
that
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
is a Gorenstein
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{W}}(\Omega)
-algebra. After possibly shrinking
U
further, we therefore assume
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)^{\vee}
is free of rank one as an
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
-module. By
(b)
, each of
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)^{\varepsilon}
,
\mathscr{M}_{c}^{d}(U)^{-\varepsilon}
and
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)^{\vee}
are isomorphic as
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
-modules. Therefore
\mathfrak{L}_{U}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
is principally generated over
\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)
by Proposition
7.2.2
(i)
.

It remains to prove the equivalences. First we set some notation. Recall that
L_{x}
denotes the residue field of
x
. By Theorem
6.4.7
(v)
we have

\dim_{L_{x}}H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})^{\pm\varepsilon}[\mathfrak{m}_{x}]=1.

Fix any non-zero vectors
\Phi_{x}^{\pm\varepsilon}\in H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{D}}_{\lambda_{x}})^{\pm\varepsilon}[\mathfrak{m}_{x}]
. Also fix
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}\in\mathfrak{L}_{U}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
a generator. Let
L_{x}=\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{E}}(U)/\mathfrak{m}_{x}
be the residue field at
x
, and let
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}(x)\in L_{x}
be the value of
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}
in
L_{x}
.

By Theorem
6.4.7
(vi)
we find that
x
is critical if and only if it is a ramification point of the weight map
and
both are equivalent to
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda_{x}}(\Phi_{x}^{\pm\varepsilon})=0
(for either
\pm\varepsilon
). To complete our proof, we therefore show that
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda_{x}}(\Phi_{x}^{\pm\varepsilon})=0
if and only if
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}(x)=0
. To this end, by Proposition
7.2.2
(iii)
and Proposition
7.3.3
(ii)
, there exists a
c\neq 0
in
L_{x}
such that

L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}(x)=c[\Phi_{x}^{\varepsilon},\Phi_{x}^{-\varepsilon}]_{\lambda_{x}}^{\mathrm{an}}.

Then, by Proposition
5.5.1
we have that

(7.4.1)

L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}(x)=c\varpi_{p}^{-\lambda_{2,x}}[\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda_{x}}(\Phi_{x}^{\varepsilon}),\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda_{x}}(\Phi_{x}^{-\varepsilon})]_{\lambda_{x}},

where we emphasize that
[-,-]_{\lambda_{x}}
is the classical twisted Poincaré pairing. From (
7.4.1
) we obviously conclude that
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}(x)=0
whenever
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda_{x}}(\Phi_{x}^{\pm\varepsilon})=0
. Conversely, if
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda_{x}}(\Phi_{x}^{\pm\varepsilon})\neq 0
then
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}(x)\neq 0
by Proposition
3.4.2
.
∎

7.5.
Final remarks

We end this paper with several remarks, separated out for easier reference.

Remark 7.5.1
.

First, we collect our results, to justify calling
\mathscr{L}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
the adjoint
L
-ideal sheaf. Suppose
x
is a very decent point on
\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
of tame level
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n}_{0})
associated with a
p
-refined automorphic representation
(\pi,\alpha)
and assume that
x
is non-critical. In the proof above, the non-zero vectors
\Phi_{x}^{\pm\varepsilon}
specialize to a pair of non-zero vectors
\mathrm{Sp}_{\lambda_{x}}(\Phi_{x}^{\pm\varepsilon})\in H^{d}_{c}(Y_{0}(\mathfrak{n}),\underline{\mathscr{V}}_{\lambda_{x}})
. Let
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}
be as in the proof above. Then by (
7.4.1
), and Theorem
3.8.2
and Corollary
3.8.4
, there exists a non-zero
\Omega_{0}
(periods and other factors depending on
\pi
, sign
\pm\varepsilon
, etc.) and an interpolation factor
e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)
such that

L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}(x)\overset{\cdot}{=}\varpi_{p}^{-\lambda_{2,x}}e_{p}(\pi,\alpha)\frac{L(1,\pi,\mathrm{Ad}^{0})}{\Omega_{0}},

where the dot means up to the non-specific constant implicit in the choice of
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}
.

We should also compare the outcome of our research with that of others. Much of work is based on generalizing the approach of Bellaïche from the case of
F=\mathbb{Q}
to the general totally real field. The theorems proven in
[
Bel21
, §9.4]
are stronger in two ways, which is the focus of the next remarks.

Remark 7.5.2
.

Bellaïche, as part of
[
Bel21
, Theorem 9.4.7]
, proves that if
x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
is sufficiently “good” and of non-algebraic weight, then
x\in\mathscr{Z}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}
if and only if the weight map ramifies at
x
. This further reinforces the connection between the
L
-ideal and ramification. A similar statement should hold for general
F
, but we did not pursue the technical challenges required to prove it. Of course, the formalism of
L
-ideals
does
say that if
x
is sufficiently good and the pairing
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}
is non-degenerate, then there is a good neighborhood
U
of
x
for which
\mathscr{Z}^{\operatorname{ad},\varepsilon}\cap U
is exactly the ramification locus of the weight map, in
U
. So, the challenge is really in justifying that there exists points with specific qualities (such as non-algebraic weight) for which
[-,-]_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}
is non-degenerate.

Remark 7.5.3
.

Bellaïche also analyzes the critical case of Theorem
7.4.3
more deeply. Namely, in
[
Bel21
, Theorem 9.4.8(ii)]
he proves that if
x
is a very decent critical point on
\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
, then the order of vanishing of
L_{p}^{\operatorname{ad}}
at
x
is
2e-2
, where
e=\dim_{L_{x}}\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\lambda}\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{m}_{x}}
. (Note
e>1
by Theorem
6.4.7
(vi)
.) His analysis is based in part on knowing that
\mathscr{E}
is a curve, a coincidence that clearly does not hold in general. We did not pursue the order of vanishing in Theorem
7.4.3
any further.

Remark 7.5.4
.

Wu and Lee–Wu have studied the case of symplectic groups and
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
over imaginary quadratic fields
[
Wu21
,
LW23
]
. They construct pairings on distribution-valued cohomology, and, in the case of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}
, relate them to adjoint
L
-values. They also state and prove theorems relating the ramification of the weight map to the vanishing of adjoint
L
-ideal sheaves in neighborhoods of
non-critical
points.

Remark 7.5.5
.

In the case of the eigencurve, so
F=\mathbb{Q}
, the second author previously studied ramification and critical behavior of cuspforms. The primary outcome of
[
Ber14
, Theorem 1.1]
is that a very decent point
x\in\mathscr{E}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
is a ramification point if and only if it has a companion point as described in §
6.4
. For general
F
, half of
loc. cit.
is already implicit in this article. Namely, if
x
is very decent and the weight map ramifies at
x
, then a companion point exists. Indeed, once
x
ramifies, then we know
x
is critical by Theorem
6.4.7
(vi)
and, so, a companion point exists by the long exact sequence in the proof of Proposition
6.4.5
. A plausible attack on the converse statement would likely need to rely on the analysis of Galois representations over
\mathscr{E}
given in
[
BH24
, §6.6]
.

Appendix A
Hecke-theoretic properties of pairings

The goal of this appendix is to provide a reference for the Hecke-theoretic properties of the Poincaré pairings in §
4
and
5
, and therefore implicitly also in §
2
. The purpose of placing the calculations in an appendix is that they are formally similar, essentially of an algebraic nature.

A.1.
Recollection and summary of notations

We have found it easiest to make constructions referring to the Hilbert modular variety of level
K
in its basic form

Y_{K}=G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash G(\mathbbm{A})/KK_{\infty}^{+}
as in §
2.1
. We assume throughout that
K\subseteq G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
is a level such that
\det(K)\subseteq Z(\mathbbm{A}_{f})\cap K
. For instance,
K=K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
. We also fix a ring
A
and a monoid
\Delta\subseteq G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
that contains
K
and such that
K\cap gKg^{-1}
is finite index in
K
for all
g\in\Delta
.

Let
M
be a left
A[\Delta]
-module. We make
M
an
A
-linear
(G(\mathbb{Q})^{+},K)
-bimodule by declaring
G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
to act trivially and
k\in K
to act on the right via
k^{-1}\in\Delta
on the left. We form the adelic cochains
C^{\bullet}_{?}(K,M)
with or without compact supports as in §
2.3
. If
g\in\Delta
and
\varphi\in C^{\bullet}_{?}(K,M)
and
\sigma\in C_{\bullet}(D_{\mathbbm{A}})
, we write

(g\cdot\varphi)(\sigma)=g\varphi(\sigma g)\in M.

Then,
g\cdot\varphi
is an adelic cochain, except at a new level as we now recall. For
h\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
, write
h_{g}=ghg^{-1}
. Write
\Delta_{g}=g\Delta g^{-1}
as well as
K_{g}=gKg^{-1}
and so on. Then, define
g^{\ast}M
to be the left
\Delta_{g}
-module given by by
h_{g}m=hm
for all
h\in\Delta
(cf. §
2.4
). Then,
g\cdot\varphi\in C^{\bullet}_{?}(K_{g},g^{\ast}M)
. Despite this, the Hecke operator
[KgK]
itself acts on
C^{\bullet}_{?}(K,M)
by a formal sum

[KgK]\varphi=\sum g_{i}\cdot\varphi

where
KgK=\bigsqcup g_{i}K
. We may
always
choose the
g_{i}
in such a way that

(A.1.1)

\bigsqcup g_{i}K=KgK=\bigsqcup Kg_{i}.

(See the proof of
[
DS05
, Lemma 5.5.1]
, which adapts directly to the adelic setup.) We will also consider the Archimedean Hecke operators described in §
2.5
.

We also recall the calculation of cup products, since our pairings depend on them. Suppose
\sigma\in C_{2d}(D_{\mathbbm{A}})
and
i+j=2d
. We write
\sigma_{i}
for the
i
-th front face and
\sigma_{j}
for its
j
-th back face. Suppose in addition to
M
we have a second right
K
-representation
N^{\prime}
. Then, if
\varphi\in C^{i}_{c}(K,M)
and
\psi\in C^{j}(K,N^{\prime})
, their cup product
\varphi\cup\psi\in C^{2d}_{c}(K,M\otimes_{A}N^{\prime})
is defined by

(\varphi\cup\psi)(\sigma)=\varphi(\sigma_{i})\otimes\psi(\sigma_{j}).

We now place ourselves in a specific context. Let
A
,
\Delta
, and
K
be as above. However, assume that
N
is a
right

A[\Delta]
-module and there exists
\chi:Z(\mathbbm{A})\cap K\rightarrow A^{\times}
such that

(A.1.2)

n|_{x}=\chi(x)n,

for all
x\in Z(\mathbbm{A})\cap K
and
n\in N
. We denote by
\chi_{\det}:K\rightarrow A^{\times}
the composition
\chi_{\det}=\chi\circ\det
. We then make the following constructions.

(1)

Let
N^{\vee}=\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(N,A)
be the left
A[\Delta]
-module determined by
(g\mu)(n)=\mu(n|_{g})
for all
g\in\Delta
and
\mu\in N^{\vee}
. We then
fix
a
\Delta
-stable submodule
M\subseteq N^{\vee}
.

(2)

Recall, if
g\in G(\mathbbm{A})
, then
g^{\prime}=\det(g)g^{-1}
. We have
K^{\prime}=K
because
\det(K)\subseteq K
. Set

\Delta^{\prime}=\{g^{\prime}\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})\mid g\in\Delta\}.

We
define

N^{\prime}=N
as an
A
-module, with a left
\Delta^{\prime}
-module action
gn=n|_{g^{\prime}}
if
g\in\Delta^{\prime}
and
n\in N^{\prime}
.

Note that
K\subseteq\Delta\cap\Delta^{\prime}
, and
x\in Z(\mathbbm{A})\cap K
acts on both
M
and
N^{\prime}
on the left via
\chi(x)
because
x^{\prime}=x
for
x\in Z(\mathbbm{A})
. Therefore, the central character of the
right

K
-representations
M
and
N^{\prime}
is given by
\chi^{-1}
.

The pairing
\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}:M\otimes_{A}N^{\prime}\rightarrow A
is defined by
\langle\mu,n\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}=\mu(n)
. If
g\in\Delta
, then

(A.1.3)

\langle g\mu,n\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}=\langle\mu,g^{\prime}n\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}.

If
k\in K
then
\langle k^{-1}\mu,k^{-1}n\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}=\chi_{\det}(k^{-1})\langle\mu,n\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}
. Therefore,
\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}
defines an
A
-bilinear pairing of
right

K
-representations

(A.1.4)

M\otimes_{A}N^{\prime}(\chi_{\det})\rightarrow A.

A.2.
The initial pairing

We now treat each of these
K
-representations
V=M
,
N^{\prime}
, or
N^{\prime}(\chi_{\det})
as trivial
G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}
-modules, so that we get
(G(\mathbb{Q})^{+},K)
-bimodules. They each define a local system

\underline{V}=G(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\left(G(\mathbbm{A})\times V\right)/KK_{\infty}^{+}=G(\mathbb{Q})^{+}\backslash\left(D_{\infty}\times G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})\times V\right)/K

on
Y_{K}
. Let
0\leq i\leq 2d
. The goal of this section is to construct a specific pairing

(A.2.1)

[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}:H^{i}_{c}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\otimes_{A}H^{2d-i}(Y_{K},\underline{N}^{\prime})\rightarrow A

and that prove that if
g\in\Delta
, the Hecke operator
[KgK]
on the first factor is adjoint to the Hecke operator
[Kg^{\prime}K]
on the second factor. We additionally prove an orthogonality statement for the sign components as in §
2.5
.

First, the canonical pairing (
A.1.4
) induces the
A
-bilinear map

\textstyle{H^{i}_{c}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\otimes_{A}H^{2d-i}(Y_{K},\underline{N}^{\prime}(\chi_{\det}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{K},\underline{M}\otimes_{A}\underline{N}^{\prime}(\chi_{\det}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}
\textstyle{H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{K},A).}

The notation “
\mathrm{coh}
” emphasizes that the target of
\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}
is cohomology. We
abuse
notation and define

H^{i}_{c}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\otimes_{A}H^{2d-i}(Y_{K},\underline{N}^{\prime}(\chi_{\det}))\xrightarrow{\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}}A

by
\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}=\operatorname{PD}\circ\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}
. So, the pairings
\langle-,-\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}
are the scalar-valued ones.

Next, we introduce a determinant twist. Recall that
\mathrm{d}(g)=\det(g)^{-1}g
on
G(\mathbbm{A})
and
\mathrm{d}
induces a map on
Y_{K}
as in §
2.4
. The identity map
N^{\prime}(\chi_{\det})\rightarrow N
defines a lift

(A.2.2)

\textstyle{\underline{N}^{\prime}(\chi_{\det})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(g,n)\mapsto(\mathrm{d}(g),n)}
\textstyle{\underline{N}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{d}}
\textstyle{Y_{K}}

to local systems on
Y_{K}
. Let
\mathrm{d}^{\ast}:H^{d}(Y_{K},N^{\prime})\rightarrow H^{d}(Y_{K},N^{\prime}(\chi_{\det}))
be its pullback. Then, define

[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}:H^{i}_{c}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\otimes_{A}H^{2d-i}(Y_{K},\underline{N}^{\prime})\rightarrow H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{K},A)

by
[\varphi,\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}=\langle\varphi,\mathrm{d}^{\ast}\psi\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}

and

[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}:H^{i}_{c}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\otimes_{A}H^{2d-i}(Y_{K},\underline{N}^{\prime})\rightarrow A

by
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}=\operatorname{PD}\circ[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}
.

Proposition A.2.1
.

The pairing
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}
satisfies the following compatibilities.

(i)

If
g\in\Delta
then

[[KgK]\varphi,\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}=[\varphi,[Kg^{\prime}K]\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}.

(ii)

If
\zeta\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
then

[T_{\zeta}\varphi,\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}=\zeta[\phi,T_{\zeta}\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}.

(In part
(ii)
, the scalar
\zeta
on the right-hand side means
\prod_{\tau}\zeta_{\tau}=\pm 1
.)

Proof of Proposition
A.2.1
.

We first prove
(i)
. Let
g\in\Delta
. As in (
A.1.1
), we may choose
g_{i}\in\Delta
such that

\bigsqcup g_{i}K=KgK=\bigsqcup Kg_{i}.

Then, we have

Kg^{\prime}K=(KgK)^{\prime}=\bigsqcup g_{i}^{\prime}K.

So, it suffices to show that
[g\cdot\varphi,\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}=[\varphi,g^{\prime}\cdot\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}
if
g\in\Delta
and
\varphi\in C^{i}_{c}(K,M)
and
\psi\in C^{2d-i}(K_{g},g^{\ast}N^{\prime})
.

We first make the calculation at the level of cohomology. Let
\sigma\in C_{2d}(D_{\mathbbm{A}})
. Recall, the pullback
r_{g}^{\ast}:H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{K_{g}},A)\rightarrow H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{K},A)
is defined in §
2.4
. We have:

(A.2.3)

\displaystyle[g\cdot\varphi,\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\langle g\varphi(\sigma_{i}g),(\mathrm{d}^{\ast}\psi)(\sigma_{2d-i})\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}

\displaystyle=\langle g\varphi(\sigma_{i}g),\psi(\mathrm{d}(\sigma_{2d-i})\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi(\sigma_{i}g),g^{\prime}\psi(\mathrm{d}(\sigma_{2d-i})\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}

(by (
A.1.3
), noting that
g^{\prime}_{g}=g^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi(\sigma_{i}g),g^{\prime}\psi(\mathrm{d}(\sigma_{2d-i})\mathrm{d}(g)g^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}

(because
\mathrm{d}(h)h^{\prime}=1
for all
h\in G(\mathbbm{A})
)

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi(\sigma_{i}g),g^{\prime}\cdot\psi)(\mathrm{d}(\sigma_{2d-i}g)g^{\prime})\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}

(because
\mathrm{d}
is multiplicative)

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi(\sigma_{i}g),(g^{\prime}\cdot\psi)(\mathrm{d}(\sigma_{2d-i}g))\rangle_{\mathrm{can}}

\displaystyle=[\varphi,(g^{\prime}\cdot\psi)]_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}(\sigma g)

\displaystyle=r_{g}^{\ast}[\varphi,g^{\prime}\cdot\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}(\sigma).

The lines without justification are based only on definitions. By (
2.2.3
), we have
\operatorname{PD}r_{g}^{\ast}=\operatorname{PD}
for
g\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
. Therefore, applying
\operatorname{PD}
to either side, we see
[g\cdot\varphi,\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}=[\varphi,g^{\prime}\cdot\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}
, as claimed. This completes the proof of
(i)
.

The proof of
(ii)
follows the same path. Let
t_{\zeta}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\zeta&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in G(\mathbb{R})
. By definition,
T_{\zeta}=r_{t_{\zeta}}^{\ast}
. There is no action on the coefficients of cohomology. Note that
t_{\zeta}^{\prime}=\zeta t_{\zeta}
and since
\zeta\in G(\mathbb{R})^{+}
is scalar we have
r_{t_{\zeta}}=r_{t_{\zeta}^{\prime}}
as functions on
Y_{K}
. Therefore, (
A.2.3
) shows that

[T_{\zeta}\varphi,\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}=r_{t_{\zeta}}^{\ast}[\varphi,T_{\zeta}\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}^{\mathrm{coh}}

in
H^{2d}_{c}(Y_{K},A)
. By (
2.2.3
), we have
[T_{\zeta}\varphi,\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}=\operatorname{sgn}(\det t_{\zeta})[\varphi,T_{\zeta}\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}=\zeta[\varphi,T_{\zeta}\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}
, as claimed.
∎

A.3.
An Atkin–Lehner-type twist

We now adjust the canonical pairing
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}
. We define a new pairing

[-,-]_{M}:H^{i}_{c}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\otimes_{A}H^{2d-i}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\rightarrow A

and calculate the adjoint of the Hecke operators
[KgK]
for
g\in\Delta
. The specific pairing depends on an Atkin–Lehner-type operation, and an auxiliary choice of monoid-equivariant morphism. To begin, suppose that
{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}\in G(\mathbbm{A}_{f})
such that

(a)

\Delta_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}=\Delta^{\prime}
, and

(b)

K_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}=K
(which we assume is
K^{\prime}
, as above).

By assumption
(b)
, the morphism
r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}:Y_{K}\rightarrow Y_{K}
is well-defined, and it lifts via the identity map

\textstyle{{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}\underline{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(g,\mu)\mapsto(g{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}},\mu)}
\textstyle{\underline{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y_{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}}
\textstyle{Y_{K}}

to a map of local systems. By pullback, we get
r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}:H^{\ast}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\rightarrow H^{\ast}(Y_{K},{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}\underline{M})
.

By assumption
(a)
it also makes sense to
assume
that we are given an
A
-linear map

(A.3.1)

\alpha:{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}M\rightarrow N^{\prime}

that is equivariant for the action of
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}
. Therefore, we get an
A
-linear map

H^{\ast}(Y_{K},{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}\underline{M})\xrightarrow{\alpha}H^{\ast}(Y_{K},\underline{N}^{\prime})

that is
equivariant
for the Hecke action
[KhK]
with
h\in\Delta^{\prime}
.

Lemma A.3.1
.

Let
g\in\Delta
. Then, if
\psi\in H^{\ast}(Y_{K},\underline{M})
, then
\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}([KgK]\psi)=[Kg_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}K]\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}(\psi)
.

Proof.

By assumption
(b)
we have
{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}KgK{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{-1}=Kg_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}K
, if
g\in\Delta
. Therefore, it suffices to show

\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}(g\cdot\psi)=g_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}\cdot\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}(\psi)

as adelic cochains, for
g\in\Delta
, and assuming
\psi\in C^{\bullet}(K,M)
is a lift of the class
\psi
in the lemma statement. Since
\alpha
is equivariant for the action of
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}
, it suffices to show that

r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}(g\cdot\psi)=g_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}\cdot r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}(\psi).

To this end, let
\sigma\in C_{\bullet}(D_{\mathbbm{A}})
. Then,

\displaystyle r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}(g\cdot\psi)(\sigma)=(g\cdot\varphi)(\sigma{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}})

\displaystyle=g\psi(\sigma{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}g)

(within
M
)

\displaystyle=g_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}\psi(\sigma g_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}})

(within
{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}M
)

\displaystyle=g_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}(r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}(\psi))(\sigma g_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}})

\displaystyle=g_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}\cdot r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}(\psi)(\sigma).

This completes the proof.
∎

We now define
[-,-]_{M}
by

\textstyle{H^{i}_{c}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\otimes_{A}H^{2i-d}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{M}}
\textstyle{H^{i}_{c}(Y_{K},\underline{M})\otimes_{A}H^{2i-d}(Y_{K},\underline{N}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}}
\textstyle{A.}

For the next proposition, note that if
g\in\Delta
, then
g^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}
, and thus
(g^{\prime})_{{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{-1}}\in\Delta
as well.

Proposition A.3.2
.

The pairing
[-,-]_{M}
satisfies the following compatibilities.

(i)

If
g\in\Delta
then
[[KgK]\varphi,\psi]_{M}=[\varphi,[K(g^{\prime})_{{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{-1}}K]\psi]_{M}
.

(ii)

If
\varepsilon,\eta\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
and
\varepsilon\neq-\eta
, then
H^{i}_{c}(Y_{K},\underline{M})^{\varepsilon}
is orthogonal to
H^{2d-i}(Y_{K},\underline{M})^{\eta}
for
[-,-]_{M}
.

Proof.

We first prove
(i)
. If
g\in\Delta
, note that

\displaystyle[[KgK]\varphi,\psi]_{M}=[[KgK]\varphi,\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}(\psi)]_{\mathrm{can}}

\displaystyle=[\varphi,[Kg^{\prime}K]\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}(\psi)]_{\mathrm{can}}

(by Proposition
A.2.1
)

\displaystyle=[\varphi,\alpha r_{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{\ast}([K(g^{\prime})_{{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{-1}}K]\psi)]

(by Lemma
A.3.1
)

\displaystyle=[\varphi,\psi]_{M}.

We now show
(ii)
. Let
\zeta\in\{\pm 1\}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
. Recall
T_{\zeta}=r_{t_{\zeta}}^{\ast}
as in §
2.5
. Since
t_{\zeta}\in G(\mathbb{R})
, we see
\alpha r_{\tau}^{\ast}
commutes with
T_{\zeta}
. Therefore, it is enough to prove the same statement with
[-,-]_{M}
replaced with
[-,-]_{\mathrm{can}}
. So, suppose
\varphi\in H^{i}_{c}(Y_{K},\underline{M})^{\varepsilon}
and
\psi\in H^{2d-i}(Y_{K},\underline{N}^{\prime})^{\eta}
. Recall that the notation
\widehat{\varepsilon}
for a sign
\varepsilon=\pm 1
was introduced in §
2.5
. Using that notation,
T_{\zeta}\varphi=\widehat{\varepsilon}(\zeta)
, while
T_{\zeta}\psi=\widehat{\eta}(\zeta)
. On the other hand,
\widehat{(-1)}(\zeta)=\zeta
. Therefore, Proposition
A.2.1
(ii)
implies that

\widehat{\varepsilon}(\zeta)[\varphi,\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}=\widehat{(-\eta)}(\zeta)[\varphi,\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}.

Since
\zeta
is arbitrary, we conclude that either
\varepsilon=-\eta
or
[\varphi,\psi]_{\mathrm{can}}=0
.
∎

A.4.
Conclusion for main text

We encountered two similar situations in the main text. Fix
\mathfrak{n}\subseteq\mathscr{O}_{F}
an integral ideal. Let
\Delta=\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})
and
K=K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
as in §
2.6
. In the context of §
A.3
, choose

{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-\nu&0\end{pmatrix}

where
\nu\in\mathbbm{A}_{F,f}
is chosen such that
\nu\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F}=\mathfrak{n}\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{F}
. There are two contexts to which the calculations apply.

(1)

The classical context in §
4.1
: we take
N=\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
and
M=\mathscr{V}_{\lambda}
. The morphism
\alpha
is given by
\alpha(\ell)={{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}_{p}\theta_{\lambda}(\ell)
.

(2)

The overconvergent context in §
5.4
: we take
N=\mathscr{A}_{\Omega}
and
M=\mathscr{D}_{\Omega}
. The morphism
\alpha
is the one given in Proposition
5.3.1
.

Correspondingly, we have the following result.

Corollary A.4.1
.

Parts
(i)
and
(ii)
of Proposition
4.1.2
and Proposition
5.4.2
are true.

Proof.

Suppose
g\in\Delta_{0}^{+}(\mathfrak{n})
is diagonal. Then, one quickly calculates that

(A.4.1)

\left[(g^{\prime})_{{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{-1}}\right]_{v}={{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}_{v}^{-1}g_{v}^{\prime}{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}_{v}=\begin{cases}g_{v}^{\prime}&\text{if $v\nmid\mathfrak{n}$;}\\
g_{v}&\text{if $v\mid\mathfrak{n}$.}\end{cases}

Let
u\in K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
be defined by

u_{v}=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}&\text{if $v\nmid\mathfrak{n}$;}\\
1&\text{if $v\mid\mathfrak{n}$}.\end{cases}

Thus,
u^{\prime}\in K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
as well, and using (
A.4.1
) we find

u(g^{\prime})_{{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{-1}}u^{\prime}=g.

Thus
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})gK_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
and
K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})(g^{\prime})_{{{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}}^{-1}}K_{0}(\mathfrak{n})
define the same double cosets.
Therefore Proposition
4.1.2
(i)
and
5.4.2
(i)
follow from Proposition
A.3.2
(i)
. The parts
(ii)
follow directly from the corresponding part of Proposition
A.3.2
.
∎
